Four conventions differ between the sources and are fixed as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Empty exits.} 11 and 12 give a transition with no departing
coordinate, and a coordinate-free state, the weight $1$: ``an empty exit
still costs one point'' (12), ``the fallback value is one, not zero'' (11).
Part~II uses this convention throughout. 09 gives an empty retirement set
the weight $0$ ($\max\varnothing:=0$). For positive pure fibres the final
answers coincide, since the terminal weight is infinite and absorbs every
earlier finite term; intermediate dynamic-programming values differ
(Remark~\ref{nh:wpo:rem:conventions}), so certificates are not
interchangeable. The weight-$0$ convention is wrong for finite fibres, which
09 excludes; it is used only in Part~III.
\item \textbf{Maximization domain.} 07 and 11 maximize over all nonempty
chains of $\M(Q)$, 12 over maximal chains, 09 over saturated chains from
$\min Q$ to $\max Q$. These agree by
Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:saturation}.
\item \textbf{Capacity range.} 12's abstract theorem allows exponents
$\rho_q\ge0$; 11's requires $\rho_q>0$ with the fallback weight $1$. These
are equivalent: a coordinate of length $\omega^0=1$ takes only the value $0$,
so deleting it changes neither the order nor any weight.
\item \textbf{Labels.} The $N$-poset is $a<c$, $b<c$, $b<d$ everywhere here, as
in Part~I, 07, 11 and 12; 09 labels it $a<c$, $a<d$, $b<d$, and its
examples are transcribed by the isomorphism $a\leftrightarrow b$,
$c\leftrightarrow d$. The persistent example of \eqref{eq:counterheight} is
an isolated $x$ beside $y<z$, as in Part~I and 12; 07 and 11 call these
$a$ and $b<c$, 09 calls them $x$ and $a<b$. The delivered data files keep
their own labels.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Height, rank bounds and the two additions}\label{nh:sec:prelim}

All posets and ordinals are sets and we work in ZFC; no large-cardinal
hypothesis and no countability restriction is used. Rank and height are
those of Section~\ref{sec:tools}: $r_P(x)=\sup_{y<x}(r_P(y)+1)$ and
$\hgt(P)=\sup_{x\in P}(r_P(x)+1)$, with empty suprema $0$. Thus
$\hgt(\varnothing)=0$, a singleton has height $1$, an ordinal chain $\alpha$
has height $\alpha$, and $\K(\varnothing)=\{\varnothing\}$ has height $1$, not
$0$. These agree with the rank of the tree of finite strictly descending
sequences, including its empty root.

\begin{lemma}[Height tools; 07, 11, 12]\label{nh:lem:rankbound}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be well-founded posets.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If $R:X\to\eta$ is strictly increasing on strict comparisons, then
$r_X(x)\le R(x)$ for every $x$ and $\hgt(X)\le\eta$. (A poset admitting such
a map is automatically well-founded.)
\item If $f:X\to Y$ satisfies $x<x'\Rightarrow f(x)<f(x')$, then
$\hgt(X)\le\hgt(Y)$; no reflection of incomparability is required. In
particular order embeddings cannot decrease height, and a strictly
increasing copy of an ordinal $\eta$ in $X$ gives $\hgt(X)\ge\eta$.
\item For the ordinal sum of posets, $\hgt(X+Y)=\hgt(X)+\hgt(Y)$.
\item Natural sum is strictly increasing in each argument. Ordinary addition
is nondecreasing in each argument, strictly increasing and continuous in its
right argument, and need not be strictly increasing in its left argument.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A descending sequence in $X$ would give a descending sequence of ordinals.
By well-founded induction,
$r_X(x)=\sup_{y<x}(r_X(y)+1)\le\sup_{y<x}(R(y)+1)\le R(x)$, and
$R(x)<\eta$ gives $r_X(x)+1\le\eta$; this is (i). For (ii), induction in $X$
gives $r_X(x)\le r_Y(f(x))$, since each strict predecessor of $x$ maps to a
strict predecessor of $f(x)$; take suprema. Along an embedded chain the point
at position $\xi$ has rank at least $\xi$. In $X+Y$ the rank of a point of $X$
is unchanged and that of $y\in Y$ is $\hgt(X)+r_Y(y)$, by induction and
continuity of ordinary addition in its right variable; this proves (iii).
Natural addition aligns exponents and adds coefficients in Cantor normal
form, which gives its strict monotonicity; the assertions on ordinary
addition follow from its recursive definition.
\end{proof}

No assertion about linearizations is involved: height is not maximal order
type, and no maximal-linearization theorem enters Part~II. Throughout, both
an upper rank bound and an attaining chain are supplied; height is never
inferred merely by listing some chains, since it is the rank argument that
excludes larger, branching contributions (11). The two additions
play different roles and are never interchanged:
\[
 \omega+\omega^2=\omega^2,\qquad \omega^2+\omega>\omega^2,\qquad
 \omega\ns\omega^2=\omega^2+\omega.
\]
Appending a nonzero ordinal on the right deletes the lower-exponent tail of
the left operand below the leading exponent of the right operand, then
combines coefficients at that exponent if present. A \emph{pure ordinal} is
$\omega^\rho$, including $\omega^0=1$. By \eqref{eq:closure}, finite natural
sums of ordinals below a pure ordinal stay below it; moreover, if
$\kappa=\omega^\sigma<\lambda=\omega^\rho$ with $\rho>0$ and $n<\omega$, then
$\kappa\cdot n<\lambda$, and every finite $n$ is below every such $\lambda$
(11). These facts hold for uncountable exponents as well.

\begin{lemma}[Absorption with a strict margin; 12]\label{nh:lem:absorb}
For any ordinal $p$, pure ordinal $w=\omega^\rho$ and $\delta<w$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:absorb}
 p\ns\delta<p+w.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $\rho=0$, $\delta=0$ and the claim is $p<p+1$. Suppose $\rho>0$. Split $p$
into its terms of exponent at least $\rho$, written $p_{\ge\rho}$, and its
tail $p_{<\rho}<\omega^\rho$. Natural addition gives
$p\ns\delta=p_{\ge\rho}+(p_{<\rho}\ns\delta)<p_{\ge\rho}+\omega^\rho=p+\omega^\rho$,
by finite natural-sum closure below $\omega^\rho$.
\end{proof}

The strict margin controls transitions between states: coordinates that have
not yet retired stay in a natural-sum term, while a completed stage
contributes to an ordinary-sum prefix. Part~III uses a different mechanism,
deferred absorption (Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:absorb}), for the same purpose.
\section{A finite persistence theorem}\label{nh:sec:persistence}

The rank argument is isolated from the representation of finitely generated
downsets by first proving it for abstract finite state systems (12, 11).

\subsection{State systems with convex coordinate lifetimes}

\begin{definition}[Persistence system; 12, 11]\label{nh:def:system}
Let $T$ be a finite poset and $U$ a finite set of coordinate labels. For each
$t\in T$ choose an active set $A_t\subseteq U$, and require
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:convex}
 s\le t\le u\quad\Longrightarrow\quad A_s\cap A_u\subseteq A_t .
\end{equation}
Give each label $q$ a nonzero ordinal length $\lambda_q$. The
\emph{persistence order} is
\[
 Y=Y(T,A,\lambda)=\Bigl\{(t,x):t\in T,\ x\in\prod_{q\in A_t}\lambda_q\Bigr\},
\]
with the empty product a singleton, ordered by
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:stateorder}
 (t,x)\le(u,y)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t\le u\ \text{and}\ x_q\le y_q\ \text{for every }q\in A_t\cap A_u .
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Condition \eqref{nh:eq:convex}, 12's convex lifetimes and 11's
no-resurrection condition (NR), says that along a chain a label may be absent
at first, become active and disappear, but cannot disappear and then
reappear: each label's set of active states is order-convex. Empty active
sets are allowed. Coordinates shared by two states persist; a coordinate
absent at the later state imposes no comparison there.

\begin{lemma}[12, 11]\label{nh:lem:order}
Relation \eqref{nh:eq:stateorder} is a well-founded partial order.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Reflexivity is immediate, and antisymmetry forces equality of states and
then of every coordinate. For transitivity, let $(s,x)\le(t,y)\le(u,z)$; a
label shared by $s$ and $u$ is active at $t$ by \eqref{nh:eq:convex}, so
$x_q\le y_q\le z_q$. Within one state, $x\mapsto\bigns_q x_q$ strictly
increases on strict product comparisons, so each state's product is
well-founded; a strictly decreasing sequence changes its state only finitely
often, and afterwards lies in one of these products. (Alternatively, use
well-founded induction on the finite state order.)
\end{proof}

The finite-product and finite-union closure of well-quasi-orders also shows
that $Y$ is a WPO; only well-foundedness is needed for the height proof (11).

\begin{example}[Why \eqref{nh:eq:convex} is structural; 12, 11]\label{nh:ex:noreturn}
Take states $0<1<2$ with $A_0=A_2=\{q\}$ and $A_1=\varnothing$. Then
$(0,1)\le(1,\varnothing)\le(2,0)$ by \eqref{nh:eq:stateorder}, but
$(0,1)\not\le(2,0)$. Without \eqref{nh:eq:convex} the relation need not
be transitive, and taking its transitive closure would define a different
construction. The condition is used twice below: for transitivity, and to
stop a coordinate varied in an earlier block of a chain from reappearing.
\end{example}

\subsection{Retirement costs}

For the rest of this section the lengths are pure,
$\lambda_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$ with $\rho_q\ge0$. For states $s<t$ put
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:weights}
 \ew(s,t)=\max\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\lambda_q:q\in A_s\setminus A_t\}\bigr),
 \qquad
 \tw(t)=\max\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\lambda_q:q\in A_t\}\bigr).
\end{equation}
For a nonempty chain (a \emph{state path}) $C=(t_0<\cdots<t_m)$, not
necessarily saturated and not necessarily starting at a minimal state, write
$A_i=A_{t_i}$ and define the exit sets and stage costs
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:chaincost}
 \begin{gathered}
 E_i=A_i\setminus A_{i+1}\ (i<m),\qquad E_m=A_m,\\
 w_i=\max\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\lambda_q:q\in E_i\}\bigr),\qquad
 L(C)=w_0+w_1+\cdots+w_m .
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Thus $w_i=\ew(t_i,t_{i+1})$ for $i<m$ and $w_m=\tw(t_m)$. The fallback value
is one, not zero: a coordinate-free state is not absent, and a strict
transition with no departing coordinate still contributes a finite step.
Finally put $\HH(T)=\max_C L(C)$ over all nonempty chains, and
$\HH(\varnothing)=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Finite persistence theorem; 12 Thm~3.3, 11 Thm~3.3]
\label{nh:thm:persistence}
For a finite persistence system with pure lengths,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:persistence}
 \boxed{\hgt(Y)=\max_{C\text{ a maximal chain of }T}L(C)=\HH(T).}
\end{equation}
For every nonempty chain $C$ of $T$, the configurations over the states of
$C$ form an induced suborder $Y_C$ of height exactly $L(C)$ containing a chain
of that exact type. There is an explicit strictly increasing ordinal-valued
map on $Y$ with all values below $\HH(T)$.
\end{theorem}

12 proves the theorem with $\rho_q\ge0$ by a rank map over a fixed chain and a
finite cover of the descending-sequence tree. 11 proves it with $\rho_q>0$ and
the fallback weight by a leading-exponent induction; by convention~3 of
Section~\ref{nh:sec:notation} the two statements are equivalent. The
statement does not claim that either rank map equals the pointwise rank
(11); exact ranks are computed separately in
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:local}.

\subsection{First proof (12): a rank map over a fixed chain}

Fix $C=(t_0<\cdots<t_m)$, write $A_i=A_{t_i}$, and let $p_i=\sum_{k<i}w_k$ be
the completed prefix. At a configuration $x$ in stage $i$ set
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:R}
 R_C(i,x)=p_i\ns\Bigl(\bigns_{q\in A_i}x_q\Bigr),
\end{equation}
with the natural sum over the empty set equal to $0$.

\begin{lemma}[12]\label{nh:lem:fixedupper}
$R_C$ is strictly increasing on strict comparisons of $Y_C$, and
$R_C(i,x)<L(C)$ for every configuration over $C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Range: every coordinate active at stage $i$ retires later, either before a
later state or at the final stage, and by \eqref{nh:eq:convex} it cannot
return after retiring. Hence its length is at most $W_i=\max_{i\le k\le m}w_k$,
the natural sum $\delta_i$ of the values at stage $i$ is below $W_i$, and
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:absorb} gives
$R_C(i,x)=p_i\ns\delta_i<p_i+W_i\le p_i+\sum_{k=i}^mw_k=L(C)$.

Within one stage a componentwise strict increase raises at least one summand
and so raises $R_C$. Let $(i,x)<(j,y)$ with $i<j$, and split the active set
at stage $i$ into $P=A_i\cap A_j$ and $D=A_i\setminus A_j$. Every member of
$D$ retires at some transition $k\in[i,j)$, since it cannot disappear and
return. With $W=\max_{i\le k<j}w_k$, the natural sum
$\delta_D=\bigns_{q\in D}x_q$ is below $W$ (also when $D=\varnothing$), so
$p_i\ns\delta_D<p_i+W\le p_j$. Adding the persistent coordinates by natural
sum and using $x_q\le y_q$ for $q\in P$,
\[
 R_C(i,x)=(p_i\ns\delta_D)\ns\bigns_{q\in P}x_q
 <p_j\ns\bigns_{q\in P}x_q\le p_j\ns\bigns_{q\in A_j}y_q=R_C(j,y).
\]
The same ordinal function controls movement inside a stage and across
arbitrarily many stages.
\end{proof}

By Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:rankbound}, $\hgt(Y_C)\le L(C)$. The proof keeps the
shared coordinates; it never replaces the strata by totally ordered blocks.

\begin{lemma}[Block realization; 12 Lemma~3.5, 11 Lemma~4.1]
\label{nh:lem:fixedlower}
For every nonempty chain $C$ of $T$, $Y_C$ contains a chain of order type
$L(C)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $w_i>1$, choose $q_i\in E_i$ with $\lambda_{q_i}=w_i$; at stage $i$, vary its
value through all $\xi<w_i$ and keep every other coordinate $0$. This is a
chain of type $w_i$. If $w_i=1$, take the all-zero configuration at that
stage alone (at the last stage, a whole largest-length block, or one tuple
if $A_m$ is empty). For $i<j$, a coordinate varied at stage $i$ is absent at
every later state of $C$: if it reappeared, \eqref{nh:eq:convex} with
$t_{i+1}$ as the middle state would fail. So all coordinates shared by stages
$i$ and $j$ were $0$ at stage $i$, and every chosen configuration at stage $i$
is below every chosen configuration at stage $j$, strictly because the states
differ. The ordered concatenation has type $w_0+\cdots+w_m$.
\end{proof}

A long variation of a coordinate can be placed wholly before the next stage
only if that coordinate disappears there; persistent coordinates cannot supply
the same long block repeatedly. It remains to pass from one chain of states
to the whole system. Let $\Dec(P)$ be the tree of finite strictly decreasing
sequences in $P$, including the empty sequence; its rank at a nonempty
sequence is the rank of the last element, and at the root it is $\hgt(P)$.

\begin{lemma}[Finite unions of prefix-closed trees; 07 Lemma~5.1, 12 Lemma~3.6]
\label{nh:lem:treeunion}
Let $T_1,\dots,T_k$ be well-founded prefix-closed trees on a common alphabet,
each containing the empty sequence. Their union $T$ is well-founded, and at
every node $s$ of $T$
\[
 r_T(s)=\max_{i:\,s\in T_i}r_{T_i}(s);
\]
in particular the root rank of $T$ is the largest root rank of the $T_i$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An infinite branch of $T$ would have arbitrarily long prefixes in one of the
finitely many $T_i$, and prefix closure would put the whole branch into that
tree. For the rank identity induct on $s$: the rank at $s$ is the supremum,
over children $u$, of $r_T(u)+1$; substituting the induction hypothesis and
exchanging the supremum with a \emph{finite} maximum (successor commutes with
finite maxima) gives the right-hand side. Prefix closure ensures that
$u\in T_i$ implies $s\in T_i$; a tree in which $s$ has no child contributes
$0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Finite monotone-index reduction; 07 Prop.~5.2]
\label{nh:prop:chaincover}
Let $\sigma:X\to F$ be a monotone map from a well-founded poset to a finite
nonempty poset, and for a maximal chain $C$ of $F$ let $X_C=\sigma^{-1}(C)$ with
the induced order. Then
\[
 \hgt(X)=\max_{C\text{ maximal chain in }F}\hgt(X_C).
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The labels of a finite strictly descending sequence of $X$ form, after
removing repetitions, a chain of $F$, which lies in a maximal chain $C$.
Hence $\Dec(X)=\bigcup_C\Dec(X_C)$, a finite union of prefix-closed trees.
Apply Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:treeunion} at the root.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Completion of the first proof of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence}]
Apply Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:chaincover} to the state map $Y\to T$.
Lemmas~\ref{nh:lem:fixedupper} and~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower} give
$\hgt(Y_C)=L(C)$ for every maximal chain; an optimal $C$ exists since there are
finitely many, and Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower} gives the attaining chain.
A shorter chain of states extends to a maximal one, and its configurations
form an induced suborder of the latter's; so maximizing over all nonempty
chains gives the same value $\HH(T)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A distinction that prevents a false proof; 12, 07]
An arbitrary finite cover of a poset by induced subposets does not make its
height the maximum of theirs: two singletons cover a two-point chain, and a
union can create descending sequences crossing between pieces. Here the
whole descending-sequence tree is covered by prefix-closed subtrees, so every
entire descending sequence lies in one piece. Finiteness is essential for the
maximum.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Second proof (11): leading-exponent peeling}

This route supplies a recursive upper-bound certificate and the recurrence
\eqref{nh:eq:peel}. By convention~3 we may delete coordinates of length $1$
and assume every $\rho_q>0$. For a state $t$ let
$\depth(t)=\max\{k:t_0<\cdots<t_k=t\}$; thus $s<t$ implies
$\depth(s)<\depth(t)$.

Assume some active set is nonempty and let
\[
 \Lambda=\max\{\lambda_q:q\in A_t\text{ for some }t\}=\omega^{\rho},\qquad
 U^{\top}=\{q:\lambda_q=\Lambda\}.
\]
For $s<t$ let $\delta(s,t)=1$ if $(A_s\setminus A_t)\cap U^\top\ne\varnothing$
and $\delta(s,t)=0$ otherwise, and define, along a linear extension of $T$,
\begin{align}
 d_\Lambda(t)&=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup\{d_\Lambda(s)+\delta(s,t):s<t\}\bigr),
   \label{nh:eq:d}\\
 c_\Lambda&=\max_{t\in T}\bigl(d_\Lambda(t)+\mathbf 1_{A_t\cap U^\top\ne\varnothing}\bigr),
   \label{nh:eq:c}\\
 Z&=\{t\in T:d_\Lambda(t)=c_\Lambda\}.\label{nh:eq:Z}
\end{align}
So $d_\Lambda(t)$ is the largest number of $\Lambda$-departure events on a path
ending at $t$, and $c_\Lambda$ also allows a terminal $\Lambda$-block. We call
$Z$ the terminal region.

\begin{lemma}[11]\label{nh:lem:leadingdata}
$c_\Lambda\ge1$ and $d_\Lambda\le c_\Lambda$. The set $Z$ is upward closed in
$T$ and no state of $Z$ has an active label of length $\Lambda$. If $s<t$ and
$d_\Lambda(s)=d_\Lambda(t)$, then $A_s\cap U^\top\subseteq A_t\cap U^\top$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A state with an active $\Lambda$-label gives $c_\Lambda\ge1$; the other bound
follows from \eqref{nh:eq:c}. By \eqref{nh:eq:d}, $d_\Lambda$ is nondecreasing
along the order, so $s\in Z$, $s<t$ give
$c_\Lambda=d_\Lambda(s)\le d_\Lambda(t)\le c_\Lambda$. An active
$\Lambda$-label at $t\in Z$ would force $c_\Lambda\ge c_\Lambda+1$. Finally,
deleting a $\Lambda$-label along $s<t$ forces $d_\Lambda(t)\ge d_\Lambda(s)+1$.
\end{proof}

The restriction of the system to $Z$ is again a persistence system, with fewer
distinct lengths; write $Y_Z$ for its order. This is the induction parameter.
The exponents themselves need not be finite or countable.

\begin{lemma}[Upper-bound step; 11]\label{nh:lem:upperstep}
If a strictly increasing map $R_Z:Y_Z\to\HH(Z)$ is available (the empty map
when $Z=\varnothing$), then there is a strictly increasing map
$R:Y\to\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\kappa$ be the largest length below $\Lambda$ used anywhere, and $\kappa=1$
if there is none. For $(t,x)\in Y$ put
\[
 u(t,x)=\kappa\cdot\depth(t)+\bigns_{\substack{q\in A_t\\ \lambda_q<\Lambda}}x_q,
 \qquad
 v(t,x)=\bigns_{\substack{q\in A_t\\ \lambda_q=\Lambda}}x_q,
 \qquad
 \vartheta(t,x)=v(t,x)\ns u(t,x).
\]
The lower-coordinate natural sum is below $\kappa$ (or absent when
$\kappa=1$), so $u(t,x)<\kappa\cdot(\depth(t)+1)<\Lambda$, using finiteness of
the depth; also $v(t,x)<\Lambda$ and $\vartheta(t,x)<\Lambda$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:peelR}
 R(t,x)=\begin{cases}
 \Lambda\cdot d_\Lambda(t)+\vartheta(t,x),&t\notin Z,\\
 \Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+R_Z(t,x),&t\in Z.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The first line is below $\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda$ because $d_\Lambda<c_\Lambda$
off $Z$; the second is below $\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z)$.

Strictness. Within one state off $Z$, a strict coordinate increase strictly
raises $u$ or $v$ and lowers neither, so $\vartheta$ and $R$ rise. Let $s<t$ both
lie off $Z$. If $d_\Lambda(s)<d_\Lambda(t)$, then
$R(s,x)<\Lambda(d_\Lambda(s)+1)\le\Lambda d_\Lambda(t)\le R(t,y)$. If
$d_\Lambda(s)=d_\Lambda(t)$, no $\Lambda$-coordinate is lost
(Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:leadingdata}); shared coordinates do not decrease and new
ones are nonnegative, so $v(s,x)\le v(t,y)$, while
$u(s,x)<\kappa(\depth(s)+1)\le\kappa\depth(t)\le u(t,y)$. Thus $\vartheta$ rises
strictly even if lower-length coordinates disappear. Inside $Z$ use the
strictness of $R_Z$ and strict monotonicity of ordinary addition in its right
argument. A comparison from outside $Z$ into $Z$ crosses the bound
$\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda$, and the reverse direction is impossible because $Z$
is upward closed.
\end{proof}

The finite depth term pays for state changes that erase smaller coordinates
while leaving every largest coordinate alive. It is harmless at scale $\Lambda$,
yet without it the natural sum of surviving coordinates alone need not be
monotone.

\begin{lemma}[Attaining the step; 11]\label{nh:lem:lowerstep}
If $\HH(Z)$ is attained by a state path whenever $Z\ne\varnothing$, then some
state path of $T$ has cost $\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $Z\ne\varnothing$ and choose an attaining path in $Z$ with first state
$t$. As $d_\Lambda(t)=c_\Lambda$, some path ending at $t$ realizes $c_\Lambda$
departure events of $\Lambda$-labels. Keep the predecessor state of each such
event, discard the other states except $t$, and concatenate with the path in
$Z$. Each newly consecutive pair in the kept prefix has exit weight $\Lambda$:
a $\Lambda$-label left right after the earlier state, and
\eqref{nh:eq:convex} prevents its presence at any later kept state. The cost
is $\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z)$. If $Z=\varnothing$, a state attaining
\eqref{nh:eq:c} has an active $\Lambda$-label and $d_\Lambda=c_\Lambda-1$;
keep the predecessor states of $c_\Lambda-1$ departures on a path to it, and
let that state contribute the last $\Lambda$-block. The cost is
$\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence}]
Induct on the number of distinct lengths active somewhere. If $T$ is empty
everything holds with value $0$. If no label is active, $Y\cong T$, every
weight is $1$, $\HH(T)$ is the largest chain cardinality, the depth map is a
strict map into it, and a longest chain attains it. Otherwise form
$\Lambda,c_\Lambda,Z$; no $\Lambda$-label is active on $Z$, so the induction
hypothesis applies to $Y_Z$. Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:upperstep} and
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:rankbound} give $\hgt(Y)\le\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z)$;
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:lowerstep} supplies a path of that cost, which
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower} realizes by a chain; and every path cost is at
most $\hgt(Y)$ by the same lemma. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:peel}
 \boxed{\HH(T)=\hgt(Y)=\Lambda\cdot c_\Lambda+\HH(Z),}
\end{equation}
and the induction supplies the strict map and the attaining chain.
\end{proof}

The recurrence \eqref{nh:eq:peel} and the path formula come out together;
neither is justified by extrapolation from finite approximations.

\section{Algorithms and certificates for persistence systems}
\label{nh:sec:systemalgorithms}

\subsection{An ordinal longest-path computation (11)}

For the weights \eqref{nh:eq:weights} define, along a linear extension,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:pDP}
 \mathsf p(t)=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup\{\mathsf p(s)+\ew(s,t):s<t\}\bigr),
 \qquad
 \HH(T)=\max_{t\in T}\bigl(\mathsf p(t)+\tw(t)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Here $\mathsf p(t)$ is the transition part of a path ending at $t$, before its
final state is charged; it starts at $0$ so that a path may begin at $t$.

\begin{proposition}[11]\label{nh:prop:pDP}
The recurrence \eqref{nh:eq:pDP} computes $\HH(T)$, and storing a maximizing
predecessor yields an attaining state path.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A path ending at $t$ is $t$ alone or has a penultimate state $s<t$; its
transition sum is that of the path to $s$ followed by $\ew(s,t)$. Ordinary
addition is nondecreasing in its left argument, so the largest prefix value
at $s$ can be kept without losing a better continuation, and finite maxima
are attained. Append the terminal weight and maximize over endpoints.
\end{proof}

All strict comparable pairs are used; no restriction to Hasse edges is
claimed, and the code does not assume that an optimal path is saturated. With
$m=|T|$ and $\ell=|U|$, all edge weights cost $O(m^2\ell)$ membership and
ordinal-comparison operations and the program $O(m^2)$ ordinal additions and
comparisons. These are operation counts, not bit-complexity claims for
arbitrary notations; building $T$ from a skeleton may itself be exponential,
and checking \eqref{nh:eq:convex} on all triples costs $O(m^3\ell)$ (in the
applications it is proved structurally).

\subsection{Two complementary certificates (11)}

A maximizing path is a \emph{lower} certificate: one checks its state
comparisons and the finite list of weights, and
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower} turns it into an ordinal-indexed chain. The
recursive records $(\Lambda,d_\Lambda,c_\Lambda,Z)$ are an \emph{upper}
certificate: one checks \eqref{nh:eq:d}, \eqref{nh:eq:c} and \eqref{nh:eq:Z}
at every stage, and the recipe \eqref{nh:eq:peelR} interprets them as a
strict rank map. It explains why every point, not only the points of a
maximizing path, receives an ordinal below the proposed height. 11's
reference implementation computes both answers, and its peeling routine never
calls the weighted-path routine; this separates implementation failure modes
but does not replace the proof.

\begin{corollary}[Finite CNF support; 11 Cor.~5.2]\label{nh:cor:support}
The positive exponents in $\HH(T)$ are among the $\rho_q$ of used labels; its
finite term, if any, is a natural number at most $|T|$; and the sum of the
coefficients of its positive-exponent terms is at most the number of used
labels.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each weight of an attaining path is $1$ or a pure power $\omega^{\rho_q}$, and
ordinary addition of such a list can delete terms or merge equal exponents
but creates no new exponent. For every infinite nonterminal weight choose a
witnessing label as in Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower}; it disappears before the
next state and never returns, so it witnesses no later weight, the terminal
one included. A path has at most $|T|$ weights, so at most that many finite
ones survive.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Order-pattern invariance with certificates; 11 Cor.~5.3]
\label{nh:cor:parametric}
Keep the finite system fixed and replace its positive exponents by other
positive ordinals with the same equalities and strict order relations. The
Cantor-normal-form coefficient pattern of $\HH(T)$ is unchanged, only the
exponents being relabelled, and the same peeling certificate remains valid.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At each stage the set of labels of largest remaining length is unchanged,
hence so are $\delta$, $d_\Lambda$, $c_\Lambda$ and $Z$. Induct on the
finitely many exponent levels using \eqref{nh:eq:peel}; the final
coordinate-free height is unchanged.
\end{proof}

No regularity, cofinality or countability assumption on an exponent is hidden
in the theorem, and a symbolic implementation may compute with a finite
ordered alphabet of exponent levels and substitute actual ordinals at the end.
The lexicographic-sum forms of this invariance are
Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:scale} and, for Part~III, Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:chambers}.

\section{Arbitrary capacities and exact ranks in persistent systems}
\label{nh:sec:local}

\subsection{Phase refinement}

Keep \eqref{nh:eq:convex} but let every length $\gamma_q$ be an arbitrary
\emph{nonzero} ordinal. Split its Cantor normal form into a finite ordinal sum
of pure blocks $\gamma_q=\sum_{b<B_q}\omega^{\tau_{q,b}}$, equal exponents
repeated according to their coefficients, so that each value of the coordinate
has a unique block index $b$ and a unique offset below $\omega^{\tau_{q,b}}$.
Refine a state $t$ into the states $(t,\sigma)$ with
$\sigma\in\prod_{q\in A_t}B_q$, ordered by
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:phaseorder}
 (s,\sigma)\le(t,\varsigma)\iff s\le t\ \text{and}\ \sigma_q\le\varsigma_q
 \ \text{for every }q\in A_s\cap A_t .
\end{equation}
The active labels of $(t,\sigma)$ are the pairs $(q,\sigma_q)$ with
$q\in A_t$ and $\tau_{q,\sigma_q}>0$, of length $\omega^{\tau_{q,\sigma_q}}$; a
singleton phase carries no coordinate.

\begin{theorem}[Phase refinement; 11 Thm~8.1, 12 Cor.~6.4]\label{nh:thm:phase}
The refined system is a finite persistence system with pure lengths
satisfying \eqref{nh:eq:convex}, and its persistence order is isomorphic to
the original one. Hence the height of every finite persistence system with
nonzero ordinal lengths, and a chain attaining it, are computed by refinement
followed by Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Relation \eqref{nh:eq:phaseorder} is a finite partial order by the
transitivity argument of Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:order}, now with block indices.
If a refined label $(q,b)$ is active at both ends of a chain of three refined
states, the original label is active at the middle state by
\eqref{nh:eq:convex}, and the block index there is squeezed between two equal
indices; so refined lifetimes are convex. Decompose each coordinate value into
block and offset. This is bijective because the block intervals partition
$\gamma_q$. Values in different blocks compare by block index, regardless of
offsets; within an infinite block they compare by offset; a singleton block
offers no choice. So the original comparison is exactly the refined-state
comparison together with inequalities on the common refined active labels.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why impure lengths cannot simply be substituted; 12, 11]
\label{nh:rem:impure}
Two comparable states carrying the same coordinate of length $\omega+1$ give
the order $2\times(\omega+1)$ of height $\omega+2$ (its greatest tuple has
natural-sum rank $1\ns\omega=\omega+1$), while the unrefined cost rule gives
$1+(\omega+1)=\omega+1$ (12). One state with two coordinates of length
$\omega+1$ gives $(\omega+1)\times(\omega+1)$, of height $\omega\cdot2+1$
(Section~\ref{nh:sec:examples}), while the unrefined terminal maximum gives
$\omega+1$ (11). Phase refinement is a substantive correction, not
bookkeeping.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Exact point ranks in any finite persistent system (11)}

Let $z=(t,x)$ be a point of a persistence order with nonzero ordinal lengths
$\gamma_q$. Let $J=\{s:s\le t\}$, keep the active sets on $J$, and cap
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:capped}
 \gamma'_q=\begin{cases}x_q+1,&q\in A_t,\\ \gamma_q,&q\notin A_t.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Every $\gamma'_q$ is nonzero.

\begin{lemma}[Principal-ideal representation; 11]\label{nh:lem:ideal}
The inclusive principal ideal $Y_{\le z}$ is isomorphic to $Y(J,A,\gamma')$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A point $(s,y)$ is below $z$ exactly when $s\le t$ and $y_q\le x_q$, that is
$y_q<x_q+1$, for every $q\in A_s\cap A_t$; all other coordinates keep their
ranges, and the order on the restricted tuples is unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact point ranks in persistent systems; 11 Thm~8.4]
\label{nh:thm:local}
Form the capped system of Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:ideal}, refine it by
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:phase} and compute its height $H_z$. Then $H_z$ is a
successor and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:local}
 \boxed{r_Y(z)=\pred(H_z),}
\end{equation}
where $\pred(\eta+1)=\eta$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The principal ideal has greatest element $z$, and ranks of its points agree
with their ranks in $Y$ because all their predecessors lie in the ideal. So
$\hgt(Y_{\le z})=r_Y(z)+1$, and the two preceding results compute this height.
\end{proof}

In executable Cantor normal form the predecessor decrements the positive
finite last coefficient (deleting the final term when it reaches zero); a
returned limit would signal an implementation error. The algorithm does not
claim that the rank maps of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence} are exact: it
solves a different finite instance for each point, whose state space may be
larger.
\section{Support antichains and an exact finite dynamic program}
\label{nh:sec:dag}

\subsection{The support model, including the empty downset}

For a finite poset $Q$ let $\Ant(Q)$ be the set of all antichains, including
$\varnothing$, under the Hoare order $A\le_HB$ iff every $a\in A$ lies below
some $b\in B$. The map $A\mapsto{\downarrow}A$ is an isomorphism onto the
lattice $\J(Q)$ of downsets of $Q$, with inverse $I\mapsto\max I$. For an
antichain $A$ and $x\in\prod_{q\in A}\alpha_q$ write
$\langle A;x\rangle={\downarrow}\{(q,x_q):q\in A\}$, with
$\langle\varnothing;\varnothing\rangle=\varnothing$.

\begin{lemma}[Support coordinates; 12 Lemma~4.1, 07 Lemma~2.2]
\label{nh:lem:support}
Let $Q$ be finite and every $\alpha_q>0$. The map $(A,x)\mapsto\langle A;x\rangle$
is an order isomorphism from
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:supportmodel}
 \Bigl\{(A,x):A\in\Ant(Q),\ x\in\prod_{q\in A}\alpha_q\Bigr\},
 \qquad (A,x)\le(B,y)\iff A\le_HB\ \text{and}\ x_q\le y_q\ (q\in A\cap B),
\end{equation}
onto $\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$. Moreover, for antichains of any finite
poset,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:noreturn}
 A\le_HB\le_HC\quad\Longrightarrow\quad A\cap C\subseteq B .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A finite generating set can be reduced to its maximal elements. They form an
antichain of points, no two in one chain fibre, and they are exactly the
maximal points of the generated downset; this gives existence and uniqueness,
the empty set included. A generator $(a,x_a)$ lies below a generator
$(b,y_b)$ exactly when $a<b$, or $a=b$ and $x_a\le y_b$. If $a\in A\cap B$, no
other member of the antichain $B$ lies above $a$, so the comparison at $a$ must
be $x_a\le y_a$; this proves both directions of the order criterion. For
\eqref{nh:eq:noreturn} take $a\in A\cap C$ and $b\in B$, $c\in C$ with
$a\le b\le c$; since $a,c\in C$, antichainness forces $c=a$ and then $b=a$.
\end{proof}

Inequality \eqref{nh:eq:noreturn} is the no-return lemma of 07
(Lemma~7.1) and 11 (Lemma~6.2); maximality of the antichains is not used. When
all $\alpha_q$ are pure, the support model is exactly the persistence system of
Definition~\ref{nh:def:system} with state poset $\Ant(Q)$ and active sets $A$.
No assumption excluding finite cofinal subsets is needed, and singleton
fibres ($\alpha_q=1=\omega^0$) are legitimate (12). Since there are finitely
many supports and each carries a finite product of ordinal chains,
$\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ is a WPO; well-foundedness alone also follows
because a descending sequence changes support only finitely often (07).

\subsection{The ideal-state recurrence (12)}

Let $Q$ be a finite poset with pure nonzero weights $\lambda_v$ (weight $1$
allowed). For $I\in\J(Q)$ and $v$ minimal in $Q\setminus I$, $I\cup\{v\}$ is a
downset and the active set changes from $\max I$ to $\max(I\cup\{v\})$; the
retiring set is
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:retiredideal}
 E(I,v)=\{a\in\max I:a<v\}.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{align}
 \mathsf c(I,v)&=\max\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\lambda_a:a\in E(I,v)\}\bigr),
   \label{nh:eq:edgecost}\\
 \tau_Q&=\max\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\lambda_a:a\in\max Q\}\bigr),
   \label{nh:eq:terminal}
\end{align}
put $\Fdp(\varnothing)=0$ and, for every nonempty ideal $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:dp}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Fdp(J)=\max_{v\in\max J}
 \Bigl(\Fdp(J\setminus\{v\})+\mathsf c(J\setminus\{v\},v)\Bigr).}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite algorithm; 12 Thm~4.2]\label{nh:thm:dag}
For a finite poset $Q$ and pure nonzero ordinal weights,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:dpheight}
 \boxed{\hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\lambda}(Q))\bigr)=\Fdp(Q)+\tau_Q.}
\end{equation}
This includes $Q=\varnothing$, where the answer is $1$. An optimizing path in
the downset lattice supplies an explicit attaining chain of finitely generated
downsets.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A maximal chain of the finite lattice $\J(Q)$ starts at $\varnothing$, ends at
$Q$ and adds one vertex at each step (otherwise an intermediate ideal could be
inserted); such chains are the linear extensions of $Q$. Under the support
model the exit weight of the state $\max I$ before adjoining $v$ is
\eqref{nh:eq:edgecost}, and the terminal weight is \eqref{nh:eq:terminal}.
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence} reduces the height to the greatest path cost
plus the terminal cost, and \eqref{nh:eq:dp} computes that cost: the last
vertex added is some $v\in\max J$, ordinary addition is monotone in its left
argument, and finite maxima are attained, so keeping only the maximum at each
state is valid. The chain is transported through Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:support}.
\end{proof}

The addition in \eqref{nh:eq:dp} must not be replaced by natural addition: the
latter proves rank bounds, it does not concatenate stages
($\omega+\omega^2=\omega^2$, whereas $\omega\ns\omega^2=\omega^2+\omega$).
The recurrence has the shape of Part~I's block recurrence \eqref{eq:DP} but
computes a different invariant with different weights.

\subsection{Certificates and complexity (12)}\label{nh:sec:dagcert}

A certificate consists of the expanded finite poset, its pure weights, a value
$\Fdp(I)$ for every ideal and one optimizing path. A verifier checks that all
ideals are present, checks every equality \eqref{nh:eq:dp}, and independently
sums the path costs and the terminal cost. The table is the upper certificate;
the path is the lower certificate, whose chain is described in
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedlower}. With $N=|Q|$ and $J=|\J(Q)|$ there are at most
$NJ$ edges and at most $NJ$ ordinal additions and comparisons; finite set
operations add their usual costs, and the stored state space is $O(J)$ ordinal
entries apart from the certificate. Since $J$ can equal $2^N$, this is an
exact finite algorithm, not a polynomial-time claim. Enumerating all linear
extensions is only an independent small-case test.

\begin{corollary}[A supplied chain partition; 12 Cor.~4.3]\label{nh:cor:chaincover}
If the expanded skeleton $Q$ is partitioned into $w$ chains of lengths
$n_1,\dots,n_w$, then $|\J(Q)|\le\prod_{i=1}^w(n_i+1)\le(N+1)^w$, and the
ideal-state method uses $O(N(N+1)^w)$ ordinal operations.
Cantor-normal-form refinement (Section~\ref{nh:sec:arbitrary}) does not
increase the minimum number of chains in a partition of the original skeleton.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
An ideal meets each chain in a prefix, so the tuple of prefix lengths
determines it; cross-chain order constraints only reduce the count. Replacing
each vertex of a chain of the original partition by its consecutive pure blocks
gives a chain partition of the refinement with no more chains.
\end{proof}

A fixed supplied chain-partition size is therefore already a tractable case in
the expanded input. This is not a bit-complexity result for succinctly encoded
large Cantor coefficients.

\begin{corollary}[Threshold certificates; 12 Cor.~4.4]\label{nh:cor:NP}
For a pure-block input, encode the finitely many exponent scales by their
ordered integer labels. Given a threshold $\gamma$ in Cantor normal form over
an explicitly ordered finite list of scales, the problem
$\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\lambda}(Q)))\ge\gamma$ belongs to $\mathrm{NP}$ with
respect to this finite encoding.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If the inequality holds, a winning linear extension of $Q$ is a witness of
length $N$: check that it is a linear extension, compute its retiring sets and
stage costs, and compare their ordinary sum with $\gamma$. A path has $N+1$
pure costs, so its normal form has coefficient sum at most $N+1$ and exponent
labels from the input together with $0$; all this is polynomial in the finite
encoding. Conversely an accepted path gives a chain of its computed type by
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence}.
\end{proof}

This is an upper bound, not an NP-completeness claim, and it refers to the
pure-block encoding: unary expansion of a succinct coefficient is not silently
counted as polynomial preprocessing.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch 42: the problem is NP-complete; hardness holds
already for height-two skeletons with every fibre $\omega$
(Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np} and Remark~\ref{nh:ur:rem:partII}).]

\section{The compressed formula for positive pure fibres}\label{nh:sec:frontier}

Throughout this section $Q$ is finite and nonempty and
$\alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$ with $\rho_q>0$. Such a fibre is a nonzero limit
with no greatest element and no finite cofinal subset, so every
$K\in\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ has the canonical frontier of
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:canonicalcompare}(ii) below (12's reproof of Part~I's
Lemma~\ref{lem:canonical}). The support model of
Section~\ref{nh:sec:dag} is exact and handles finite endpoints; here a smaller
state system, the frontier poset $\M(Q)$ of Part~I, gives \eqref{nh:eq:answer}.

\subsection{Weighted frontiers}

Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:canonicalcompare}(i) below (12 Lemma~5.1, reproving
Part~I's Lemma~\ref{lem:frontiers}) gives $A=\min(Q\setminus D(A))$ and
$A\preceq B\iff D(A)\subseteq D(B)$. Two more facts are needed (07, 11, 12):
if $A\prec B$ in $\M(Q)$ then $A\setminus B\ne\varnothing$, because one
inclusion-maximal antichain cannot properly contain another; and the active-set
assignment $A\mapsto A$ on $\M(Q)$ satisfies \eqref{nh:eq:convex}, by
\eqref{nh:eq:noreturn}. For $A\prec B$ put
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:frontierweights}
 w(A,B)=\max_{q\in A\setminus B}\alpha_q,\qquad t(A)=\max_{q\in A}\alpha_q,
\end{equation}
and for a nonempty chain $C=(A_0\prec\cdots\prec A_m)$ let
$W(C)=w(A_0,A_1)+\cdots+w(A_{m-1},A_m)+t(A_m)$ as in \eqref{nh:eq:cost}; for
$m=0$ this is $t(A_0)$.

\begin{theorem}[Mixed pure frontier formula; 07 Thm~3.1, 11 Thm~1.1, 12 Thm~5.4]
\label{nh:thm:frontier}
For finite nonempty $Q$ and $\alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$, $\rho_q>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:frontierheight}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\bigr)
 =\max_{A_0\prec\cdots\prec A_m}
 \Bigl(\sum_{i<m}\max_{q\in A_i\setminus A_{i+1}}\alpha_q
       +\max_{q\in A_m}\alpha_q\Bigr).}
\end{equation}
The maximum is over all nonempty strict chains of $\M(Q)$, one-element chains
included; it may be restricted to maximal chains, or to saturated chains from
$\min Q$ to $\max Q$ (Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:saturation}). It is attained by
a chain in $\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ of exactly that order type.
\end{theorem}

Part~III proves the same formula for well-partial-order fibres whose
finitary powersets have pure height (09 Thm~7.2 with Cor.~3.2;
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:compressed}). Two proofs follow; a third is the
special case of Part~III, whose upper bound goes through Wolk's theorem.

\subsection{First proof (12, 11): two embeddings}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical frontier and comparison across strata; 12 Lemmas~5.1--5.2, 11 Lemma~6.1]\label{nh:lem:canonicalcompare}
Write $P_q$ for the chain fibre $\alpha_q$ over $q$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \textup{(12 Lemma~5.1.)} For $A,B\in\M(Q)$, $A=\min(Q\setminus D(A))$
and $A\preceq B\iff D(A)\subseteq D(B)$.
\item \textup{(12 Lemma~5.2.)} For $K\in\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ let
$D_K=\{q\in Q:P_q\subseteq K\}$ be its set of completed fibres. There is a
unique $A\in\M(Q)$ with $D_K=D(A)$, the \emph{canonical frontier} of $K$. The
downset $K$ contains all fibres in $D(A)$, contains a possibly empty principal
downset in every fibre of $A$, and is empty in all remaining fibres.
\item \textup{(11 Lemma~6.1.)} Let $K,L\in\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ have
canonical frontiers $A,B$ and active sections $K_a=K\cap P_a$,
$L_b=L\cap P_b$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:canonicalcompare}
 K\subseteq L\iff A\preceq B\ \text{and}\ K_a\subseteq L_a
 \ \text{for every }a\in A\cap B .
\end{equation}
\item Every tuple of nonempty principal sections at $A$ occurs in the stratum
of $A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) (12.) Every predecessor of an element of $A$ is in $D(A)$, so $A$ consists
of minimal elements outside $D(A)$. Conversely, a minimal element outside
$D(A)$ must be comparable with some element of the maximal antichain $A$; it
can lie neither below nor strictly above it, so it equals it. Domination
implies inclusion of strict-predecessor sets. Conversely, suppose
$D(A)\subseteq D(B)$. An $a\in A$ either belongs to $D(B)$, in which case it
lies below a member of $B$, or is minimal outside $D(B)$ (its strict
predecessors lie in $D(A)\subseteq D(B)$) and therefore belongs to $B$. This
proves domination.

(ii) (12.) Let $A_0$ be the support of the maximal generators of $K$
(Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:support}). A fibre is entirely present exactly when some
generator has strictly greater index: internal finite generators cannot be
cofinal in a limit chain. Thus $D_K=D(A_0)$, where the same strict-predecessor
notation is used for a possibly nonmaximal antichain, including
$A_0=\varnothing$. Set $D=D_K$ and $A=\min(Q\setminus D)$. Every member of
$A_0$ is in $A$, while all predecessors of members of $A$ belong to $D$. Hence
$D(A)=D(A_0)=D$. Any element outside $D$ lies above some member of $A$; any
element of $D$ lies below a member of $A_0\subseteq A$. Consequently $A$ is
inclusion-maximal. A nonempty nonfull section must lie at the index of a
maximal generator, so its index is in $A$. Uniqueness follows from~(i).

(iii) (11.) If $K\subseteq L$, completed fibres stay completed, so
$D(A)\subseteq D(B)$ and $A\preceq B$ by~(i), and shared sections are
included. Conversely, if
$A\preceq B$, every $a\in A\setminus B$ lies below some $b\in B$, strictly
since $a\notin B$, so $a\in D(B)=D_L$ and the fibre of $a$ is completed in
$L$; completed fibres of $K$ remain completed in $L$ because
$D(A)\subseteq D(B)$; and the shared active sections are
included by hypothesis.

(iv) (12.) The downset generated by one point $(a,x_a)$ for each $a\in A$ has
support $A$, so by the proof of~(ii) its completed-fibre set is $D(A)$, and its
section at $a$ is ${\downarrow}x_a$.
\end{proof}

Parts (i) and~(ii) are Part~I's Lemmas~\ref{lem:frontiers}
and~\ref{lem:canonical} for chain fibres, and (iii) and~(iv) are Part~I's
comparison rule and the sufficiency half of \eqref{eq:tuplecondition}. The
proofs printed here are 12's and 11's reproofs, so the new height theorem does
not rest on Part~I's arguments. Not every tuple of empty or nonempty
sections belongs to the indicated stratum, but every tuple with all active
sections nonempty does, and this suffices for matching bounds (12).

Let $Y_{\M}$ be the persistence order with states $\M(Q)$, active sets $A$ and
lengths $\alpha_q$.

\begin{lemma}[Two embeddings; 12 Lemma~5.3, 11 \S6.2]\label{nh:lem:twoembeddings}
There are order embeddings
$Y_{\M}\hookrightarrow\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\hookrightarrow Y_{\M}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
First map: send $(A,x)$ to the downset generated by the points $(a,x_a)$,
$a\in A$. It contains every fibre over $D(A)$ and the nonempty principal
section ${\downarrow}x_a$ at each $a\in A$; by
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:canonicalcompare} comparison of two images is frontier
domination plus comparison on shared coordinates, which is the order of
$Y_{\M}$. Second map: use the order isomorphisms
$\theta_q:\K(\alpha_q)\to\alpha_q$, $\theta_q(\varnothing)=0$,
$\theta_q({\downarrow}\xi)=1+\xi$, which exist because
$\K(\alpha_q)\cong1+\alpha_q=\alpha_q$ for infinite $\alpha_q$. Send $K$ with
canonical frontier $A$ to $(A,(\theta_a(K_a))_{a\in A})$. By
\eqref{nh:eq:canonicalcompare} this preserves and reflects order. It need not
be surjective: empty coordinates are subject to the support condition of
Part~I's \eqref{eq:tuplecondition}, which the proof does not use.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[First proof of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:frontier}]
By Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:rankbound}(ii) the two embeddings give
$\hgt(Y_{\M})=\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q)))$, and
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence} computes the first height. Every exit along a
strict chain of $\M(Q)$ is nonempty and every state is nonempty, with pure
weights greater than $1$, so the fallback $1$ disappears and the weights are
exactly \eqref{nh:eq:frontierweights}. The first embedding transports the
attaining chain.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Height equivalence is not a rank identification; 11]
\label{nh:rem:notlocal}
The two embeddings prove equality of heights, not preservation of pointwise
rank: the zero tuple of $Y_{\M}$ need not correspond to a rank-zero downset. For
exact ranks use the support model (Theorems~\ref{nh:thm:local}
and~\ref{nh:thm:localrank}), never this compression.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Second proof (07): normalization and retirement boxes}

This route avoids abstract state systems. It normalizes onto maximal supports,
covers descending sequences by frontier chains, and computes the height over one
chain through a standalone box theorem.

\begin{definition}[Minimal completion; 07]
For an antichain $A$ of $Q$ let $\Inc(A)$ be the set of vertices incomparable
with every member of $A$, and $\mcl(A)=A\cup\min\Inc(A)$; thus
$\mcl(\varnothing)=\min Q$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Least maximal completion; 07 Lemma~4.2]\label{nh:lem:completion}
$\mcl(A)$ is a maximal antichain, and it is the least member of $\M(Q)$ lying
Hoare-above $A$. Consequently $A\le_HB$ implies $\mcl(A)\preceq\mcl(B)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:retirestrict}
 A\le_HB\quad\Longrightarrow\quad A\setminus B\subseteq\mcl(A)\setminus\mcl(B).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The members of $\min\Inc(A)$ are pairwise incomparable and incomparable with
$A$; every vertex of $\Inc(A)$ lies above one of them, and every other vertex is
comparable with a member of $A$; so $\mcl(A)$ is maximal. Let $B\in\M(Q)$ with
$A\le_HB$ and $u\in\min\Inc(A)$, and suppose no member of $B$ lies above $u$.
Maximality of $B$ gives $b\in B$ with $b<u$. Then $b\in\Inc(A)$: $a\le b$ would
give $a<u$, and $b\le a$ with $a\le b'\in B$ would force $b=a=b'$, both contrary
to $u\in\Inc(A)$. This contradicts the minimality of $u$. So $\mcl(A)\le_HB$;
apply this to $\mcl(B)$ for monotonicity. Finally, $a\in A\setminus B$ lies
strictly below some $b\in B\subseteq\mcl(B)$, so $a\notin\mcl(B)$, while
$a\in\mcl(A)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Editorial: three names for one antichain]\label{nh:rem:threenames}
For every antichain $A$, $\mcl(A)=\min(Q\setminus D(A))$. Both sets contain
$A$. A member $u\notin A$ of $\min(Q\setminus D(A))$ is not below a member of
$A$ (it would lie in $D(A)$) and not above one, say $a<u$ (then $a\notin D(A)$
would contradict minimality); so $u\in\Inc(A)$, and it is minimal there because
$\Inc(A)$ is disjoint from $D(A)$. Conversely let $u\in\min\Inc(A)$; then
$u\notin D(A)$, and a strict predecessor $v\notin D(A)$ of $u$ would lie in
$\Inc(A)$, against minimality, or satisfy $a\le v<u$ for some $a\in A$, against
$u\in\Inc(A)$. Hence 07's completion, the canonical frontier of Lemma~\ref{lem:canonical}
(for $A$ the support of $K$) and 09's canonical antichain
$A(I)=\min(Q\setminus C(I))$ of Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:canonical} (for $A=\max I$)
coincide. This identification is an editorial observation of the merge.
\end{remark}

Let $\Kmax(S)$ be the induced subposet of $\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$ on the
downsets $\langle A;x\rangle$ with $A\in\M(Q)$ (07's $Y$); its support map to
$\M(Q)$ is monotone. It omits, for example, the empty downset.

\begin{proposition}[Height-preserving normalization; 07 Prop.~4.3]
\label{nh:prop:normalization}
There is a strictly order-preserving map
$\nu:\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\to\Kmax(S)$, and hence
$\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q)))=\hgt(\Kmax(S))$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $\nu(\langle A;x\rangle)=\langle\mcl(A);z\rangle$ with $z_q=1+x_q$ for $q\in A$
and $z_q=0$ for $q\in\mcl(A)\setminus A$. Left addition $1+x_q$ is strictly
increasing in $x_q$ and stays below $\alpha_q$ because $1+\alpha_q=\alpha_q$, so
$\nu$ is defined even on the empty profile. Let
$\langle A;x\rangle\subseteq\langle B;y\rangle$. The completions are Hoare
ordered (Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:completion}). If $q\in A\cap\mcl(B)$, choose $b\in B$
with $q\le b$; both lie in the antichain $\mcl(B)$, so $q=b\in B$. Hence a
coordinate common to the two completions that is originally active at the
source is originally active at the target, and its shifted value does not
decrease by Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:support}; a filler coordinate has value $0$. So
$\nu$ is monotone. If the inclusion is strict and the completions differ, the
images differ; if they agree, \eqref{nh:eq:retirestrict} gives $A\subseteq B$,
and either $A\ne B$, when some filler $0$ becomes a positive $1+y_q$, or $A=B$
and some original coordinate increases. So $\nu$ is strict, and
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:rankbound} and inclusion give equal heights.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[07]
The normalization is a height comparison, not an order isomorphism, and not an
argument that cofinal subposets preserve rank (they need not). Its treatment of
the empty profile is essential: for a finite singleton fibre, $\K(P)$ has
height $2$ while its nonempty maximal-support part has height $1$. The
hypotheses of this section avoid that defect.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Retirement box; 07]
Let $G_0,\dots,G_m$ be pairwise disjoint nonempty finite groups of coordinates,
coordinate $q$ having capacity $\alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$, $\rho_q>0$, and let
$\lambda_i=\max_{q\in G_i}\alpha_q$. The retirement box
$\mathsf B=\mathsf B(G_0,\dots,G_m)$ is the union of the stages
\[
 \mathsf B_i=\bigl\{z:\ z_q=\alpha_q\ \text{for }q\in G_j,\ j<i;\ \
 z_q<\alpha_q\ \text{for }q\in G_j,\ j\ge i\bigr\},
\]
ordered coordinatewise. The value $\alpha_q$ is a sentinel for a retired
coordinate. The last group never retires: there is no stage $m+1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Retirement-box height; 07 Thm~6.2]\label{nh:thm:boxes}
$\hgt(\mathsf B(G_0,\dots,G_m))=\lambda_0+\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_m$, and the
box contains a chain of this order type.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Lower bound.} In stage $i$ let a coordinate $q_i\in G_i$ with
$\alpha_{q_i}=\lambda_i$ run through $\lambda_i$, keep every other nonretired
coordinate $0$ and retired coordinates at their sentinels. Every point of this
chain is below every point chosen in a later stage, since the varied
coordinate has reached its sentinel and every coordinate still active at both
stages was $0$. Concatenate.

\emph{Upper bound.} Induct on the number of groups. Let
$\Lambda=\max_i\lambda_i$, $j=\max\{i:\lambda_i=\Lambda\}$ and
$k=|\{i:\lambda_i=\Lambda\}|$; call a group large when $\lambda_i=\Lambda$ and
small otherwise. For $z\in\mathsf B_i$ with $i\le j$ let
$n_\Lambda(i)=|\{\ell<i:\lambda_\ell=\Lambda\}|$ and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:residual}
 \operatorname{res}_i(z)=\bigns_{\ell\ge i}\ \bigns_{q\in G_\ell}z_q
 \ \ns\bigns_{\substack{\ell<i\\ \lambda_\ell<\Lambda}}\ \bigns_{q\in G_\ell}\alpha_q .
\end{equation}
The second sum records the full capacities of already retired \emph{small}
groups; retired large groups are omitted and counted by $n_\Lambda(i)$. Every
summand is below $\Lambda$ and there are finitely many, so
$\operatorname{res}_i(z)<\Lambda$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:prefixrank}
 f(z)=\Lambda\cdot n_\Lambda(i)+\operatorname{res}_i(z)\qquad(z\in\mathsf B_i,\ i\le j).
\end{equation}
Since the last large group has not retired in this prefix, $n_\Lambda(i)\le k-1$
and $f(z)<\Lambda\cdot k$. If $z<z'$ lie in one stage, an active coordinate
rises and none falls, so $f$ rises. If they lie in stages $i<i'$ and a large
group retires in between, $n_\Lambda(i')>n_\Lambda(i)$ and
$f(z)<\Lambda(n_\Lambda(i)+1)\le\Lambda n_\Lambda(i')\le f(z')$. Otherwise the
count is unchanged, the residual replaces at least one active coordinate of a
retiring small group by its full capacity, and no residual term decreases; the
replacement is strict. So the prefix has height at most $\Lambda\cdot k$. The
stages after $j$ form a retirement box on $G_{j+1},\dots,G_m$, earlier
coordinates being fixed sentinels; by induction its height is at most
$\lambda_{j+1}+\cdots+\lambda_m$. Refining the order by putting every prefix
point below every suffix point gives an ordinal sum, an upper bound by
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:rankbound}. Thus
$\hgt(\mathsf B)\le\Lambda\cdot k+\lambda_{j+1}+\cdots+\lambda_m
=\lambda_0+\cdots+\lambda_m$: the terms below $\Lambda$ before its last
occurrence are absorbed and the $k$ occurrences of $\Lambda$ remain. When $j=m$
the suffix is empty.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Why the residual uses natural addition (07).} Path answers use
ordinary addition because coordinate groups retire in a prescribed order; the
within-stage bound uses natural addition because several active coordinates can
increase independently. Confusing the roles produces exactly the wrong
persistent-coordinate calculation. For $(\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2)=
(\omega,\omega^2,\omega)$ the first two stages have height at most $\omega^2$,
not $\omega^2+\omega$, because the last large group has not retired and its
active values absorb the finite natural-sum contributions of small groups; the
final stage contributes a new $\omega$, giving $\omega^2+\omega$. The prefix map
\eqref{nh:eq:prefixrank} makes this absorption rigorous for finitely many
coordinates of any capacities, uncountable ones included.

For a frontier chain $C=(A_0\prec\cdots\prec A_m)$ let $\Kmax_C(S)$ be the
induced subposet of $\Kmax(S)$ on those supports. By \eqref{nh:eq:noreturn} a
vertex in $A_i$ and $A_k$ ($i<k$) lies in every $A_j$ between, so
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:retirementgroups}
 G_i=A_i\setminus A_{i+1}\ (i<m),\qquad G_m=A_m
\end{equation}
are pairwise disjoint nonempty sets partitioning $\bigcup_iA_i$; each vertex
lies in the group of its last occurrence (07 Lemma~7.1).

\begin{proposition}[Exact height over one frontier chain; 07 Prop.~7.2]
\label{nh:prop:pathheight}
For every nonempty frontier chain $C$, $\hgt(\Kmax_C(S))=W(C)$, and a chain of
that type is explicitly constructible in $\Kmax_C(S)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Send $\langle A_i;x\rangle$ to the vector $\iota$ on $\bigcup_jA_j$ with
$\iota_q=\alpha_q$ if $q$ retired before stage $i$, $\iota_q=1+x_q$ if
$q\in A_i$, and $\iota_q=0$ if $q$ has not yet appeared. By the no-return
property these are all cases and the vector lies in stage $\mathsf B_i$ of the
box of the groups \eqref{nh:eq:retirementgroups}; shifted active values stay
below their capacities. If $\langle A_i;x\rangle\subseteq\langle A_j;y\rangle$
then $i\le j$; coordinates active at both stages do not decrease
(Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:support}), coordinates not yet introduced are $0$, retiring
coordinates rise to their sentinels and retired ones stay fixed. A proper
inclusion in one support raises an active coordinate, and a change of support
retires a group and raises a coordinate to its sentinel. So $\iota$ is strictly
increasing and Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:boxes} gives
$\hgt(\Kmax_C(S))\le\max_{G_0}\alpha+\cdots+\max_{G_m}\alpha=W(C)$. Conversely,
in support $A_i$ let a coordinate of largest capacity in $G_i$ run through its
whole fibre with every other active coordinate $0$. The varied coordinate is
absent from every later support, so all coordinates shared with a later support
are $0$ in the earlier profile, and each chosen profile at stage $i$ lies
strictly below each chosen profile at a later stage. The concatenation has type
$W(C)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:frontier}, except saturation]
By Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:normalization},
$\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q)))=\hgt(\Kmax(S))$. Apply
Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:chaincover} to the support map $\Kmax(S)\to\M(Q)$ and
Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:pathheight} to its chain pieces; one of the finitely
many chains realizes the maximum, and its constructed chain realizes the height.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Saturation does not reduce cost; 07 Prop.~7.3, 09 Lemma~6.1 and end of proof of Thm~7.2]
\label{nh:prop:saturation}
Every nonempty frontier chain extends to a saturated chain from $\min Q$ to
$\max Q$ without decreasing its cost $W$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $A\prec B\prec C$ then $A\setminus C\subseteq(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus C)$,
since a vertex of $A\setminus C$ is absent from $B$ or lies in $B\setminus C$.
Hence $w(A,C)\le\max\{w(A,B),w(B,C)\}\le w(A,B)+w(B,C)$, and replacing an edge
by the two refined edges cannot reduce the cost, ordinary addition being
nondecreasing in each argument. Prepending a smaller frontier prepends a
nonnegative ordinal. To append $B$ above a terminal $A$, note
$t(A)\le\max\{w(A,B),t(B)\}\le w(A,B)+t(B)$, since vertices of $A$ either retire
or lie in $B$. Add the bottom and top frontiers and fill every gap; finiteness
makes this terminate.
\end{proof}

This completes the second proof. No step sums the heights of whole adjacent
strata; such a sum is used only in the auxiliary upper bound after the last
largest retirement group, where it is justified (07).

\begin{corollary}[Uniform recovery; all four sources]\label{nh:cor:uniform}
If $\alpha_q=\alpha=\omega^\rho$ for every $q$, with $\rho>0$, then
$\hgt(\K(S_\alpha(Q)))=\alpha\cdot\hgt(\M(Q))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every transition and terminal weight is $\alpha$, so a chain with $\ell$
frontiers costs $\alpha\cdot\ell$; maximize over the finite poset $\M(Q)$.
\end{proof}

This is Theorem~\ref{thm:height} of Part~I, recovered as a special case and not
claimed as new (07 Cor.~8.1, 09 Cor.~7.3, 11 Cor.~6.3, 12 Cor.~5.5).

\section{Arbitrary ordinal fibres and exact point ranks}\label{nh:sec:arbitrary}

\subsection{Cantor-normal-form refinement}

Write each nonzero fibre length in Cantor normal form,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:cnf}
 \alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_{q,1}}n_{q,1}+\cdots+\omega^{\rho_{q,k_q}}n_{q,k_q},
 \qquad \rho_{q,1}>\cdots>\rho_{q,k_q}\ge0,\quad 0<n_{q,j}<\omega .
\end{equation}
Replace $q$ by a chain of $\sum_jn_{q,j}$ vertices carrying, in order,
$n_{q,1}$ blocks of weight $\omega^{\rho_{q,1}}$, then $n_{q,2}$ of the next
weight, and so on; exponent $0$ gives a singleton block. If $q<r$ put every
$q$-block below every $r$-block; blocks of incomparable vertices stay
incomparable; delete zero fibres. Call the result $Q^\sharp$ with pure weights
$\boldsymbol\lambda$, and let $U^\sharp$ be its blocks of positive exponent.

\begin{lemma}[Exact refinement; 12 Lemma~6.1; also 07, 09, 11]\label{nh:lem:refine}
$S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q)\cong S_{\boldsymbol\lambda}(Q^\sharp)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Inside a fibre the ordinal concatenation of its blocks is its Cantor normal
form; comparisons between distinct original fibres are unchanged by putting
every block below every block when the original indices were comparable.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All finite ordinal lexicographic sums; 12 Thm~6.2, 11 Cor.~7.2]
\label{nh:thm:arbitrary}
For every finite poset $Q$ and ordinals $\alpha_q\ge0$,
\[
 \boxed{\hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\bigr)=\Fdp(Q^\sharp)+\tau_{Q^\sharp},}
\]
with $\Fdp$ and $\tau$ from \eqref{nh:eq:dp} and \eqref{nh:eq:terminal}, and the
answer is attained by a chain. No countability, cofinality, purity or successor
restriction is imposed on the fibres.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An order isomorphism induces an isomorphism of finitely generated downsets.
Apply Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:refine} and Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:dag}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Second route (11 Thm~7.1).} Deleting the coordinates of length $1$
from the support model of $S_{\boldsymbol\lambda}(Q^\sharp)$ gives an exact
representation
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:mixed}
 \K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\cong
 Y\bigl(\Ant(Q^\sharp),\ (B\cap U^\sharp)_{B\in\Ant(Q^\sharp)},\ \boldsymbol\lambda|_{U^\sharp}\bigr):
\end{equation}
singleton blocks may occur in a state but carry no coordinate. 11 proves it
directly: a finitely generated downset has a unique finite antichain of maximal
actual points, at most one per fibre; over a singleton block the point is
forced, over $u\in B\cap U^\sharp$ it is a unique $\xi_u<\lambda_u$; one such
downset lies in another exactly when every maximal generator of the first lies
below one of the second, which for different fibres is the skeleton order and in
a shared infinite fibre is $\xi_u\le\eta_u$ (a shared fibre cannot be matched to
a strictly higher generator of an antichain already containing its index); and
\eqref{nh:eq:noreturn} gives \eqref{nh:eq:convex}. The persistence theorem then
gives the height and an attaining chain.

Cantor normal forms are finite for every ordinal, and no recursive notation
for the exponents is needed for the statement. An implementation needs a
representation and comparison procedure for them, and 11 does not claim a
uniform machine representation of all set ordinals. Expanding a coefficient
$n$ into $n$ blocks is a unary expansion: an algorithm polynomial in the number
of blocks is not polynomial in the bit length of a large coefficient, and the
ideal state space may already be exponential; no stronger complexity claim is
made (12).

\begin{corollary}[Nonzero limit fibres; 07 Thm~9.1, 09 Thm~9.2]\label{nh:cor:limits}
If every $\alpha_q$ is a nonzero limit ordinal, the last exponent of each
Cantor normal form is positive, every block of $Q^\sharp$ is infinite, and
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:frontier} applied to $Q^\sharp$ computes the height and
constructs an attaining chain. The reduction is not limited to countable
ordinals or to ordinals below $\eps$.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{The empty, finite and successor cases}\label{nh:sec:finitecases}

If all fibres are zero, $Q^\sharp=\varnothing$, $\Ant(Q^\sharp)=\{\varnothing\}$
and the answer is $1$, as required for $\K(\varnothing)=\{\varnothing\}$. If the
whole sum is finite with $N$ points, $Q^\sharp$ has $N$ singleton blocks, the
state order is the finite downset lattice, and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:finite}
 \hgt(\K(S))=N+1
\end{equation}
(each of the $N$ edges of a maximal ideal chain and the terminal stage cost
$1$). The fallback weight $1$ recovers exactly this case and must not be
dropped merely because the main application has infinite fibres (11). For one
chain, $\K(\alpha)\cong1+\alpha$, which is $\alpha$ for infinite $\alpha$ and
$n+1$ for finite $n$. Adjoining a greatest point adds one greatest element:
$\hgt(\K(P+1))=\hgt(\K(P))+1$; for the chain $\omega+1$ the expanded skeleton is
an $\omega$ block below a singleton, the stage costs are $1,\omega,1$, and the
answer is $\omega+1$ (12). Successor endpoints must be kept explicitly; the
maximal-frontier compression, which assumes no internally completed fibre, is
not the right formula for such inputs.

07 left finite fibres and successor tails outside its limit-fibre reduction and
observed that exponent $0$ cannot simply be admitted there: for two
incomparable singleton fibres $\K(P)$ is the four-element Boolean lattice of
height $3$, whereas the maximum-fibre value would be $1$. The support model
handles this case: \eqref{nh:eq:finite} gives $2+1=3$.

\subsection{Exact point ranks of finitely generated downsets (12)}

Let $K={\downarrow}F\in\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))$, normalized to its maximal
generators with support $A$ and positions $x_a<\alpha_a$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:localfibers}
 \beta_q=\begin{cases}
 \alpha_q,&q<a\ \text{for some }a\in A,\\
 x_q+1,&q\in A,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
(disjoint cases, $A$ being an antichain).

\begin{theorem}[Exact point-rank algorithm; 12 Thm~6.3]\label{nh:thm:localrank}
Let $H_K=\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\beta}(Q)))$, computed by
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:arbitrary}. Then $H_K$ is a successor and
$r_{\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))}(K)+1=H_K$. More precisely, in the expansion
$Q^\sharp_K$ of $S_{\boldsymbol\beta}(Q)$ every maximal block is a singleton, so
\[
 \boxed{r_{\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))}(K)=\Fdp(Q^\sharp_K).}
\]
This includes the empty downset, of rank $0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
As an induced poset, $K$ is $S_{\boldsymbol\beta}(Q)$. A finitely generated
downset of $S$ inside $K$ is generated by points of itself, hence of $K$, and a
finite subset of $K$ generates the same downset in $K$ and in $S$ because $K$ is
downward closed; so $\{L\in\K(S):L\subseteq K\}\cong\K(K)$. Ranks in a principal
lower interval agree with ambient ranks, and this interval has greatest element
$K$, so its height is $r(K)+1$. Every maximal nonzero fibre in
\eqref{nh:eq:localfibers} is a successor $x_a+1$ whose last Cantor term has
exponent $0$, so its last block is a singleton; every completed nonmaximal fibre
lies below one of them. Hence all maximal blocks of $Q^\sharp_K$ have weight $1$,
or $Q^\sharp_K$ is empty, and $\tau=1$ in both cases.
\end{proof}

This computes the rank of each specified compact downset exactly, not merely an
upper ranking function. In executable Cantor normal form the predecessor of
$H_K$ is obtained by subtracting one from its nonzero finite final coefficient,
never by commutative ordinal subtraction (12). 11 reaches the same ranks through
the exact model \eqref{nh:eq:mixed} and Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:local}; only exact
constructions, never the compressed model, are used for point ranks.
\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
\section{Worked examples and diagnostic counterexamples}\label{nh:sec:examples}

\subsection{The persistent-coordinate obstruction, now resolved (07, 11, 12)}

Let $Q$ consist of an isolated $x$ and a chain $y<z$, with
$(\alpha_x,\alpha_y,\alpha_z)=(\omega^2,\omega,\omega)$, the example of
\eqref{eq:counterheight}. Its frontiers are $\{x,y\}\prec\{x,z\}$; both
states have largest length $\omega^2$, but only $y$ retires at the
transition, so $w=\omega$ and $t(\{x,y\})=t(\{x,z\})=\omega^2$. The two-state
path costs
\[
 \underbrace{\omega}_{y\text{ retires}}+
 \underbrace{\omega^2}_{\text{terminal frontier}}=\omega^2,
\]
one-state paths cost $\omega^2$ as well, and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:obstructionfixed}
 \hgt\bigl(\K(\omega^2\sqcup(\omega+\omega))\bigr)=\omega^2,
\end{equation}
in agreement with \eqref{eq:counterheight}. The obstruction of Part~I is a
direct instance of the new rule, not an exception to it; replacing the
retirement cost by the whole first-stratum height would give the wrong value
$\omega^2+\omega^2$. An attaining chain first varies the $y$-coordinate through
$\omega$ at $x=0$, then the $x$-coordinate through $\omega^2$ in the next
frontier at $z=0$; its type is $\omega+\omega^2=\omega^2$, and the long
$x$-coordinate is never reset. In the peeling calculation the top label $x$
never departs, so $d_\Lambda=0$ on both states, $c_\Lambda=1$, $Z=\varnothing$,
and the rank bound has the form $\theta<\omega^2$ despite the state change and
the two lower fibres. The same holds with $\omega^2$ replaced by $\omega^\eta$
and the lower fibres by $\omega^\sigma$, for any $0<\sigma<\eta$ (11).

At ideal-state resolution (12), the six ideals and optimal prefix values are
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
 I&\varnothing&\{x\}&\{y\}&\{x,y\}&\{y,z\}&\{x,y,z\}\\ \hline
 \max I&\varnothing&\{x\}&\{y\}&\{x,y\}&\{z\}&\{x,z\}\\
 \Fdp(I)&0&1&1&2&\omega&\omega+1
\end{array}
\]
At the last state the two final additions give the prefix costs $2+\omega=\omega$
and $\omega+1$, and the latter wins; with terminal cost $\omega^2$,
$\Fdp(Q)+\tau_Q=(\omega+1)+\omega^2=\omega^2$. Maximizing prefixes is safe even
when different prefixes are later absorbed to the same value: the recurrence
records the actual greatest prefix rather than relying on future absorption.
Part~III's weight-$0$ table for the same example is
Remark~\ref{nh:wpo:rem:conventions}.

\subsection{The order of transitions matters (07)}

\begin{example}[07 Ex.~3.3]\label{nh:ex:diamond}
Let $Q$ be the two disjoint chains $a<c$ and $b<d$ with
$(\alpha_a,\alpha_b,\alpha_c,\alpha_d)=(\omega^2,\omega,\omega,\omega)$. The
frontier poset is a diamond, and its two saturated paths cost
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \{a,b\}\to\{b,c\}\to\{c,d\}:&\quad \omega^2+\omega+\omega=\omega^2+\omega\cdot2,\\
 \{a,b\}\to\{a,d\}\to\{c,d\}:&\quad \omega+\omega^2+\omega=\omega^2+\omega .
 \end{aligned}
\]
The first is larger. A rule that forgets the order of transitions loses
information even when every edge weight is known.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small},
 v/.style={draw=Olive,rounded corners=2pt,fill=Sage,minimum width=1.2cm,minimum height=.55cm}]
\node[v] (ab) at (0,0) {$\{a,b\}$};
\node[v] (bc) at (-2.3,1.5) {$\{b,c\}$};
\node[v] (ad) at (2.3,1.5) {$\{a,d\}$};
\node[v] (cd) at (0,3) {$\{c,d\}$};
\draw[->,thick,Olive] (ab)--node[left] {$\omega^2$} (bc);
\draw[->,thick,Olive] (bc)--node[left] {$\omega$} (cd);
\draw[->] (ab)--node[right] {$\omega$} (ad);
\draw[->] (ad)--node[right] {$\omega^2$} (cd);
\node[anchor=west] at (1,3) {terminal cost $\omega$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The frontier diamond of Example~\ref{nh:ex:diamond}; edges carry the
weight $w(A,B)$ of the retiring coordinate. The thick path is optimal.}
\label{nh:fig:diamond}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The weighted \texorpdfstring{$N$}{N}-poset (07, 11, 12)}

For $a<c$, $b<c$, $b<d$ the frontiers form the chain
$\{a,b\}\prec\{a,d\}\prec\{c,d\}$ of \eqref{eq:Nfrontiers}. The transition
weights are $\alpha_b$ and $\alpha_a$ and the terminal weight is
$\max(\alpha_c,\alpha_d)$, so for all positive pure fibres
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:N}
 \boxed{\hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(N))\bigr)=\alpha_b+\alpha_a+\max(\alpha_c,\alpha_d),}
\end{equation}
an ordered sum: $b$ retires first, then $a$. Paths that skip a frontier and
one-state paths are also in the maximum, but never exceed this one; both
transition weights are forced, and a persistent terminal coordinate can absorb
the first (07).

\centering

  dot/.style={circle,fill=Olive,inner sep=2pt},
  box/.style={draw=Olive,rounded corners=2pt,fill=Sage,inner sep=5pt}]

\draw[-{Stealth},thick,Olive] (B)--node[above] {$\alpha_a$}(C);

\end{tikzpicture}

\label{nh:fig:N}
\end{figure}

For the weighted example of Section~\ref{sec:general}, realized by the fibres
$(\alpha_a,\alpha_b,\alpha_c,\alpha_d)=(\omega^3,\omega,\omega,\omega^2)$, the
height is $\omega+\omega^3+\omega^2=\omega^3+\omega^2$: this supplies the
height missing from Part~I for that example (12). For
$(\omega^2,\omega^3,\omega,\omega^2)$ it is $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega^2=
\omega^3+\omega^2\cdot2$, whereas skipping the middle frontier would give only
$\omega^3+\omega^2$: intermediate retirement opportunities matter (12). Further
values on this one skeleton (11, 07):

\centering

\toprule
$\alpha_a$ & $\alpha_b$ & $\alpha_c$ & $\alpha_d$ & $\hgt(\K(S))$\\
\midrule
$\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega\cdot3$\\
$\omega^2$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega^2+\omega$\\
$\omega$ & $\omega^2$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega^2+\omega\cdot2$\\
$\omega^3$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega^2$ & $\omega^3+\omega^2$\\
$\omega^2$ & $\omega$ & $\omega$ & $\omega^2$ & $\omega^2\cdot2$\\

in the labelling of this report.}
