In the third row the $\omega^2$ coordinate retires first, leaving two
independent opportunities for later $\omega$ blocks; in the second, the early
$\omega$ block is absorbed by the later $\omega^2$. The difference is the
noncommutativity of ordinary addition, not a numerical approximation (11).

\subsection{Stratum heights do not determine the height}

\begin{corollary}[Stratum types do not determine the answer; 07 Cor.~8.4]
\label{nh:cor:strata}
The frontier poset together with the isomorphism type of each maximal-support
stratum does not determine $\hgt(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q)))$, even for pure
fibres on a three-vertex skeleton.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
On the skeleton $\{x,\,y<z\}$ compare $P_X=\omega^2\sqcup(\omega+\omega)$ and
$P_Y=\omega\sqcup(\omega^2+\omega^2)$. Both have the two-element frontier chain,
and every maximal-support stratum of either is isomorphic to
$\omega^2\times\omega$ (interchanging coordinates if needed). Yet
$\hgt(\K(P_X))=\omega+\omega^2=\omega^2$ and
$\hgt(\K(P_Y))=\omega^2+\omega^2=\omega^2\cdot2$. The difference is which
coordinate is shared and which retires.
\end{proof}

The first member of the pair is Part~I's counterexample. 12 gives a second
pair, comparing stratum heights only: the complete two-level poset $a,b<c,d$ with
weights $(\omega^2,\omega,\omega^2,\omega)$ also has two comparable frontiers,
each stratum of height $\omega^2$, but both lower coordinates retire and the
height is $\omega^2+\omega^2=\omega^2\cdot2$. Each claimed stratum height
follows without the main formula: the stratum is bounded by a product whose
largest pure coordinate is $\omega^2$ (natural-sum rank below $\omega^2$) and
contains a copy of that coordinate with the other active coordinates fixed
nonempty; this does not assert that the strata are isomorphic. The input
``frontier poset plus stratum heights'' is thus identical while the outputs
differ; the missing data are which coordinates persist. Part~III shows that
the resulting ambiguity is unbounded (Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:ambiguity}). An
exact finite invariant must retain not merely sizes or heights of pieces but the
capacities of coordinates lost along each transition (07).

\subsection{Successor endpoints carry real information (11)}

For two disjoint chains of type $\omega+1$, $\K$ commutes with the finite
disjoint union, so
$\K((\omega+1)\sqcup(\omega+1))\cong(\omega+1)\times(\omega+1)$, of height
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:successorproduct}
 \omega\cdot2+1 .
\end{equation}
Directly: $(\xi,\eta)\mapsto\xi\ns\eta$ is strictly increasing with largest
value $\omega\cdot2$ at $(\omega,\omega)$, bounding the height by
$\omega\cdot2+1$, and the chain
$(0,0)<(1,0)<\cdots<(\omega,0)<(\omega,1)<\cdots<(\omega,\omega)$ has exactly that
type. In the expanded skeleton each fibre is an $\omega$ block below a singleton
block, and the last state consists of singleton blocks only and contributes the
final $1$. Using only the maximal antichains of the expanded skeleton would
discard essential earlier states; all antichains retain them. Similarly
$\hgt((\omega^2+1)\times(\omega+1))=\omega^2+\omega+1$: the natural-sum bound has
maximum $\omega^2+\omega$, and the chain through $(\xi,0)$ for $\xi<\omega^2$,
then $(\omega^2,\eta)$ for $\eta<\omega$, then the greatest point attains its
successor.

These are heights of products $\K(\alpha_1\sqcup\alpha_2)\cong(1+\alpha_1)\times
(1+\alpha_2)$, not of finitary powersets \emph{of} products: the neighbouring
report on intrinsic ordinal approximations computes
$\hgt(\K((\omega+1)\times(\omega+1)))=\omega^2+1$ for the latter.

\subsection{A genuinely mixed Cantor-normal-form example (12)}

On the $N$-skeleton put
$\alpha_a=\omega^2+\omega\cdot2+1$, $\alpha_b=\omega+2$, $\alpha_c=\omega^2+1$,
$\alpha_d=\omega\cdot2$. Expansion gives eleven pure blocks and a downset lattice
with thirty-six states, and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:mixedexample}
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(N))\bigr)=\omega^2\cdot2+\omega\cdot2 .
\end{equation}
Adding all blocks of $a$, then of $b$, then of $c$, then of $d$ is an optimizing
path; its stage costs, the last terminal, are
$1,\ \omega^2,\ \omega,\ \omega,\ 1,\ \omega,\ 1,\ 1,\ \omega^2,\ 1,\ \omega,\ \omega$,
whose ordinary sum is \eqref{nh:eq:mixedexample}. The recurrence table in
\path{data/05-ranks-mixed_CNF_certificate.json} evaluates every state and
certifies that no path is larger. This is an exact symbolic consequence of
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:arbitrary}, not an extrapolation from finite approximations
to $\omega$.

\subsection{Exact ranks inside infinite orders}

In the persistent example let $K$ be generated by the point at position
$\omega+2$ of the $x$-fibre and the point at position $3$ of the $z$-fibre. The
whole $y$-fibre is present and $K\cong(\omega+3)\sqcup(\omega+4)$ as an induced
order; its finitary powerset has height $\omega\cdot2+6$, so
$r_{\K(S)}(K)=\omega\cdot2+5$. This agrees with the classical product point
rank $(\omega+2)\ns(\omega+3)=\omega\cdot2+5$; the local-rank algorithm does not
need to recognize the example as a product (12).

In $X=\omega^2\times\omega^2$ take $z=(\omega+1,\omega)$. Its capped principal
ideal is $(\omega+2)\times(\omega+1)$, of height $\omega\cdot2+2$, so
$r_X(z)=\omega\cdot2+1$. Directly, the natural-sum bound at $z$ is
$(\omega+1)\ns\omega=\omega\cdot2+1$, and the chain through $(n,0)$, then
$(\omega,n)$, then $(\omega,\omega)$ and $(\omega+1,\omega)$ has type
$\omega\cdot2+2$ (11).

\subsection{Transfinite exponents are not a new combinatorial case (12)}

On the $N$-skeleton take
$(\alpha_a,\alpha_b,\alpha_c,\alpha_d)=(\omega^{\omega+1},\omega^\omega,\omega^\omega,\omega^{\omega+1})$.
Formula \eqref{nh:eq:N} gives $\omega^\omega+\omega^{\omega+1}+\omega^{\omega+1}=
\omega^{\omega+1}\cdot2$. The proof uses only the order of the exponents, not a
finite-exponent approximation; Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:scale} makes this precise.

\subsection{Products of chains: an independent check}

The following classical product-height calculation is recorded as a separate
verification route for antichain skeletons; it is not a new-priority claim
(07, 12). The finite Milner--Rado expression is given, e.g., in
\cite[Theorem~2.20]{Hilton2016}.

\begin{lemma}[Ranks in finite products of chains; 12 App.~A, 07 Lemma~9.2]
\label{nh:lem:productrank}
For nonzero ordinal chains $\theta_1,\dots,\theta_k$ the rank of a tuple in the
componentwise product is $r(x_1,\dots,x_k)=x_1\ns\cdots\ns x_k$. Let $\rho_i$ be
the smallest exponent in the Cantor normal form of $\theta_i$ and
$\rho=\max_i\rho_i$; let $B_i$ be $\theta_i$ with one copy of its final
$\omega^\rho$ removed when $\rho_i=\rho$, and $\theta_i$ truncated to exponents
at least $\rho$ when $\rho_i<\rho$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:MR}
 \hgt\Bigl(\prod_i\theta_i\Bigr)=\Bigl(\bigns_iB_i\Bigr)+\omega^\rho .
\end{equation}
The empty product has height $1$; a product with a zero factor has height $0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (12)]
Write $z=\bigns_ix_i$. Strict monotonicity gives $\bigns_iy_i+1\le z$ when one
coordinate is decreased and the others fixed. Conversely fix $\gamma<z$, align
the exponents of $z$ and $\gamma$, and let $\delta$ be the greatest exponent at
which their coefficients differ; that of $z$ is larger. Choose $x_j$ contributing
to the coefficient at $\delta$, keep its terms above $\delta$, lower that
coefficient by one and replace its tail below $\delta$ by the tail of $\gamma$
below $\delta$. The resulting $y_j<x_j$, and the natural sum with this one
coordinate changed agrees with $z$ above $\delta$, has coefficient at $\delta$ at
least that of $\gamma$ and, when these are equal, lower tail at least that of
$\gamma$; so it is at least $\gamma$. This proves
$z=\sup\{(\bigns_iy_i)+1:\text{one }y_i<x_i,\text{ the others }y_i=x_i\}$, and
well-founded induction gives the rank formula. Hence
$\hgt(\prod_i\theta_i)=\sup_{x_i<\theta_i}((\bigns_ix_i)+1)$. For $\rho=0$
every factor is a successor with greatest element $B_i$, and the formula is the
rank of the greatest tuple plus one. For $\rho>0$ every $x_i<\theta_i$ is bounded
by some $B_i+t_i$ with $t_i<\omega^\rho$, and natural-sum closure gives the upper
bound. For the lower bound, set the coordinates with $\rho_i<\rho$ equal to
$B_i$ (allowed, since a positive lower tail remains), set all but one of the
others to $B_i$ and vary the last as $B_i+t$, $t<\omega^\rho$; these natural sums
are cofinal in the right side.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second route for limit factors (07)]
If every $\theta_i=\beta_i$ is a nonzero limit, write $\beta_i=b_i+\omega^{\tau_i}$
by removing one copy of the final monomial and put
$\Lambda=\max_i\omega^{\tau_i}$; then \eqref{nh:eq:MR} reads
$\hgt(\prod_i\beta_i)=(b_1\ns\cdots\ns b_r)+\Lambda$ (terms of $\bigns b_i$
below the exponent of $\Lambda$ are absorbed either way). The product of the
final intervals $[b_i,b_i+\omega^{\tau_i})$ is cofinal, and since natural
addition is monotone the supremum of values plus one may be restricted to it.
There $x_i=b_i+u_i=b_i\ns u_i$ with $u_i<\omega^{\tau_i}$, because the exponents
of $b_i$ are at least $\tau_i$, so the rank is $B\ns\bigns_iu_i$ with
$B=\bigns_ib_i$. All variable summands are below $\Lambda$, one of them varies
cofinally in $\Lambda$, and aligning Cantor normal forms gives
$\sup((B\ns\bigns_iu_i)+1)=B+\Lambda$. The rank recursion used for
$\bigns_ix_i$ is the one proved above: at a successor reduce a coordinate
with a nonzero finite term by one, and at a limit reduce inside the last
monomial of a coordinate of least trailing exponent.
\end{proof}

Since $\K(\bigsqcup_i\alpha_i)\cong\prod_i\K(\alpha_i)\cong\prod_i(1+\alpha_i)$,
the benchmark uses $\theta_i=1+\alpha_i$, not $\alpha_i$; the distinction matters
for finite and zero fibres. For infinite fibres $1+\beta=\beta$, and the lemma
checks Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:limits} independently on antichain skeletons. For
example (07),
\[
 \beta_1=\omega^2+\omega,\quad\beta_2=\omega^3+\omega^2
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \hgt(\K(\beta_1\sqcup\beta_2))=\omega^3+\omega^2\cdot2 .
\]

\subsection{Several tempting generalizations are false (11)}

The persistent example disproves adding full state heights.
Example~\ref{nh:ex:noreturn} shows that disappearance and reappearance can
destroy transitivity. The successor product shows that a maximum of impure
capacities is not the right product height. Replacing ordinary by natural sums
also fails: it would turn $\omega+\omega^2$ into $\omega^2+\omega$ in
\eqref{nh:eq:obstructionfixed}. Finally, replacing every infinite fibre by a
long finite chain makes the whole order finite, and then \eqref{nh:eq:finite}
forces height ``number of points plus one''. That is not an approximation scheme
for the transfinite formula: finite fibres have endpoints and pure infinite
fibres do not. The finite tests of Section~\ref{nh:sec:verification} check
representations and algorithms under their actual hypotheses, not a limit of
finite height counts.

\section{Structural consequences}\label{nh:sec:consequences}

\begin{corollary}[Small output and universal bounds; 12 Cor.~8.1, 07 Cor.~8.2]
\label{nh:cor:smalloutput}
Let $Q^\sharp$ have $N$ pure blocks. Every exponent of
$\Fdp(Q^\sharp)+\tau_{Q^\sharp}$ is $0$ or the exponent of an input block, and
the sum of its Cantor-normal-form coefficients is at most $N+1$. If $Q$ is
nonempty and all fibres are positive pure, $\alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$, then the
coefficient sum is at most $|Q|$, no finite term occurs, the leading exponent is
$\max_q\rho_q$, and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:bounds}
 \max_{q\in Q}\alpha_q\ \le\ \hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\bigr)\ \le\
 \bigns_{q\in Q}\alpha_q .
\end{equation}
Both bounds are sharp.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A winning ideal path has $N+1$ pure stage costs; ordinary addition can merge
equal exponents and erase smaller tails, but cannot introduce an exponent or
increase the number of pure summands. For the compressed formula choose one
coordinate attaining each retirement cost and one attaining the terminal cost;
a chosen retiring coordinate never appears later, by convexity, so these are at
most $|Q|$ distinct coordinates, all of positive exponent. The cost is an
ordinary sum of their monomials, hence at most their natural sum and at most the
right side of \eqref{nh:eq:bounds}. The principal downsets of one fibre form a
chain of its full type, giving the left inequality, and the leading exponent
follows from the two bounds. An antichain skeleton attains the left bound; a
chain skeleton with fibre exponents in nonincreasing order attains the right
one, where natural and ordinary sums agree (07).
\end{proof}

The optimization may have many states while the answer has a short normal form;
the difficulty is choosing the order of useful retirements, not generating new
exponent expressions (12).

\begin{corollary}[Exponent-scale invariance; 12 Cor.~8.2, 07 Cor.~8.3]
\label{nh:cor:scale}
Fix a finite pure-block skeleton and its pattern of equal and unequal
exponents. Replace the distinct exponents by any strictly increasing list of
ordinals, keeping $0$ at $0$ when it occurs and positive exponents positive.
The output keeps its Cantor-normal-form coefficient pattern, with the exponents
relabelled in the same way, and an optimizing path remains optimizing. In
particular, if all $\rho_q>0$ (07's standing hypothesis) and
$\phi:\{\rho_q\}\to\mathrm{Ord}\setminus\{0\}$ is strictly
increasing and $\hgt(\K(\lxsi_q\omega^{\rho_q}))=\omega^{\sigma_1}c_1+\cdots+
\omega^{\sigma_s}c_s$ with $\sigma_1>\cdots>\sigma_s$, then
$\hgt(\K(\lxsi_q\omega^{\phi(\rho_q)}))=\omega^{\phi(\sigma_1)}c_1+\cdots+
\omega^{\phi(\sigma_s)}c_s$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Include exponent $0$ among the scale labels to account for default costs. On
finite ordinal polynomials in these labels a strictly increasing relabelling
preserves comparison, maximum and ordinary addition: the algorithms use only
equality and relative order of exponents and finite coefficient arithmetic
(the addition rule deletes smaller trailing exponents and merges equal boundary
exponents). So it commutes with every step of \eqref{nh:eq:dp}, including tie
comparisons and terminal addition (12), and equally with every path evaluation
of \eqref{nh:eq:cost} and their finite maximum (07).
\end{proof}

The hypothesis $\rho_q>0$ of the last sentence cannot be dropped, since $\phi$
may move the exponent $0$ that the first sentence keeps fixed. For a single
vertex with $\rho=0$ the height is $\hgt(\K(1))=2=\omega^0\cdot2$, while
$0\mapsto1$ would predict $\omega\cdot2$ for $\hgt(\K(\omega))=\omega$. (This
example is an editorial addition of the merge.)

The formula is thus a finite combinatorial calculation on the \emph{weak order
pattern} of the exponents; their magnitudes, cardinalities and cofinalities are
not extra inputs, though the actual cardinalities and cofinalities of the
output of course change after relabelling (07). This is why integer exponent
ranks suffice in 07's implementation. For arbitrary fibres, apply the statement
to the expanded blocks; the block skeleton is unchanged as long as the ordering
and equalities of exponents and all finite coefficients are kept. This gives a
parameter-uniform family of identities, not separate computations for finitely
many integer exponents (12). Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:parametric} is the version
for abstract systems, with its certificate.

\begin{proposition}[A sufficient extension to non-chain coordinates; 12 Prop.~8.3]
\label{nh:prop:generalcoordinates}
In Definition~\ref{nh:def:system} replace each pure coordinate $\lambda_q$ by a
well-founded poset $P_q$ with a least element, height $\lambda_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$,
and an explicitly given chain of type $\lambda_q$, and use the order of $P_q$ on
shared coordinates. Then the formula of Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence} remains
valid.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For the upper bound replace $x_q$ in \eqref{nh:eq:R} by its rank
$r_{P_q}(x_q)<\lambda_q$; ranks are nondecreasing on weak and strictly increasing
on strict comparisons, so every step of Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:fixedupper} holds. For
the lower bound vary a retiring coordinate along its given chain, the others at
their least elements. The finite-tree argument is unchanged.
\end{proof}

This does not assert that the finitely generated downsets of arbitrary non-chain
fibres have the same exact support representation; 12 names that representation
question as an obstacle to a broad WPO extension. Part~III supplies such a
representation for WPO fibres whose finitary powersets have pure height.

\subsection{The frontier-graph algorithm (07)}\label{nh:sec:frontieralgorithm}

For $A\in\M(Q)$ let $\Fdp^{\M}(A)$ be the largest cost of a nonempty frontier
chain starting at $A$. By Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:frontier},
\begin{equation}\label{nh:eq:frontierDP}
 \Fdp^{\M}(A)=\max\Bigl(t(A),\ \max_{A\prec B}\bigl(w(A,B)+\Fdp^{\M}(B)\bigr)\Bigr),
 \qquad
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(Q))\bigr)=\Fdp^{\M}(\min Q),
\end{equation}
the inner maximum omitted at $\max Q$. Reverse topological evaluation is
well-defined on the finite poset, left addition of the fixed ordinal $w(A,B)$
preserves finite maxima, and Proposition~\ref{nh:prop:saturation} allows cover
edges only. A certificate is an attaining saturated path, its retirement groups,
one largest-capacity vertex per group and the ordinary sum of those capacities.
Checking it confirms the lower-bound chain recipe; it does not show that no
better path exists unless the finite program is also checked.

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python,caption={Height computation after the frontiers are enumerated (07).}]
for A in reverse_topological_order(frontiers):
    F[A] = largest_fibre(A)
    path[A] = [A]
    for B in successors(A):
        candidate = largest_fibre(A minus B) + F[B]  # ordinal +
        if candidate > F[A]:
            F[A] = candidate
            path[A] = [A] + path[B]
return F[minimal_vertices(Q)], path[minimal_vertices(Q)]
\end{lstlisting}

With $d$ distinct fibre exponents a cost is a decreasing list of
exponent--coefficient pairs; to add a right operand with leading exponent
$\tau$, erase the left operand's terms below $\tau$, merge coefficients at $\tau$
and append the rest; no floating point is used. With $M=|\M(Q)|$, all frontier
comparisons cost $O(M^2|Q|)$ with plain set representations, the program
$O(M^2d)$ exponent comparisons and coefficient operations on the full graph,
naive cover extraction $O(M^3)$ (not automatically preferable), and enumerating
antichains by subsets $O(2^{|Q|}|Q|^2)$. The method is polynomial in an explicit
frontier graph, \emph{not} asserted to be polynomial in the size of the
skeleton. For arbitrary ordinal exponents these are operation counts relative to
an oracle deciding their comparisons; 07's code uses positive integers,
interpreted by Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:scale} as order labels for any finite set of
positive exponents. Limit-fibre expansion has $\sum_{q,j}n_{q,j}$ vertices, so
large coefficients make naive expansion expensive; a compressed implementation
is a separate problem.
\section{Implementation, verification and proof audit of Part~II}
\label{nh:sec:verification}

Each of 07, 11 and 12 ships an independent standard-library Python package; 09's
is described in Section~\ref{nh:wpo:sec:verification}. The files are shipped
under the prefixes of Table~\ref{nh:tab:sources} in \path{code/} and
\path{data/}, and the delivered text of their scripts, Makefiles and audits
still uses the delivered names (\path{code/verify.py}, \texttt{examples/\dots},
\texttt{data/\dots}). The commands below are quoted in that delivered layout;
the report's \texttt{README.md} explains how to recreate it on a scratch copy.
Every suite rewrites its own evidence files and must not be run inside the
repository. The recorded runs used Python~3.13.5. Before placement every suite
was rerun on a copy; fresh outputs equalled the recorded ones apart from line
endings, interpreter versions and timings, and a 152-instance comparison fed
through all four programs found no disagreement (placement commit
\texttt{1a1396d4d}; that comparison script is not shipped).

\subsection{12: ideal-state heights and point ranks}

The ordinal class stores hereditary Cantor normal forms below $\eps$; a finite
ordinal is a nonnegative JSON integer and a nonfinite one a list of
\texttt{[exponent, coefficient]} pairs in strictly decreasing exponent order,
so $\omega^2+\omega\cdot3+2$ is \texttt{[[2,1],[1,3],[0,2]]}. Arithmetic is
exact; cyclic inputs, invalid normal forms and out-of-range generator positions
are rejected, and a default cap of twenty expanded vertices is a safety limit
for the exponential state space, not a mathematical restriction. The principal
commands of the delivered layout are \texttt{python code/verify.py} and
\texttt{python code/heights.py examples/\{weighted\_N,mixed\_CNF,point\_rank\}.json};
an input with a \texttt{generators} field requests a point rank. The recorded
run passed 63{,}270 assertions; its ledger is shipped as
\path{data/05-ranks-verification.json}. The census is $1,1,2,7,40,357$
naturally labelled posets on zero through five vertices ($408$), covering every
isomorphism type at these sizes but not isomorphism-reduced.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Validation family (12) & Assertions or cases\\
\midrule
Dynamic program versus all linear extensions & 2,196\\
Full recurrence-and-path certificate checks & 2,196\\
All-support versus maximal-frontier formula & 860\\
Recovery of the uniform frontier theorem & 814\\
Disjoint-chain cases versus the classical product-height formula & 820\\
Finite support-order pairs versus generated downset inclusion & 12,585\\
Exact local ranks versus directly computed finite ranks & 1,603\\
Finite support injectivity / direct heights / size-plus-one checks & 291 each\\
Ordinary / natural addition associativity on a fixed ordinal grid & 19,683 each\\
Strict absorption inequalities & 1,809\\
Exponent-scale relabelling checks & 100\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Principal families in 12's deterministic run; the ledger also records
normal-form, example and invalid-input tests.}
\label{nh:tab:ranksverify}
\end{table}

The path oracle enumerates linear extensions and updates active sets itself,
without the production recurrence or cost helper; the finite-rank oracle builds
actual finite downsets and ranks them by strict inclusion; the Cartesian oracle
uses a separate Milner--Rado calculation. They share the separately tested
ordinal representation, so they are ``not three independent proof assistants''
(12). No test replaces an infinite ordinal by a large integer and reads off a
trend; infinite examples are evaluated symbolically after the theorems reduce
them to finite calculations. The ledger certifies the recorded execution of the
finite and symbolic suite, not novelty, absence of proof errors, or Lean kernel
checking.

\subsection{07: retirement paths}

The evaluator \path{code/02-retire-retirement.py} accepts cover edges or any
acyclic relation with the intended closure and rejects cycles, missing fibre
values, nonpositive exponents and finite tails in limit-fibre mode. Its outputs
give a height, a saturated maximizing path, groups and pivot vertices, and for
limit fibres the original owner of each expanded vertex. The recorded run of its
\texttt{verify.py} passed 3{,}210{,}888 assertions
(\path{data/02-retire-verification_results.json}) on the $1,2,7,40,357,4824$
naturally labelled posets on one through six vertices ($5{,}231$): every
exponent assignment in $\{1,2,3\}^n$ through five vertices ($90{,}201$ weighted
instances) and eight deterministic assignments per six-vertex order, $128{,}793$
weighted instances in all. The full-comparison program agrees with a cover-edge
program on all of them, and on the $90{,}201$ smaller ones both agree with
explicit enumeration of all nonempty frontier chains evaluated by a separate
monomial-word routine. Minimal completion, Hoare monotonicity, strict
retirement, disjoint retirement groups, uniform recovery, exponent relabelling,
coefficient budgets and the universal bounds are checked throughout; sampled
profile maps are checked for strict monotonicity through four vertices;
limit-fibre expansion is compared with ordinary sums on chain skeletons and with
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:productrank} on antichain skeletons ($258$ instances each); and
sampled prefix maps \eqref{nh:eq:prefixrank} are checked for range and
strictness. These tests do not compute the rank of an infinite
descending-sequence tree, prove anything for all ordinals, establish
consistency, or certify novelty; they look for finite combinatorial errors,
wrong ordinal arithmetic, wrong boundary conventions and disagreements between
implementations.

\subsection{11: persistent systems and local ranks}

11 ships an ordinal engine (\path{code/04-peel-ordinals.py}, below $\eps$),
the solver \path{code/04-peel-persistent_height.py} (compact pure-frontier
construction, all-ordinal expanded construction, weighted-path and peeling
algorithms, phase refinement, exact point ranks), and two test programs. The
solver rejects cyclic skeletons, invalid normal forms and violations of
\eqref{nh:eq:convex}; general expansion has a default guard of $18$ blocks
(configurable) and phase refinement one of $4{,}096$ refined states, and the
algorithm stays exponential in the worst case. The main verifier passed
129{,}593 assertions (\path{data/04-peel-verification.json}) on the same
labelled census $1,2,7,40,357,4824$.

\midrule

Accepted abstract two-label persistent systems through four states & 9,714\\
Rejected abstract assignments violating \eqref{nh:eq:convex} & 1,011\\
Finite all-antichain representation bijections & 147\\
Finite representation order-pair comparisons & 47,221\\

Weighted-path answers were compared with independent peeling answers and with
exhaustive path enumeration on the smaller cases, and pure maximal-frontier
answers with exact all-antichain answers; arithmetic used an independent
coefficient-array model below $\omega^4$. The local-rank program checked $940$
finite coordinate systems and $6{,}132$ individual finite point ranks against
direct enumeration, plus $37$ symbolic regressions
(\path{data/04-peel-local_rank_verification.json}). These are not a Lean
formalization, not an evaluation of infinite bad-sequence trees and not a
replacement for the rank-map proof; agreement of two algorithms alone would not
prove that either computes the height.

\subsection{Proof dependencies, delicate points and formalization routes}
\label{nh:sec:audit}

\subsubsection{Dependency map}

The principal chain of 12 is: Cantor normal form $\Rightarrow$ strict absorption
$\Rightarrow$ fixed-chain upper bound; convex lifetimes $\Rightarrow$ attaining
chain; finite tree union plus these bounds $\Rightarrow$ finite persistence
theorem; support antichains $\Rightarrow$ ideal-state recurrence; finite CNF
refinement $\Rightarrow$ arbitrary ordinal fibres; principal lower intervals
$\Rightarrow$ exact point ranks. The compressed frontier theorem is a separate
embedding-sandwich corollary. Neither the product-height benchmark nor Part~I's
survivor formula is a premise. 07's route and 11's peeling route are summarized
below.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.27\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
Result (07) & Depends on & Sensitive point\\
\midrule
Profile criterion & finite generators & equality constraints only on shared vertices\\
Maximal-support normalization & minimal completion; left shift & empty profile and strictness, not cofinality\\
Finite-chain cover & prefix-closed descending trees & finitely many chain pieces\\
Retirement-box theorem & natural-sum residual; finite induction & retired large groups counted once, not per coordinate\\
Fixed-path theorem & no return; box theorem & active shared coordinates never reset\\
Global height formula & normalization; chain cover; path theorem & height, not maximal order type\\
Limit-fibre extension & finite Cantor normal forms & no exponent-zero blocks\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{07's proof-dependency audit.}
\label{nh:tab:retireaudit}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Where a shortcut would change the result (11).} First,
\eqref{nh:eq:convex} is used both for transitivity and when concatenating
coordinate blocks. Second, state changes that preserve every highest coordinate
still need a strictly increasing lower-scale term, which is why
$\kappa\cdot\depth(t)$ occurs in \eqref{nh:eq:peelR}. Third, the terminal region
is $Z=\{d_\Lambda=c_\Lambda\}$, not the set of states whose active maximum happens
to be small. Fourth, the fallback weight is one, which is essential for
endpoints and local ranks. In the compact frontier proof the two embeddings give
equality of heights, not an isomorphism; only the exact mixed-block and
phase-refinement isomorphisms are used for point ranks.

\paragraph{07's checklist for an independent reader.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item In a profile comparison, a vertex active in both supports cannot be
dominated by a different target vertex; hence exactly the shared-coordinate
inequalities of Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:support}.
\item In the completion proof, a hypothetical $b<u$ must be shown incomparable
with \emph{every} old support vertex before minimality of $u$ is used.
\item In the normalization use $1+x$, not $x+1$: left addition is strictly
increasing and stays below every infinite pure fibre, also at limit values.
\item In the tree cover, cover complete descending sequences, not points.
\item In the prefix residual, record retired small coordinates at full capacity
and omit retired large groups; count large groups, not their coordinates.
\item At a path transition, an old pivot lies below a strictly higher support
vertex and never returns; shared old coordinates were kept at $0$.
\item In the block extension, check that the final Cantor exponent is positive;
finite tails are outside that reduction (and inside
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:arbitrary}).
\end{enumerate}
None of these checks invokes the status or correctness of any other repository
report.

No theorem of Part~II imports a large-cardinal assertion or an admitted
repository or Lean declaration; the repository is the source of the target and of
credited examples, not a logical dependency. The only background facts are
elementary ordinal arithmetic, well-founded rank induction and finite-poset
arguments. No proof assistant has checked any of it, and no placeholder
formalization is supplied that could be mistaken for one (07); no Lean source is
delivered as though checked (12).

\subsubsection{Formalization routes proposed by the sources}

All three sources separate finite combinatorics from ordinal ranks and define
height by well-founded rank, never as the output of the proposed algorithm.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{12:} formalize strict absorption and the fixed-chain rank function,
then finite tree union and the support-antichain isomorphism (the pure-block
theorem), then CNF refinement, recurrence correctness and the
principal-lower-interval rank identity. A certificate checker should be proved
sound against the recurrence rather than make its JSON output an axiom;
proof-relevant certificates need only the finite poset, weights, all ideal values
and a path, with diagrams and formatted ordinals outside the trusted interface.
It can be staged without the repository's large-cardinal material or its
unrefereed research theorems.
\item \emph{07:} finite antichains, Hoare order, completion and no-return first
(testable on finite skeletons), then support profiles and the shifted
normalization; for the transfinite layer, strict-map monotonicity, the tree
union, and the box theorem, which needs only finite products of ordinals,
natural sums, sentinels and induction on the group list. The first milestone is
the prefix map \eqref{nh:eq:prefixrank}, the one place where a subtle nonuniform
upper bound enters; a second is strictness of the normalization on the empty
profile; finally, connect an executable frontier-graph evaluator to the
mathematical maximum.
\item \emph{11:} define the tagged product over a finite state poset and label
type, prove the order axioms from \eqref{nh:eq:convex} and well-foundedness
separately; formalize departure indicators, $d_\Lambda$, $c_\Lambda$ and upward
closure of $Z$ by natural-number induction; in the ordinal layer, natural-sum
monotonicity, closure below a pure power and Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:upperstep}, with
the induction on the number of active lengths; then the exact all-antichain and
phase-refinement isomorphisms, the compression as two embeddings (never coerced
into an isomorphism), the principal-ideal lemma, and a small checked interpreter
for CNF expressions. This is a proposed plan, not a completed verification.
\end{itemize}
\clearpage
\phantomsection\part*{Part III.\quad Nonuniform heights: WPO fibres of pure finitary-powerset height}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part III.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } Nonuniform heights: WPO fibres of pure finitary-powerset height}

\noindent\emph{This part is manuscript 09. Its hypothesis is incomparable with
those of Part~II: the fibres need not be chains, but their finitary powersets
must have positive pure height, and finite or successor fibres are excluded.
It keeps its own hypotheses and 09's weight-$0$ convention for empty
retirements.}

\section{Setting, hypotheses and classical inputs}\label{nh:wpo:sec:setting}

\subsection{The hypothesis}

Let $Q$ be a finite nonempty poset and let each fibre $P_q$ be a nonempty
well-partial-order, and write $S=\lxsi_{q\in Q}P_q$ as in
Problem~\ref{prob:target}. Throughout this part assume
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:hyp}
 h_q:=\hgt(\K(P_q))=\omega^{\rho_q},\qquad\rho_q>0 .
\end{equation}
09 writes $\alpha_q$ for $h_q$. It is a height of a finitary powerset,
\emph{not} a fibre length and \emph{not} $\hgt(P_q)$: for an ordinal chain
fibre $\alpha$, $h_q=\hgt(\K(\alpha))=1+\alpha=\alpha$ when $\alpha$ is
infinite, but in general $\hgt(P_q)$ alone does not suffice. A \emph{positive
pure power} means $\omega^\rho$ with $\rho>0$: infinite, a limit, additively
indecomposable, and closed under finite natural sums of smaller ordinals. The
restriction $\rho>0$ is deliberate; the finite ordinal $1$ is not admitted as a
weight in this part. Part~I's hypothesis concerns maximal order type,
$\ot(\K(P_q))=\omega^{\beta_q}$; the hypothesis here concerns height, and
neither is assumed from the other.

\subsection{Classical inputs}

A WPO is a partial order with no infinite descending sequence and no infinite
antichain. If $P$ is a WPO, so is $\K(P)$ \cite{AbriolaEtAl2024}: enumerate each
finite set as a word and apply Higman's lemma, as in Section~\ref{sec:tools}.
Finite disjoint unions of WPOs are WPOs, and adding cross-fibre comparabilities
cannot create a bad sequence, so $S$ is a WPO.

\begin{theorem}[Classical maximal-chain theorem \cite{Wolk1967,MMS2012}; 09 Thm~2.1]
\label{nh:wpo:thm:wolk}
A WPO $X$ has a chain of order type $\hgt(X)$; hence
$\hgt(X)=\max\{\otp(C):C\subseteq X\text{ a chain}\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[A compactness proof of the classical input (09)]
For $\beta<r_X(x)$ there is $y<x$ of rank exactly $\beta$: among predecessors
of $x$ of rank at least $\beta$ choose one of least rank; were its rank larger
than $\beta$, the rank recurrence would give a lower-rank predecessor of rank at
least $\beta$. So each level $L_\beta=\{x:r_X(x)=\beta\}$, $\beta<\hgt(X)$, is
nonempty, and it is finite, since equal-rank elements are incomparable and a
WPO has no infinite antichain. Give the levels the discrete topology; their
product is compact in ZFC. For $\beta<\gamma<\hgt(X)$ impose the closed
condition $x_\beta<x_\gamma$. Finitely many conditions involve finitely many
levels; choose an element at the greatest of them and use the predecessor
observation repeatedly to obtain a chain through the others. So the conditions
have the finite intersection property, and compactness gives a family
satisfying all of them, a chain of type $\hgt(X)$. Conversely rank is strictly
increasing on chains, so no chain is longer.
\end{proof}

This is the form of Wolk's theorem (Theorem~9 of \cite{Wolk1967}; stated as
Theorem~1.2 of \cite{MMS2012}) used here. Maximal order type \emph{among
chains} is not maximal order type \emph{among linear extensions}; only the
former is used. An arbitrary well-founded poset need not admit this chain
interpretation of rank, so the WPO assumption must not be dropped.

\subsection{Support ideals}

Write $\J(Q)$ for the downsets (``ideals'', without directedness) of $Q$. For
$I\in\J(Q)$ call $\max I$ its \emph{active set} (09's ``active frontier''
$F(I)$, which need not be a frontier) and $C(I)=I\setminus\max I$ its
\emph{completed part}; $C(I)=D(\max I)$ in Part~I's notation, and $C(I)$ concerns
vertices of $Q$, not a chain. Both are empty for $I=\varnothing$.

\section{The exact formula}\label{nh:wpo:sec:statement}

For $I\subseteq J$ in $\J(Q)$ define, with the \emph{empty maximum equal to
$0$},
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:weight}
 \Wz(I,J)=\max\{h_q:q\in\max I\setminus\max J\},\qquad
 \Tz(I)=\max\{h_q:q\in\max I\},\quad \Tz(\varnothing)=0 .
\end{equation}
A linear extension $\pi=(q_1,\dots,q_n)\in\LE(Q)$ gives the saturated ideal
chain $I_j=\{q_1,\dots,q_j\}$, $I_0=\varnothing$, and the value
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:pathvalue}
 \Vz(\pi)=\Wz(I_0,I_1)+\Wz(I_1,I_2)+\cdots+\Wz(I_{n-1},I_n)+\Tz(Q),
\end{equation}
every plus sign ordinary ordinal addition.

\begin{theorem}[Pure-height substitution; 09 Thm~3.1]\label{nh:wpo:thm:main}
Under \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:hyp},
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:main}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \hgt\Bigl(\K\Bigl(\lxs_{q\in Q}P_q\Bigr)\Bigr)=\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)
 :=\max_{\pi\in\LE(Q)}\Vz(\pi).}
\end{equation}
For each $\pi$ there is an explicitly described chain in the powerset of order
type $\Vz(\pi)$, once height-attaining chains in the component powersets have
been chosen; in particular the maximum is attained.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}[Ordinal fibres; 09 Cor.~3.2]\label{nh:wpo:cor:ordinal}
For arbitrary positive ordinals $\rho_q$,
$\hgt(\K(\lxsi_q\omega^{\rho_q}))=\Phi(Q;(\omega^{\rho_q})_q)$, with no
restriction to countable exponents or to a notation system.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The nonempty finitely generated downsets of an ordinal chain $\alpha$ are the
initial intervals $[0,\xi]$, so $\K(\alpha)$ has type $1+\alpha=\alpha$ for
infinite $\alpha$. Apply Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:main}.
\end{proof}

This recovers the chain-fibre formula of Part~II by an independent route, and
Section~\ref{nh:wpo:sec:compression} shows that $\Phi$ equals the frontier
formula \eqref{nh:eq:frontierheight} with $\alpha_q$ replaced by $h_q$.

\section{A normal form for finitely generated downsets}\label{nh:wpo:sec:profiles}

\begin{lemma}[No internally completed fibre; 09 Lemma~4.1]\label{nh:wpo:lem:notop}
A fibre satisfying \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:hyp} has no finite cofinal subset; its
underlying set is not an element of $\K(P_q)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A finite cofinal subset would generate $P_q$, giving $\K(P_q)$ a greatest
element and a successor height, contrary to the limit $\omega^{\rho_q}$.
\end{proof}

This is the height analogue of Part~I's remark that purity of $\ot(\K(P_q))$
excludes finite cofinal subsets.

\begin{proposition}[Support-profile normal form; 09 Prop.~4.2]\label{nh:wpo:prop:profiles}
Every nonempty $D\in\K(S)$ is uniquely represented by a nonempty ideal
$I\in\J(Q)$ and nonempty $B_q\in\K(P_q)\setminus\{\varnothing\}$ for
$q\in\max I$, with
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:profile}
 D\cap P_q=\begin{cases}
 P_q,&q\in C(I),\\ B_q,&q\in\max I,\\ \varnothing,&q\notin I,
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
and every such profile represents an element of $\K(S)$. For profiles
$(I,(B_q))$ and $(J,(E_q))$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:comparison}
 D\subseteq E\iff I\subseteq J\ \text{and}\ B_q\subseteq E_q\ \
 (q\in\max I\cap\max J).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Take a finite generating set of $D$. Its support $I=\{q:D\cap P_q\ne\varnothing\}$
is a downset, since a point of a strictly higher fibre lies above every point of
a lower one. For $q\in\max I$ no generator in a strictly higher fibre contributes
to $D\cap P_q$, which is therefore a nonempty finitely generated downset of
$P_q$, proper by Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:notop}. For $q\in C(I)$ some $r\in\max I$
satisfies $q<r$, and one point of $D\cap P_r$ forces the whole $q$-fibre into
$D$. This proves \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:profile} and uniqueness. Conversely the union
of finite generating sets of the $B_q$ generates exactly \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:profile}:
every vertex of $C(I)$ lies below a vertex of $\max I$, and every $B_q$ is
nonempty. For inclusion, support inclusion and inclusion on common active fibres
are necessary; they suffice because a formerly active fibre that is no longer
active is now completed and completed fibres stay completed.
\end{proof}

A fixed-support stratum is thus a finite product of nonempty component
powersets. Different strata are \emph{not} arranged as an ordinary
lexicographic sum: common active coordinates keep the comparison conditions of
\eqref{nh:wpo:eq:comparison}, and ignoring them is exactly the incorrect
resetting operation that the transition formula avoids.

\begin{lemma}[Nonempty height-attaining component chains; 09 Lemma~4.3]
\label{nh:wpo:lem:componentchains}
For each $q$ there is a chain $\mathbf C_q\subseteq\K(P_q)\setminus\{\varnothing\}$
of order type $h_q$; it has a least element $b_q$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$\K(P_q)$ is a WPO of height $h_q$; apply Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:wolk}. If the
chain contains $\varnothing$, delete that first element; removing one first
element from an infinite additively indecomposable type leaves the type
unchanged.
\end{proof}

\section{The ordinal mechanism: bounded progress is absorbed later}
\label{nh:wpo:sec:absorption}

\begin{lemma}[A finite product bound; 09 Lemma~5.1]\label{nh:wpo:lem:productbound}
Let a chain vary in finitely many coordinates whose rank values lie below
positive pure powers $h_q$, partitioned into unrestricted coordinates $R$ and
bounded coordinates $B$, every $p\in B$ being bounded throughout the chain by an
element of rank $\beta_p<h_p$. Put $\lambda=\max_{q\in R}h_q$ and
$\gamma=\bigns_{p\in B}(\beta_p+1)$ (empty maximum and sum $0$). Then the chain
has order type at most $\lambda\ns\gamma$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Map each tuple to the natural sum of its coordinate ranks; along a strict product
comparison one coordinate rank rises and none falls, so the map is strictly
increasing. The unrestricted part is below $\lambda$ (or $0$) and the bounded
part below $\gamma$ (or $0$), so the chain embeds in $\lambda\ns\gamma$ whenever
there is a coordinate; the coordinate-free case is not needed for nonempty
strata.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Deferred absorption; 09 Lemma~5.2]\label{nh:wpo:lem:absorb}
Let each of $\lambda_0,\dots,\lambda_k$ be $0$ or a positive pure power, with
$\lambda_k>0$; for $i<k$ put $L_i=\max_{j>i}\lambda_j$, and suppose
$\gamma_i<L_i$ for $i<k$ and $\gamma_k=0$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:absorb}
 (\lambda_0\ns\gamma_0)+\cdots+(\lambda_k\ns\gamma_k)=\lambda_0+\cdots+\lambda_k .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Work from the right. If the suffix after position $i$ is already
$S_i=\lambda_{i+1}+\cdots+\lambda_k$, its leading monomial is $L_i$ (its
coefficient may exceed one). Every exponent of $\gamma_i$ is below that of $L_i$,
and ordinary addition of $S_i$ erases all terms of smaller exponent from the
preceding summand; if $\lambda_i$ has smaller exponent it is erased too, and
otherwise inserting $\gamma_i$ does not affect its surviving terms. So
$(\lambda_i\ns\gamma_i)+S_i=\lambda_i+S_i$; the last step uses $\gamma_k=0$.
\end{proof}

The lemma lets a persistent large coordinate contribute a bounded amount before
it is finally used at full scale; it does not let that coordinate's full pure
power be added at every earlier stage. The strict inequality $\gamma_i<L_i$ is
the substantive point. It plays the role of Part~II's strict-margin
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:absorb}, applied to a chain after the fact rather than inside
a rank map.

\section{Proof of the pure-height substitution theorem}\label{nh:wpo:sec:proof}

\begin{lemma}[Refinement inequalities; 09 Lemma~6.1]\label{nh:wpo:lem:refinement}
For ideals $I\subseteq J\subseteq K$,
$\Wz(I,K)\le\Wz(I,J)+\Wz(J,K)$, and for a nonempty ideal $I$,
$\Tz(I)\le\Wz(I,Q)+\Tz(Q)$. Consequently the value of any finite strictly
increasing path of nonempty support ideals, with terminal weight at its last
ideal, is at most the value of some saturated ideal path from $\varnothing$ to
$Q$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $q\in\max I\setminus\max K$, then either $q\notin\max J$, contributing to
$\Wz(I,J)$, or $q\in\max J\setminus\max K$, contributing to $\Wz(J,K)$; an
ordinary sum is at least its larger summand. A vertex witnessing $\Tz(I)$ either
stays active at $Q$ or lies in $\max I\setminus\max Q$. Prepending $\varnothing$
adds a zero weight, the last ideal extends to $Q$ by the second inequality, and
intermediate ideals are inserted one vertex at a time by the first, using
monotonicity of ordinary addition inside a finite sum. A saturated path is a
linear extension.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Upper bound: an arbitrary chain}

Let $\mathbf C\subseteq\K(S)$ be a chain. If it has no nonempty member, its type
is at most $1$, below the infinite bound. Otherwise its nonempty supports form a
finite chain $I_0\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq I_k$; let $\mathbf C_i$ be the
nonempty convex segment with support $I_i$, and for $i<k$ put
\[
 R_i=\max I_i\setminus\max I_{i+1},\qquad B_i=\max I_i\cap\max I_{i+1},\qquad
 \lambda_i=\max_{q\in R_i}h_q,
\]
with $\lambda_k=\Tz(I_k)$; $R_i$ and $B_i$ partition $\max I_i$. Choose a member
$E^{(i+1)}$ of $\mathbf C_{i+1}$. By \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:comparison}, for $p\in B_i$
its coordinate $E^{(i+1)}_p$ bounds \emph{every} $p$-coordinate in $\mathbf C_i$.
Put $\beta_{i,p}=r_{\K(P_p)}(E^{(i+1)}_p)$ and
$\gamma_i=\bigns_{p\in B_i}(\beta_{i,p}+1)$, $\gamma_k=0$; as $h_p$ is a limit,
$\beta_{i,p}+1<h_p$. Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:productbound} gives
$\otp(\mathbf C_i)\le\lambda_i\ns\gamma_i$, and for $i=k$ all coordinates are
unrestricted.

Each persistent coordinate is charged in full later. A vertex $p\in B_i$ stays
active until it first stops being maximal in a later support and never becomes
active again; if it retires at a transition $j>i$ then $h_p\le\lambda_j$, and if
it never retires then $p\in\max I_k$ and $h_p\le\lambda_k$. So
$h_p\le L_i=\max_{j>i}\lambda_j$ for $p\in B_i$, every summand of $\gamma_i$ is
below the pure power $L_i$, and $\gamma_i<L_i$. By
Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:absorb},
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:chainupper}
 \otp(\mathbf C\setminus\{\varnothing\})=\sum_{i\le k}\otp(\mathbf C_i)
 \le\sum_{i\le k}(\lambda_i\ns\gamma_i)=\lambda_0+\cdots+\lambda_k .
\end{equation}
The right side is a nonzero finite sum of positive infinite monomials (possibly
with zero terms), so adding one on the left leaves it unchanged, and including
$\varnothing$ does not enlarge the bound. By Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:refinement} it
is at most $\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)$. Finally $\K(S)$ is a WPO and
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:wolk} converts the bound on chains into
$\hgt(\K(S))\le\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)$.

\subsection{Lower bound: spend a coordinate just before it retires}

Fix $\pi$ with support ideals $I_j$ and choose $\mathbf C_q$, $b_q$ as in
Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:componentchains}. At a step $I_{j-1}\subsetneq I_j$ of
nonzero weight choose $r_j\in\max I_{j-1}\setminus\max I_j$ with
$h_{r_j}=\Wz(I_{j-1},I_j)$; at support $I_{j-1}$ vary the $r_j$-coordinate through
$\mathbf C_{r_j}$ and hold every other active coordinate at its base $b_q$. This
block has type $\Wz(I_{j-1},I_j)$; skip zero-weight steps. At support $Q$ choose
$r_*\in\max Q$ with $h_{r_*}=\Tz(Q)$ and vary it through $\mathbf C_{r_*}$ with
the others at their bases. The blocks concatenate: a coordinate varied in an
earlier block is completed at the next step and fully included later, and a
coordinate active in an earlier and a later block had not yet been selected in
the earlier one, so its earlier value is its base, below its later value in its
chain. So \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:comparison} holds across every pair of blocks, strictly
since the supports differ. The component chains need not be cofinal, since a
strictly higher fibre dominates a whole lower fibre. The resulting chain has type
\eqref{nh:wpo:eq:pathvalue}, and maximizing over the finitely many $\pi$ proves
the theorem.\hfill$\square$

\begin{remark}[Why this is not a reset argument; 09]
Only a coordinate about to be fully dominated is varied through its whole
component chain; a persistent coordinate stays at its base in the lower bound. In
the upper bound it may vary, but only below an actual later coordinate value.
These are two sides of one constraint.
\end{remark}

\section{Compression to inclusion-maximal antichains}\label{nh:wpo:sec:compression}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical completion of a support frontier; 09 Lemma~7.1]
\label{nh:wpo:lem:canonical}
For $I\in\J(Q)$ put $A(I)=\min(Q\setminus C(I))$. Then $A(I)\in\M(Q)$,
$\max I\subseteq A(I)$, and $C(I)=\{q:(\exists a\in A(I))\,q<a\}=D(A(I))$. If
$I\subseteq J$ then $A(I)\preceq A(J)$, and for a one-vertex enlargement
$J=I\cup\{v\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:canonicalretire}
 A(I)\setminus A(J)=\max I\setminus\max J .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$C(I)$ is a downset. Every strict predecessor of a vertex of $\max I$ lies in
$C(I)$, so $\max I\subseteq A(I)$. Each point of $C(I)$ lies below a member of
$\max I$, and each point outside $C(I)$ lies above a minimal point of the
complement, so every vertex is comparable with a member of the antichain $A(I)$.
Minimality gives one inclusion for $C(I)$, and the fact that completed vertices
lie strictly below $\max I$ the other. If $I\subseteq J$ then $C(I)\subseteq
C(J)$; a member $a$ of $A(I)$ either lies in $C(J)$, hence below a member of
$A(J)$, or has all strict predecessors in $C(I)\subseteq C(J)$ and so lies in
$A(J)$. For a one-vertex enlargement the new vertex is maximal in $J$, the newly
completed vertices are exactly $\max I\setminus\max J$, and a member of $A(I)$
leaves $A(J)$ exactly when it enters $C(J)$.
\end{proof}

By Remark~\ref{nh:rem:threenames}, $A(I)$ is also 07's completion
$\mcl(\max I)$ and Part~I's canonical frontier. With $w$ and $t$ as in
\eqref{nh:eq:frontierweights}, but built from the heights $h_q$:

\begin{theorem}[Maximal-frontier formula; 09 Thm~7.2]\label{nh:wpo:thm:compressed}
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:compressed}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)=
 \max_{\substack{A_0\prec\cdots\prec A_k\\ A_0=\min Q,\ A_k=\max Q}}
 \bigl(w(A_0,A_1)+\cdots+w(A_{k-1},A_k)+t(A_k)\bigr).}
\end{equation}
When $\min Q=\max Q$ the length-zero path is allowed. It suffices to maximize
over saturated chains of $\M(Q)$, though all comparable pairs are also correct.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Map a saturated support path to its canonical antichains $A(I_j)$. By
Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:canonical} they are nondecreasing from $\min Q$ to $\max Q$
and their transition weights equal the support weights; repeated antichains are
zero-weight steps and can be deleted. Conversely a strict chain of frontiers
gives the ideals $I_i={\downarrow}A_i$ with $\max I_i=A_i$; prepending
$\varnothing$ costs $0$, the last ideal is $Q$, and
Lemma~\ref{nh:wpo:lem:refinement} refines it to a saturated support path of at
least the same value. For $A\prec B\prec C$, $w(A,C)\le w(A,B)+w(B,C)$, so
inserting intermediate frontiers cannot decrease a value.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Uniform formula, independently recovered; 09 Cor.~7.3]
\label{nh:wpo:cor:uniform}
If every $h_q=\alpha=\omega^\rho$, $\rho>0$, then
$\hgt(\K(\lxsi_qP_q))=\alpha\cdot\hgt(\M(Q))$.
\end{corollary}

For chain fibres this is Theorem~\ref{thm:height}; for WPO fibres with a common
pure powerset height it is new relative to Part~I, whose height theorem assumes
ordinal fibres. No unrefereed repository theorem is imported.

\section{First-successor scheduling and exact finite algorithms}
\label{nh:wpo:sec:algorithms}

\subsection{A second interpretation of the weights}

For $\pi\in\LE(Q)$ with positions $\operatorname{pos}_\pi$, define the
retirement time $\tau_\pi(q)=\min\{\operatorname{pos}_\pi(r):q<r\}$ for
$q\notin\max Q$ and $\tau_\pi(q)=n+1$ for $q\in\max Q$. A vertex first stops being
maximal in the current support when its first strict successor is inserted, so
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:firstsuccessor}
 \Vz(\pi)=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\max\{h_q:\tau_\pi(q)=j\},
\end{equation}
chronologically, with empty groups contributing $0$; the final group is
$\max Q$. This needs no frontiers and is the independent linear-extension
calculation of 09's verification suite.

\subsection{The support-ideal and compressed programs}

Let $\mathsf d^0(\varnothing)=0$ and, for nonempty ideals $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:dp}
 \mathsf d^0(J)=\max_{v\in\max J}\Bigl[\mathsf d^0(J\setminus\{v\})+
 \Wz(J\setminus\{v\},J)\Bigr],\qquad \Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)=\mathsf d^0(Q)+\Tz(Q).
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Correctness of the recurrence; 09 Prop.~8.1]\label{nh:wpo:prop:dp}
$\mathsf d^0(J)$ is the greatest transition sum of a saturated ideal path from
$\varnothing$ to $J$, without terminal term, and recording a maximizing
predecessor reconstructs an extension attaining \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:dp}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The last vertex of a saturated path to $J$ lies in $\max J$, and deleting it
leaves a saturated path to $J\setminus\{v\}$; conversely every such path extends
by $v$. For a fixed edge weight $\eta$ the map $\xi\mapsto\xi+\eta$ is
nondecreasing, so a greatest predecessor value gives a greatest value after the
edge, even though distinct values may become equal. Induct on $|J|$.
\end{proof}

There are at most $2^n$ ideals with at most $n$ incoming edges each, so after
finite preprocessing the program uses $O(n2^n)$ ordinal additions and comparisons,
and $O(n^22^n)$ elementary set operations with naive frontier handling: an
exponential algorithm, not a polynomial-time claim. The compressed program sets
$\mathsf g(\min Q)=0$ and $\mathsf g(B)=\max_{A\prec B}(\mathsf g(A)+w(A,B))$ for
$B\ne\min Q$, with $\Phi=\mathsf g(\max Q)+t(\max Q)$; cover edges suffice, and
with $m=|\M(Q)|$ it uses $O(m^2)$ ordinal updates beyond finding the frontiers.
09's implementation deliberately uses all comparable pairs so that its
correctness does not depend on a transitive-reduction routine. In
pseudocode (09):
\begin{lstlisting}
Input: finite poset Q, positive pure weights h[q]
D[empty] := 0
for each nonempty ideal J, in increasing cardinality:
    D[J] := maximum over v in Max(J) of
            D[J - {v}] + max(h[q] : q in Max(J - {v}) - Max(J))
return D[Q] + max(h[q] : q in Max(Q))
# Empty maxima are 0. Every + above is ordinary ordinal addition.
# Empty Q is a separate case with answer 1.
\end{lstlisting} Neither program
infers component heights (the weights $h_q$ are inputs justified outside the
computation) or decides equality of arbitrary ordinal notations.

\begin{remark}[The two empty-exit conventions on one example]
\label{nh:wpo:rem:conventions}
For the persistent example ($x$ isolated, $y<z$, heights
$(\omega^2,\omega,\omega)$; 09's vertices $(x,a,b)=(0,1,2)$), 09's support
program gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
 \text{bitmask}&0&1&2&3&6&7\\ \hline
 I&\varnothing&\{x\}&\{y\}&\{x,y\}&\{y,z\}&\{x,y,z\}\\
 \mathsf d^0(I)&0&0&0&0&\omega&\omega
\end{array}
\]
and $\omega+\omega^2=\omega^2$; its compressed certificate needs the two states
$\{x,y\},\{x,z\}$ and one edge of weight $\omega$. Part~II's weight-$1$ program
gives $0,1,1,2,\omega,\omega+1$ on the same ideals
(Section~\ref{nh:sec:examples}) and the same final value. For positive pure
weights the answers always agree, because the terminal weight is infinite and
absorbs every earlier finite term; the tables, and therefore the certificates,
differ. The weight-$0$ convention is wrong as soon as a fibre is finite: a
singleton fibre has a two-element finitary powerset. 09 excludes such fibres, so
this is a scope boundary, not an error.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Finite certificates (09)}

A support certificate lists every ideal, its active set, its value
$\mathsf d^0(I)$ and a selected predecessor for each nonempty ideal. A checker
verifies $\mathsf d^0(\varnothing)=0$, $\mathsf d^0(I)+\Wz(I,J)\le\mathsf d^0(J)$
for every unit edge, and equality on each selected edge; these prove both finite
path bounds. It also validates a linear extension whose full value is the claimed
answer. The supplied checker does not call the optimizer but shares its exact
arithmetic and poset routines, so it is not an independent formal kernel; the
first-successor calculation tests the shared choices from another direction. A
certificate proves the \emph{finite optimization} relative to
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:main}; it formalizes neither that theorem, nor Wolk's
theorem, nor the input height claims.

\subsection{A bounded-width consequence (09)}

If $Q$ has width at most $b$, every ideal is generated by an antichain of size at
most $b$, so $|\J(Q)|\le\sum_{j=0}^b\binom nj$, and enumerating those antichains
and their downclosures gives a finite-state algorithm in time $n^{O(b)}$,
transition generation included. This is an XP bound in $b$, not an FPT bound. The
default program enumerates subsets directly; the bound describes a proved
algorithmic variant, not a performance claim about that implementation.

\section{Substitution invariance and limit-ordinal fibres}\label{nh:wpo:sec:extension}

\begin{corollary}[Pure-height congruence; 09 Cor.~9.1]\label{nh:wpo:cor:congruence}
If $(P_q)$ and $(P'_q)$ are families of WPOs with
$\hgt(\K(P_q))=\hgt(\K(P'_q))=\omega^{\rho_q}$, $\rho_q>0$, then
$\hgt(\K(\lxsi_qP_q))=\hgt(\K(\lxsi_qP'_q))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Both sides are the same finite optimization in the common weights.
\end{proof}

This is a genuine loss of dependence on the internal structure of a fibre, but
only once its own finitary-powerset height is supplied. Replacing that input by
$\hgt(P_q)$, by its maximal linear-extension type, or by its width is not
justified; and knowing $\hgt(\K(P_q))$ does not give $\hgt(\K(\K(P_q)))$:
unrestricted iteration is a different problem.

\begin{theorem}[All nonzero limit-ordinal fibres; 09 Thm~9.2]\label{nh:wpo:thm:limits}
For nonzero limit ordinals $\beta_q$, expanded as in \eqref{nh:eq:cnf} (all
exponents positive) into $Q^\sharp$ with pure weights $\alpha^\sharp_v$,
$\hgt(\K(\lxsi_q\beta_q))=\Phi(Q^\sharp;(\alpha^\sharp_v)_v)$. Finite ordinal
normal forms and finite ordinal comparisons determine the answer exactly; the
algorithm expands coefficients literally, and its size is measured in expanded
blocks.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{nh:lem:refine} and Corollary~\ref{nh:wpo:cor:ordinal}.
\end{proof}

No recursive expansion of an exponent's own normal form is needed: a block
$\omega^{\varepsilon_0}$ is one pure block with exponent label $\varepsilon_0$. A
zero fibre is deleted and the induced skeleton used; the all-empty case is
separate, $\K(\varnothing)$ being a singleton of height $1$. Successor fibres
have finite terminal terms and are not covered by this theorem; for chain fibres
they are covered by Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:arbitrary} of Part~II. This agrees with
Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:limits}.

\section{Exponent patterns, bounds and lost information}\label{nh:wpo:sec:invariants}

\begin{theorem}[Exponent-order invariance; 09 Thm~10.1]\label{nh:wpo:thm:chambers}
Fix $Q$ and two families of positive exponents $(\rho_q)$, $(\sigma_q)$ with the
same equality and strict-order pattern. Replacing each $\rho$ by the corresponding
$\sigma$ preserves the Cantor-normal-form coefficients of every path value, every
program value and the final height, and preserves exactly the set of maximizing
linear extensions and the set of maximizing frontier chains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each transition takes the largest exponent in a finite subset; a path adds the
selected monomials in order, which deletes strictly smaller trailing exponents and
merges equal ones; comparing two results depends only on ordered exponents and
finite coefficients. All of this commutes with a strictly increasing relabelling
of the finite exponent set, for paths, maxima and both recurrences.
\end{proof}

The parameter space thus splits into finitely many weak-order patterns
(``chambers''), each completely represented by integer priorities. A priority is
not automatically an ordinal exponent: 09's optional symbolic labels may denote
arbitrary positive exponents in a declared order, and checking that order is
outside the program's scope. This theorem adds the preservation of maximizer sets
to Corollaries~\ref{nh:cor:scale} and~\ref{nh:cor:parametric}.

\begin{proposition}[Support and size of the answer; 09 Prop.~10.2]\label{nh:wpo:prop:budget}
Every exponent of $\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)$ occurs among the $\rho_q$; its leading
exponent is $\max_q\rho_q$; its coefficient sum is at most $n=|Q|$; it has no
finite tail; and $\max_qh_q\le\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)\le\bigns_qh_q$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The retirement groups of \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:firstsuccessor}, terminal group
included, partition $Q$. One maximizer per nonempty group gives at most $n$
distinct monomials whose ordinary sum is the path value; normalization neither
introduces exponents nor raises the coefficient count, and the value is at most
their natural sum. A vertex of globally largest weight lies in one group, whose
selected weight is that maximum, and no later summand can erase it; so every
schedule has the stated leading exponent and at least the largest input monomial.
\end{proof}

Even for enormous input exponents the answer is thus a finite coefficient vector
on the input exponent labels.

\subsection{Unbounded ambiguity of local stratum heights}\label{nh:wpo:sec:ambiguity}

For a frontier $A$ the unrestricted product of active coordinates has height
$t(A)=\max_{q\in A}h_q$ (natural-sum rank bound above; vary a largest coordinate
through its attaining chain below), and so has the fixed-support stratum at
${\downarrow}A$. Thus $t(A)$ is exactly the local height label one might keep
from a stratification.

\begin{theorem}[Local labels do not determine the height; 09 Thm~10.3]
\label{nh:wpo:thm:ambiguity}
For every $m\ge1$ and pure powers $\beta<\alpha$ with positive exponents there is
one finite skeleton $Q_m$ with $m$ weight assignments for which the labelled
frontier poset $(\M(Q_m),t)$ is identical while the global heights are the
distinct values $\alpha,\alpha\cdot2,\dots,\alpha\cdot m$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take an isolated $x$ of weight $\alpha$ beside a chain $a_1<\cdots<a_m$ whose
vertices get weight $\alpha$ or $\beta$. The frontiers are exactly
$A_i=\{x,a_i\}$, an $m$-element chain, and every $t(A_i)=\alpha$. For
$j\in\{1,\dots,m\}$ give exactly $j-1$ of $a_1,\dots,a_{m-1}$ weight $\alpha$ and
the other chain vertices weight $\beta$. The compressed formula has successive
weights $h_{a_1},\dots,h_{a_{m-1}}$ and terminal weight $\alpha$; each smaller
term is absorbed by a later $\alpha$, and the sum is $\alpha\cdot j$.
\end{proof}

The deficiency of a stratum-only formula is therefore unbounded in the number of
stages: even the whole frontier poset with every local height label is
insufficient, and the identities of the coordinates leaving between frontiers
carry indispensable information. This strengthens Part~I's single counterexample
and Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:strata}.

\section{Worked examples and a height-two reduction}\label{nh:wpo:sec:examples}

\subsection{Chains, antichains and the persistent coordinate}

If $Q$ is a chain $q_1<\cdots<q_n$, its frontiers are its singletons and
$\Phi=h_{q_1}+\cdots+h_{q_n}$. If $Q$ is an antichain, $\M(Q)=\{Q\}$ and
$\Phi=\max_qh_q$. This does not contradict the natural-sum bound for product
ranks: the supremum of natural sums of finitely many coordinates strictly below
pure limits is the largest such limit, not their natural sum. For the persistent
skeleton $\{x,\,y<z\}$ the frontiers are $\{x,y\}\prec\{x,z\}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:persistent}
 \Phi=h_y+\max(h_x,h_z);
\end{equation}
$(h_x,h_y,h_z)=(\omega^2,\omega,\omega)$ gives $\omega+\omega^2=\omega^2$, and
changing only $h_y$ to $\omega^2$ gives $\omega^2\cdot2$, although both frontier
labels remain $\omega^2$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.98,every node/.style={font=\small},
 point/.style={circle,fill=Olive,inner sep=1.7pt}]
 \begin{scope}[xshift=0cm]
  \node[point,label=below:$x$] (x) at (0,0.7) {};
  \node[point,label=below:$y$] (y) at (1.2,0) {};
  \node[point,label=above:$z$] (z) at (1.2,1.4) {};
  \draw[Olive] (y)--(z);
  \node[align=center] at (0.6,-0.7) {Persistent coordinate};
 \end{scope}
 \begin{scope}[xshift=4cm]
  \node[point,label=below:$a$] (a) at (0,0) {};
  \node[point,label=below:$b$] (b) at (1.5,0) {};
  \node[point,label=above:$c$] (c) at (0,1.4) {};
  \node[point,label=above:$d$] (d) at (1.5,1.4) {};
  \draw[Olive] (a)--(c) (b)--(c) (b)--(d);
  \node at (0.75,-0.7) {$N$-skeleton};
 \end{scope}
 \begin{scope}[xshift=8.3cm]
  \node[point,label=below:$a$] (a) at (0,0) {};
  \node[point,label=below:$b$] (b) at (1.5,0) {};
  \node[point,label=above:$c$] (c) at (0,1.4) {};
  \node[point,label=above:$d$] (d) at (1.5,1.4) {};
  \draw[Olive] (a)--(c) (b)--(d);
  \node at (0.75,-0.7) {Two parallel chains};
 \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The skeletons of 09's examples, in this report's labelling (09 draws
the $N$-skeleton as $a<c$, $a<d$, $b<d$). The persistent coordinate contributes
fully only at the terminal stage; the parallel chains permit different
retirement orders.}
\label{nh:wpo:fig:examples}
\end{figure}

\subsection{A genuinely non-series-parallel skeleton}

The $N$-skeleton $a<c$, $b<c$, $b<d$ has connected comparability and
incomparability graphs, so it is neither a disjoint union nor an ordinal sum and
is not generated from singletons by those operations. Its frontiers form the
chain \eqref{eq:Nfrontiers}, and $\Phi=h_b+h_a+\max(h_c,h_d)$. For
$(h_a,h_b,h_c,h_d)=(\omega^2,\omega^3,\omega,\omega)$ this is
$\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$ (09's weights $(\omega^3,\omega^2,\omega,\omega)$ in
its labelling). A path jumping from the first frontier to the last would charge
only $\max(h_a,h_b)$ before the terminal term; refining a transition can keep an
additional lower-order term, so grouping matters even when the leading exponent
is fixed.

\subsection{Optimization depends on the exponent pattern}

For two disjoint chains $a<c$ and $b<d$, either $a$ or $b$ may retire first. With
$t_{cd}=\max(h_c,h_d)$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:parallel}
 \Phi=\max(h_a+h_b+t_{cd},\ h_b+h_a+t_{cd})=(h_a\ns h_b)+t_{cd},
\end{equation}
because putting the larger of two monomials first produces their natural sum. For
$(\omega^2,\omega^3,\omega,\omega)$ the result is $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$;
retiring $a$ before $b$ would lose the $\omega^2$, and swapping the source
weights swaps the preferred order. The limit fibres $\beta_a=\omega^2+\omega$ and
$\beta_b=\omega\cdot2$ on a two-point antichain expand to exactly this pair of
two-step chains with block weights $(\omega^2,\omega)$ and $(\omega,\omega)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:limitworked}
 \hgt\bigl(\K((\omega^2+\omega)\sqcup(\omega\cdot2))\bigr)=\omega^2+\omega\cdot2 ;
\end{equation}
both $\max(\beta_a,\beta_b)=\omega^2+\omega$ and
$\beta_a\ns\beta_b=\omega^2+\omega\cdot3$ miss it. Purity matters in the simple
antichain formula, and block expansion handles this nonpure limit case exactly.

\subsection{Height-two skeletons as first-neighbour schedules}

(09 writes $L$, $U$, $Z$, $N(\ell)$ and $G_i(\sigma)$ for the sets and
groups renamed here, to avoid the label set $U$ and the terminal region $Z$
of Part~II and the $N$-poset.)

Let $Q$ be the disjoint union of $Q_{\mathrm{lo}}$, $Q_{\mathrm{up}}$ and $Q_{\mathrm{iso}}$, every strict relation having
the form $\ell<u$ with $\ell\in Q_{\mathrm{lo}}$, $u\in Q_{\mathrm{up}}$; every point of $Q_{\mathrm{lo}}$ has an upper
neighbour, every point of $Q_{\mathrm{up}}$ a lower neighbour, and $Q_{\mathrm{iso}}$ is the set of isolated
vertices. Write $\mathcal N(\ell)\subseteq Q_{\mathrm{up}}$ for the nonempty upper-neighbour
set of $\ell$; all of $Q_{\mathrm{up}}\cup Q_{\mathrm{iso}}$ is maximal.

\begin{theorem}[Height-two scheduling formula; 09 Thm~11.1]\label{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo}
For a permutation $\sigma=(u_1,\dots,u_s)$ of $Q_{\mathrm{up}}$ let
$\Gamma_i(\sigma)=\{\ell\in Q_{\mathrm{lo}}:u_i\in\mathcal N(\ell),\ u_j\notin\mathcal N(\ell)
\text{ for }j<i\}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:wpo:eq:heighttwo}
 \Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)=\max_\sigma\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^s\max_{\ell\in\Gamma_i(\sigma)}h_\ell
 +\max_{q\in Q_{\mathrm{up}}\cup Q_{\mathrm{iso}}}h_q\Bigr],
\end{equation}
the case $Q_{\mathrm{up}}=\varnothing$ being the antichain case.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Restrict a linear extension to $Q_{\mathrm{up}}$. A lower vertex retires at its first upper
neighbour in this order, so its retirement group is the corresponding
$\Gamma_i(\sigma)$, and inserting lower or isolated vertices retires nothing already
active. Every permutation of $Q_{\mathrm{up}}$ is realized by inserting all of $Q_{\mathrm{lo}}\cup Q_{\mathrm{iso}}$ first.
So \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:firstsuccessor} becomes \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:heighttwo} with no lost
schedules.
\end{proof}

For example take $Q_{\mathrm{lo}}=\{0,1,2\}$, $Q_{\mathrm{up}}=\{3,4,5\}$,
$\mathcal N(0)=\{3,4\}$, $\mathcal N(1)=\{4,5\}$, $\mathcal N(2)=\{3,5\}$, source
weights $(\omega^3,\omega^2,\omega)$ and upper weights $\omega$. The order
$(3,5,4)$ retires $\{0,2\}$, then $\{1\}$, then nothing new, giving
$\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$ with the terminal term, and the shipped certificate
\path{data/03-wpo-height_two_overlap_certificate.json} establishes
optimality. This is a concrete finite set-system scheduling problem; its
computational complexity is not classified here.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch 42: Part~IV classifies it. With all weights
equal, the largest number of nonempty groups is the maximum uniquely
restricted matching number of the incidence graph, and deciding the height
threshold is NP-complete already when every fibre is $\omega$
(Theorems~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive}, \ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform}
and~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np}); for unequal weights \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:heighttwo} is
an ordered-matching maximum (Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:weighted}).]

\section{Implementation, verification and foundations of Part~III}
\label{nh:wpo:sec:verification}

09's module \path{code/03-wpo-frontier_heights.py} contains exact finite
Cantor-normal-form arithmetic, transitive closure and cycle rejection, both
programs, limit-fibre expansion and a certificate checker, with the standard
library only and no floating point; answers are decreasing
priority--coefficient lists. It takes pure weights or limit-fibre normal forms;
the persistent input is \texttt{\{"n": 3, "edges": [[1, 2]], "priorities": [2, 1, 1]\}},
vertices numbered from $0$ and edges any acyclic generating relation. Without
labels priorities are read as finite exponents; an optional dictionary such as
\texttt{\{"1": "omega", "2": "omega+1", "3": "epsilon\_0"\}} labels increasing
positive exponents, and the program does not prove that the strings denote
ordinals in the declared order; the justification for relabelling is
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:chambers}. An input-size guard rejects more than $22$
vertices, expanded blocks included: a practical guard for an exponential
implementation, neither a restriction of the theorem nor a promise that every
$22$-vertex instance is cheap. In the delivered layout the commands are
\texttt{python code/verify.py} and \texttt{python code/frontier\_heights.py}
applied to \texttt{examples/nonuniform\_N.json} (with \texttt{--output} and
\texttt{--check}) and \texttt{examples/limit\_fibers.json}; the verifier
overwrites only its own results and generated examples and certificates.

The recorded run (Python 3.13.5, seed 24092026) enumerated, for each $n\le6$,
every transitive subrelation of the natural strict order on $\{0,\dots,n-1\}$:
$1,1,2,7,40,357,4824$ posets for $n=0,\dots,6$, $5{,}232$ in all and $5{,}231$
nonempty, covering every isomorphism type but not an unlabelled count. Uniform
checks cover the census; weighted checks use all three-priority assignments for
$n\le4$, all assignments from $\{1,3\}$ for $n=5$, and one seeded four-priority
assignment for each of the first $256$ six-vertex posets; every weighted instance
enumerates \emph{all} its linear extensions and values them by the first-successor
rule.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Check or measured quantity (09) & Count\\
\midrule
Weighted instances & 15,131\\
Independently valued linear extensions & 249,057\\
Uniform finite certificates & 5,232\\
Weighted finite certificates & 15,131\\
Weighted program versus all-extension maximum & 15,131\\
Weighted support/compressed program agreement & 15,131\\
Uniform maximal-antichain height identities & 5,231\\

\caption{Selected categories of 09's ledger, shipped as
\texttt{data/03-wpo-verification.json}. The total also includes witness values,
