\subsection{A placement-time consistency check}\label{nh:ur:sec:crosscheck}

Before this part was written, the uniform objective of
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo} (the largest number of nonempty
first-neighbour groups over all orders of the upper vertices) was compared
by brute force with the maximum uniquely restricted matching number, computed
directly from the definition (a matching whose saturated vertices induce a
subgraph with exactly one perfect matching). All $4{,}056$ bipartite
incidence matrices with one to three lower rows and one to four upper
vertices, every lower row nonempty, were enumerated: the two values agreed in
every case, with $0$ mismatches. This is Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive}
on those instances, computed independently of 04's programs. The check was
run during placement; its script is not shipped.

\section{Ordinals, fibres and first-neighbour schedules}\label{nh:ur:sec:setup}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\subsection{Height and finite generation}
For a well-founded partially ordered set $P$ put
\[
 \rk_P(x)=\sup\{\rk_P(y)+1:y<x\},\qquad
 \hgt(P)=\sup_{x\in P}(\rk_P(x)+1),
\]
as in Section~\ref{sec:tools}. In particular, the empty order has height $0$
and a one-element order has height $1$. $\K(P)$ is the set of finitely
generated downsets of $P$, including $\varnothing$, ordered by inclusion;
thus $\K(\varnothing)$ is a singleton. The following elementary facts are
used repeatedly.

\begin{lemma}[04]\label{nh:ur:lem:ordinalfacts}
Let $\alpha=\omega^\rho$ with $\rho>0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item A natural sum of finitely many ordinals less than $\alpha$ is less than
$\alpha$ and is strictly increasing in each coordinate.
\item Ordinary addition is weakly increasing in each argument.
\item A finite sum of monomials with exponents strictly below $\rho$ is less
than $\omega^\rho$ and is absorbed when $\omega^\rho$ is added on its right.
\item Every final segment of $\alpha$ starting below $\alpha$ has order type
$\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Cantor normal form proves the first and third assertions: a finite collection of
exponents below $\rho$ has a largest member below $\rho$. The second follows
from the recursive definition of ordinal addition. For the fourth, if
$\alpha=\beta+\gamma$ with $\beta<\alpha$, then $\gamma\le\alpha$; if also
$\gamma<\alpha$, additive indecomposability would imply
$\beta+\gamma<\alpha$. Hence $\gamma=\alpha$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Height-at-most-two skeletons}
Let $Q$ be a finite nonempty poset with no chain of three distinct elements.
There is a canonical decomposition
\[
 Q=\Qlo\sqcup\Qup\sqcup\Qiso,
\]
where $\Qlo$ consists of nonmaximal minimal elements, $\Qup$ of nonminimal
maximal elements, and $\Qiso$ of isolated elements. Every strict relation has
the form $\ell<u$ with $\ell\in\Qlo$ and $u\in\Qup$, and both endpoints of
every edge have a neighbour. Set
\[
 G_Q=(\Qlo,\Qup,E),\qquad \ell u\in E\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ell<u,
 \qquad \Nb(\ell)=\{u\in\Qup:\ell u\in E\}.
\]
$\Nb$ also denotes the usual neighbourhood of a set on the other side: for
$X\subseteq\Qup$, $\Nb(X)=\bigcup_{u\in X}\Nb(u)\subseteq\Qlo$. For a general
finite bipartite graph $G$ the two sides are still called lower rows and
upper columns, and the same letters are used.

For ordinals $(\alpha_q)_{q\in Q}$ the lexicographic sum
$\lxsi_{q\in Q}\alpha_q$ has elements $(q,\beta)$ with $\beta<\alpha_q$.
Elements in one fibre are ordered by their ordinal coordinates; elements in
different fibres are ordered exactly when their indices are ordered in $Q$.
No relation is added between incomparable fibres.

\subsection{The finite weighted objective}
Give each lower vertex a positive ordinal exponent $\rho_\ell$ and give a
positive terminal exponent $\rtop$. For a permutation
$\sigma=(u_1,\ldots,u_s)$ of $\Qup$ define the retirement groups
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:groups}
 \Gamma_i(\sigma)=
 \{\ell\in\Qlo:u_i\in\Nb(\ell),\ \Nb(\ell)\cap\{u_1,\ldots,u_{i-1}\}=\varnothing\},
\end{equation}
exactly as in Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo}. The groups are disjoint and
their union is $\Qlo$. An index is \emph{productive} when its group is
nonempty. Set
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:objective}
 b_i(\sigma)=\max_{\ell\in\Gamma_i(\sigma)}\omega^{\rho_\ell},\qquad
 \Psi(G,\boldsymbol\rho;\rtop)=
 \max_\sigma\left(b_1(\sigma)+\cdots+b_s(\sigma)+\omega^{\rtop}\right),
\end{equation}
with an empty maximum equal to $0$ (the convention of Part~III). The empty
permutation has value $\omega^{\rtop}$. Columns with no neighbours are allowed
for this finite objective; they simply contribute $0$.

The terminal term is a permanent boundary condition, not an extra lower
vertex. When passing to a residual graph later, it must be retained
unchanged, including when the residual graph has no vertices.

\begin{remark}[Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo} in this notation]
\label{nh:ur:rem:import}
For a nonempty height-at-most-two skeleton $Q$ let
$\alpha_q=\omega^{\rho_q}$ with $\rho_q>0$ for every $q\in Q$, and put
$\rtop=\max\{\rho_q:q\in\Qup\cup\Qiso\}$. Since $\hgt(\K(\omega^{\rho}))=\omega^\rho$
for $\rho>0$, these fibres satisfy the hypotheses of Part~III with
$h_q=\alpha_q$, and Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo} states
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:import}
 \hgt\Bigl(\K\Bigl(\lxsi_{q\in Q}\alpha_q\Bigr)\Bigr)
 =\Phi(Q;\boldsymbol h)=\Psi\bigl(G_Q,(\rho_\ell)_{\ell\in\Qlo};\rtop\bigr).
\end{equation}
This is the precise inherited result that 04 uses for nonuniform transfinite
heights. It is an unformalized and unrefereed theorem of this report. The
finite results about $\Psi$ in this part are independent of it. The uniform
instance of \eqref{nh:ur:eq:import}, and hence the ordinal-height
NP-completeness result, is proved directly in
Section~\ref{nh:ur:sec:uniform}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Encoding versus mathematics]\label{nh:ur:rem:encoding}
The mathematical results allow arbitrary set-sized positive ordinal
exponents. The executable input uses positive integers in binary. More
generally a finite list of exponent labels with a supplied correct total
order suffices for the finite optimization. No algorithm deciding the order
of arbitrary ordinal descriptions is assumed. The empty skeleton is excluded
from \eqref{nh:ur:eq:import}; its powerset height is $1$, not an empty
maximum.
\end{remark}

\section{Triangular matchings and productive schedules}\label{nh:ur:sec:matching}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\begin{definition}\label{nh:ur:def:ur}
A matching $M$ in a finite graph is \emph{uniquely restricted} if the
subgraph induced by its saturated vertices has $M$ as its unique perfect
matching. Write $\nuur(G)$ for the largest cardinality of such a matching,
the empty matching allowed. In a bipartite graph, an ordered matching
$((\ell_1,u_1),\ldots,(\ell_k,u_k))$ is \emph{admissible} or
\emph{triangular} if
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:triangular}
 \ell_i u_j\notin E\quad\text{whenever }j<i.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}
An ordinary maximum matching need not be uniquely restricted. The distinction
is precisely the possibility of switching along an alternating cycle.

\begin{lemma}[Classical characterization, with proof; 04]\label{nh:ur:lem:ur}
For a bipartite matching $M$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item $M$ is uniquely restricted;
\item $G$ contains no $M$-alternating cycle;
\item $M$ admits a triangular ordering.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An alternating cycle permits exchanging its matched and unmatched edges,
producing another perfect matching on the same saturated vertices. Conversely,
the symmetric difference of two different perfect matchings on those vertices
is a disjoint union of alternating cycles. This proves (i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii).

Make a directed graph whose vertices are the matched edges
$e_i=(\ell_i,u_i)$, putting $e_i\to e_j$ for $i\ne j$ when
$\ell_i u_j\in E$. A directed cycle gives an alternating cycle by inserting
the matching edges between its cross-edges. Every alternating cycle gives a
directed cycle in this way, after choosing its orientation. Thus (ii) is
equivalent to acyclicity of this directed graph. A topological ordering has no
cross-edge from a later row to an earlier column, which is exactly
\eqref{nh:ur:eq:triangular}. Conversely such an ordering forbids a directed
cycle.
\end{proof}
The characterization is part of the classical theory~\cite{GHL2001}; the
proof is included to fix the orientation used in all subsequent
constructions.

\begin{theorem}[Productive schedules are uniquely restricted matchings; 04]
\label{nh:ur:thm:productive}
The maximum number of productive indices in a permutation of $\Qup$ equals
$\nuur(G)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Given a schedule, keep its productive columns in their original order. From each
corresponding group choose one lower vertex. Distinct groups give distinct lower
vertices, and freshness says that a chosen row is adjacent to none of the
earlier chosen columns. The resulting matching is triangular. Thus every
schedule has at most $\nuur(G)$ productive indices.

Conversely, order a uniquely restricted matching triangularly, schedule its
upper endpoints first in that order, and then schedule the remaining upper
vertices arbitrarily. Each matched row is fresh when its own upper endpoint is
scheduled. Hence the schedule has at least $|M|$ productive indices. Maximize
over $M$ and use the first inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coverage by a maximum matching's upper endpoints; 04]
\label{nh:ur:lem:coverage}
Suppose every lower vertex has a neighbour. If $M$ is a maximum uniquely
restricted matching and $X$ is its set of upper endpoints, then
$\Nb(X)=\Qlo$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $\ell\notin\Nb(X)$, choose $u\in\Nb(\ell)$. Necessarily $u\notin X$, and
$\ell$ is not a matched row. Append $(\ell,u)$ to a triangular ordering of
$M$. The new row is adjacent to no previous matched column, so this is a larger
triangular matching, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The established set-system interpretation}\label{nh:ur:sec:grundy}
Consider the family of subsets $(\Nb(u))_{u\in\Qup}$ of $\Qlo$. A legal
covering sequence chooses sets, each of which introduces a new element, until
their union is $\Qlo$. Its maximum possible length is the Grundy covering
number in the terminology of~\cite{BHR2016}. Removing nonproductive steps
from a full permutation leaves such a sequence; a legal sequence can be
extended until it covers all of $\Qlo$. Thus
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive} identifies this covering number with
$\nuur(G)$. This answers the request of the complexity item of
Section~\ref{nh:sec:questions} (09's ``largest number of nonempty retirement
groups'') for a connection with an established finite optimization problem,
rather than introducing a new name for an old unweighted parameter.

\section{Closure systems and maximal frontiers}\label{nh:ur:sec:closure}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

The matching identification also explains the finite frontier height in
Part~I's uniform formula (Theorem~\ref{thm:height}). The correspondence is
explicit. For $X\subseteq\Qup$ put
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:closure}
 \ncl(X)=\{u\in\Qup:\Nb(u)\subseteq\Nb(X)\}.
\end{equation}
This is a closure operator: it is extensive and monotone, and
$\Nb(\ncl(X))=\Nb(X)$ proves idempotence. In this section $G=G_Q$ has no
isolated upper vertices, so $\ncl(\varnothing)=\varnothing$.

\begin{theorem}[04]\label{nh:ur:thm:frontiers}
The closed subsets of $\Qup$, ordered by inclusion, are isomorphic to the
inclusion-maximal antichains of $Q$, ordered by Hoare domination. The
isomorphism is
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:frontier}
 X\longmapsto A_X=(\Qlo\setminus\Nb(X))\cup X\cup\Qiso.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for the frontier poset $\M(Q)$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:frontierheight}
 \hgt(\M(Q))=\nuur(G_Q)+1.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The set in \eqref{nh:ur:eq:frontier} is an antichain. An omitted lower vertex
is comparable with a member of $X$. An omitted upper vertex can be added
precisely when all its neighbours are in $\Nb(X)$, that is, when it belongs to
$\ncl(X)$. Thus $A_X$ is maximal exactly when $X$ is closed. Conversely, a
maximal antichain with upper part $X$ must contain every isolated vertex and
every lower vertex outside $\Nb(X)$, and no other lower vertex; the same
observation forces closure.

Recall that $A\preceq B$ in the Hoare order means that every element of $A$
is below or equal to some element of $B$. If $A_X\preceq A_{X'}$, every
maximal upper vertex of $X$ must itself belong to $X'$, so $X\subseteq X'$.
If $X\subseteq X'$, a lower vertex in $A_X$ either remains in $A_{X'}$ or
acquires a neighbour in $X'$; all upper and isolated elements of $A_X$
remain. This proves the order isomorphism.

A productive sequence of length $k$ gives $k+1$ strictly increasing closed sets
by taking the closures of its successive prefixes, including the empty prefix.
Freshness makes the neighbourhood unions strictly increase. For the converse,
extend a longest chain of closed sets to include bottom and top, and write it
as $X_0\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq X_k$. For each $i\ge1$ choose
$u_i\in X_i\setminus X_{i-1}$. Since $X_{i-1}$ is closed, $u_i$ has a
neighbour outside $\Nb(X_{i-1})$, hence outside the union of the
neighbourhoods of the previously chosen columns. The sequence is productive.
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive} now gives
\eqref{nh:ur:eq:frontierheight}; for a finite poset the rank height is the
largest number of elements in a chain.
\end{proof}

The result is compatible with Part~I's uniform identity
$\hgt(\K(S_\alpha(Q)))=\alpha\cdot\hgt(\M(Q))$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:height}).
For the $N$-poset, a path on four vertices, $\nuur=2$ and
\eqref{nh:ur:eq:frontierheight} recovers the three-element frontier chain
\eqref{eq:Nfrontiers}. The next section does not use Part~I's identity: it
supplies a separate proof from the actual downsets.

\begin{remark}[A second route through Part~I; merge]\label{nh:ur:rem:secondroute}
Theorem~\ref{thm:height} and \eqref{nh:ur:eq:frontierheight} together give
the formula of Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform} at once. 04 deliberately does
not take this route, so that its uniform theorem, and with it the
NP-completeness of Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np}, depends on no unrefereed
theorem of this report. The two routes are independent proofs of the same
statement; the observation that they combine is the merge's own.
\end{remark}

\section{A direct proof of the uniform height}\label{nh:ur:sec:uniform}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform height, independently proved; 04]\label{nh:ur:thm:uniform}
Let $Q$ be finite, nonempty and of height at most two. For every
$\alpha=\omega^\rho$, $\rho>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:uniform}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_\alpha(Q))\bigr)
   =\alpha\cdot\bigl(\nuur(G_Q)+1\bigr).}
\end{equation}
The multiplication on the right is ordinary multiplication by a positive
finite ordinal on the right.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Profiles of finitely generated downsets}
For $D\in\K(S_\alpha(Q))$ let
\[
 X(D)=\{u\in\Qup:D\text{ meets the }u\text{-fibre}\}.
\]
Every lower fibre in $\Nb(X(D))$ is wholly included in $D$. Every other
lower fibre, every isolated fibre and every upper fibre is either empty or a
principal initial segment, because a finite collection of generators inside
an ordinal chain has a largest member. No upper or isolated fibre can be
wholly included: $\alpha$ is a limit ordinal and has no finite cofinal
subset. A lower fibre not below $X(D)$ cannot be wholly included either.

The active coordinates are therefore
\[
 A(X)=(\Qlo\setminus\Nb(X))\cup X\cup\Qiso .
\]
For $q\in A(X(D))$ let $\theta_q(D)<\alpha$ be the order type of the initial
segment of its fibre present in $D$. It is $0$ or a successor; for
$q\in X(D)$ it is positive. This gives a complete profile of $D$. Notice
that $A(X)$ need not be a maximal antichain when $X$ is not closed. The rank
argument must use all actual supports, not merely the maximal frontiers.

\subsection{A finite progress function}
For $X\subseteq\Qup$ let $\operatorname{prog}(X)$ be the greatest number of
productive steps in an ordering of $X$ alone, with freshness still measured
against all lower rows. Then $\operatorname{prog}(\varnothing)=0$ and
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{prog}(X)&\le\nuur(G_Q),\label{nh:ur:eq:d1}\\
 X\subseteq X'&\Longrightarrow \operatorname{prog}(X)\le\operatorname{prog}(X'),\label{nh:ur:eq:d2}\\
 X\subseteq X',\ \Nb(X')\ne\Nb(X)&\Longrightarrow
 \operatorname{prog}(X')\ge\operatorname{prog}(X)+1.
 \label{nh:ur:eq:d3}
\end{align}
The first statement follows by extending an ordering to all of $\Qup$ and
applying Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive}. For the other two, take an
optimal ordering of $X$ and append the columns in $X'\setminus X$. If a new
lower neighbour is acquired, at least one appended step must be productive.

\subsection{The ordinal ranking}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:rankmap}
 \Theta(D)=\alpha\cdot\operatorname{prog}(X(D))+
       \bigns_{q\in A(X(D))}\theta_q(D).
\end{equation}
The natural-sum part is less than $\alpha$ by
Lemma~\ref{nh:ur:lem:ordinalfacts}. Thus
$\Theta(D)<\alpha\cdot(\nuur(G_Q)+1)$.

We claim that $D\subsetneq D'$ implies $\Theta(D)<\Theta(D')$. Write
$X=X(D)$ and $X'=X(D')$. Necessarily $X\subseteq X'$. If
$\Nb(X')\setminus\Nb(X)$ is nonempty, \eqref{nh:ur:eq:d3} gives
\[
 \Theta(D)<\alpha\cdot(\operatorname{prog}(X)+1)\le
 \alpha\cdot\operatorname{prog}(X')\le\Theta(D').
\]
Otherwise no old active coordinate disappears. The old cuts weakly increase,
and the new upper coordinates, if any, are positive. If
$\operatorname{prog}(X')>\operatorname{prog}(X)$ the same ordinal block
comparison works. If $\operatorname{prog}(X')=\operatorname{prog}(X)$, strict
inclusion of the profiles forces either a strict increase of an existing cut
or a newly positive upper cut. Strict monotonicity of the finite natural sum
proves $\Theta(D)<\Theta(D')$.

A strictly increasing map into the ordinals proves well-foundedness.
Moreover, transfinite induction in the definition of rank gives
$\rk_{\K(S_\alpha(Q))}(D)\le\Theta(D)$: every predecessor has rank plus one at
most $\Theta(D)$. Taking suprema proves
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:upper}
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_\alpha(Q))\bigr)\le\alpha\cdot(\nuur(G_Q)+1).
\end{equation}
This proof uses neither a maximal-chain theorem for well-partial-orders nor
the persistence theorems of Parts~II--III.

\subsection{An attaining chain}
Let $k=\nuur(G_Q)$ and choose a productive sequence $(u_1,\ldots,u_k)$ of
maximum length, with fresh witnesses $\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_k$. It covers all
lower vertices: otherwise it could be extended by a column meeting an
uncovered row. For $1\le i\le k$ and $\beta<\alpha$ let
\[
 D_{i,\beta}={\downarrow}\{(u_j,0):j<i\}
              \ \cup\ {\downarrow}\{(\ell_i,\beta)\}.
\]
For fixed $i$ these downsets form a strict chain of order type $\alpha$.
Freshness ensures that the $\ell_i$-fibre is not already full. Every member
of block $i$ is strictly below every member of block $i+1$: the newly
inserted upper generator $(u_i,0)$ completes the previous witness's whole
fibre and is itself absent from every member of block $i$.

After the last block, start with the downset generated by all $(u_j,0)$.
Choose any maximal vertex $q\in\Qup\cup\Qiso$ and increase the cut in its
fibre through principal initial segments above its current cut. This is
another strict chain of type $\alpha$, by
Lemma~\ref{nh:ur:lem:ordinalfacts}(iv), and all its members can be chosen
strictly above all preceding blocks. If $k=0$, the skeleton is an antichain
and this final block alone supplies the construction. The union of these
$k+1$ blocks is a chain of type $\alpha\cdot(k+1)$.

For completeness, if $(x_\xi)_{\xi<\delta}$ is a strictly increasing chain in
a well-founded order, induction gives $\rk(x_\xi)\ge\xi$, so its height is at
least $\delta$. The constructed chain and \eqref{nh:ur:eq:upper} prove
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform}.

\begin{remark}[The terminal block and the missing empty case; 04]
\label{nh:ur:rem:terminal}
The final $+1$ inside the finite coefficient records a genuinely unbounded
maximal fibre, not the empty downset as a separate successor. This is why a
nonempty antichain has height $\alpha$, while a single relation has height
$\alpha\cdot2$. For an empty skeleton the height is instead $1$.
\end{remark}

\section{NP-completeness with one exponent scale}\label{nh:ur:sec:complexity}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\begin{definition}\label{nh:ur:def:problem}
The decision problem \textsc{Uniform-Height-Two} takes a finite nonempty
height-at-most-two poset $Q$ and a nonnegative integer $k$, and asks whether
\[
 \hgt\bigl(\K(S_\omega(Q))\bigr)\ge\omega\cdot(k+1).
\]
The order relation is given explicitly, or equivalently by its bipartite
incidence graph and its isolated vertices. The integer $k$ may be binary
encoded.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[04]\label{nh:ur:thm:np}
\textsc{Uniform-Height-Two} is NP-complete. The same holds with any fixed
positive pure ordinal $\omega^\rho$ replacing $\omega$, when the threshold is
specified by its finite coefficient.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform} the answer is yes exactly when
$\nuur(G_Q)\ge k$. A certificate is a list of $k$ disjoint incidence edges
with a triangular order. Distinctness, incidence and all forbidden earlier
cross-edges can be checked in polynomial time. A yes-instance has
$k\le\min(|\Qlo|,|\Qup|)$, so the certificate is polynomial in the input
length.

For hardness, use the established NP-completeness of the bipartite uniquely
restricted matching threshold problem~\cite{GHL2001}. Given its instance
$(G,k)$, form the poset with one element per vertex and the relation
$\ell<u$ exactly on its edges. Isolated graph vertices are isolated elements
of the poset and do not affect $\nuur$. Add one isolated element if necessary
to guarantee a nonempty poset; adding such an element does not affect the
matching number. Assign the single fixed fibre $\omega$ to every element and
ask the displayed height question with coefficient $k+1$. This is a
polynomial transformation, and Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform} proves
equivalence of the answers. The same reduction works for a fixed
$\omega^\rho$.
\end{proof}

This is an explicit reduction from an established NP-complete problem, not a
claim to reproduce the original reduction establishing the matching problem's
hardness. It suffices to close the complexity question of
Section~\ref{nh:sec:questions} and is independent of
Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo}.

\begin{corollary}[04]\label{nh:ur:cor:priorityhard}
The number of distinct exponent priorities alone cannot yield an FPT or even
an XP algorithm for the unrestricted height-two problem unless
$\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$. In particular the leading-coefficient threshold
problem is NP-complete at one scale.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Both an FPT bound $f(d)N^c$ and an XP bound $N^{f(d)}$ become polynomial at the
fixed parameter value $d=1$, contrary to Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np} unless
$\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[04]\label{nh:ur:prop:weightednp}
With positive integer exponents in binary and a threshold in finite Cantor
normal form, deciding whether $\Psi$ is at least that threshold is
NP-complete. Under Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo} this is also the
corresponding nonuniform pure-fibre ordinal-height threshold problem.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A permutation of the upper vertices is a polynomial-size certificate. Compute
its groups, their maximum exponents, and the ordinary sum with the terminal
monomial. A sum of at most $|\Qlo|+1$ nonzero monomials has at most that many
surviving units in its coefficient sum. Exponents are copied from the input,
never numerically expanded. Standard comparisons and additions of binary
integers therefore give polynomial-time verification, including comparison
with the supplied finite threshold. Hardness is the one-scale restriction in
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The threshold problem of Part~II; merge]\label{nh:ur:rem:partII}
\textsc{Uniform-Height-Two} is a special case of the threshold problem of
Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:NP}: every fibre $\omega$ is a pure block, there is one
exponent scale, and $\omega\cdot(k+1)$ is a threshold in Cantor normal form.
Hence that problem, which Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:NP} (12) places in
$\mathrm{NP}$ for the explicit pure-block encoding, is NP-complete already for
height-two skeletons and one scale. The hardness half rests on
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np} and~\cite{GHL2001}; the membership half for general
skeletons rests on Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:NP} and so on
Theorem~\ref{nh:thm:persistence}. This combination is the merge's
observation; 04 states NP-completeness for height-two skeletons only.
\end{remark}

\section{An exact ordered-matching formula for all priorities}\label{nh:ur:sec:weighted}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

The unweighted identification is only the first layer. To preserve
lower-order terms one must retain an admissible \emph{order} of the matching
edges.

\begin{theorem}[Weighted ordered-matching variational principle; 04]
\label{nh:ur:thm:weighted}
For every finite instance of \eqref{nh:ur:eq:objective},
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:matchingweighted}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Psi(G,\boldsymbol\rho;\rtop)=
 \max_{\substack{M\text{ uniquely restricted}\\
                 ((\ell_1,u_1),\ldots,(\ell_k,u_k))\text{ admissible order of }M}}
 \left(\omega^{\rho_{\ell_1}}+\cdots+
       \omega^{\rho_{\ell_k}}+\omega^{\rtop}\right).}
\end{equation}
The empty matching contributes $\omega^{\rtop}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Given a full schedule, discard its nonproductive columns and choose, in every
productive group, a lower row of maximum exponent. The chosen edges form a
triangular matching by freshness. Their ordered monomials are exactly the
nonzero summands of the schedule, so the schedule value occurs on the right
side of \eqref{nh:ur:eq:matchingweighted}.

Conversely, start with an admissibly ordered matching. Schedule its columns
first in that order. Each corresponding matched row is still fresh, so the
maximum exponent of that retirement group is at least the matched row's
exponent. Weak monotonicity of ordinary addition in each argument shows that
the value of this prefix is at least the sum of the matched-row monomials.
Append all remaining columns. Adding their monomials before the terminal term
cannot decrease the result: $\eta+\xi\ge\eta$ and hence
$(\eta+\xi)+\omega^{\rtop}\ge\eta+\omega^{\rtop}$. The full schedule is
therefore at least the candidate matching value. Taking maxima in both
directions proves equality.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient budget and priority invariance; 04]\label{nh:ur:cor:budget}
Every exponent in $\Psi$ is one of the lower exponents or the terminal
exponent; its total Cantor coefficient sum is at most $|\Qlo|+1$. Strictly
increasing relabellings of the finite ordered set of exponent values preserve
the coefficient vector and the set of maximizing schedules and ordered
matchings.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every candidate has at most $|\Qlo|+1$ monomials. Ordinary addition deletes
smaller trailing exponents and merges equal terminal exponents; it introduces
neither new exponents nor additional coefficient units. The deletion, merging
and lexicographic comparison rules depend only on equality and order among
the exponents, so commute with a strictly increasing relabelling.
\end{proof}
The invariance also follows from the report's exponent-order invariance
(Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:chambers}; compare Corollary~\ref{nh:cor:scale} and
Proposition~\ref{nh:wpo:prop:budget}); here it is immediate from the matching
formula. A short output does not imply a short search, as
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:np} shows.

\subsection{Why natural-sum edge weights are wrong}
\begin{example}[Forced absorption; 04]\label{nh:ur:ex:forced}
Let $\Qlo=\{a,b\}$, $\Qup=\{c,d\}$ and
\[
 \Nb(a)=\{c\},\qquad \Nb(b)=\{c,d\},\qquad
 (\rho_a,\rho_b)=(2,1),\qquad\rtop=1.
\]
The two-edge matching $\{(a,c),(b,d)\}$ is uniquely restricted. Its only
admissible order puts $(b,d)$ before $(a,c)$, since scheduling $c$ first
retires $b$. Its value is
\[
 \omega+\omega^2+\omega=\omega^2+\omega.
\]
Both full schedules attain that value. In contrast, replacing the ordered sum
by the natural sum of matched weights and then adding the terminal term gives
$\omega^2+\omega\cdot2$, a false answer.
\end{example}
The skeleton is this report's $N$-poset $a<c$, $b<c$, $b<d$, with the same
labels. The Part~III formula for it,
$\Phi=h_b+h_a+\max(h_c,h_d)$ (Section~\ref{nh:wpo:sec:examples}), gives the
same value $\omega+\omega^2+\omega=\omega^2+\omega$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.6cm,y=1.35cm,every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[circle,draw,inner sep=2.5pt,label=left:{$a:\omega^2$}] (a) at (0,0) {$a$};
\node[circle,draw,inner sep=2.5pt,label=left:{$b:\omega$}] (b) at (0,1) {$b$};
\node[circle,draw,inner sep=2.5pt] (c) at (2,0) {$c$};
\node[circle,draw,inner sep=2.5pt] (d) at (2,1) {$d$};
\draw[very thick] (a)--(c) (b)--(d);
\draw[dashed] (b)--(c);
\node[align=center] at (1,-0.85) {The cross-edge forces $d$ before $c$.\\
The earlier $\omega$ is absorbed by $\omega^2$.};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Example~\ref{nh:ur:ex:forced} (04). The thick edges are a uniquely
restricted matching. The dashed edge is not in the matching but imposes the
order that determines the ordinal sum.}
\label{nh:ur:fig:forced}
\end{figure}

The example is deliberately small: a weighted cardinality objective or a
maximum natural-sum matching loses precisely the precedence information on
which lower ordinal terms depend. The directed graph in
Lemma~\ref{nh:ur:lem:ur} is therefore not just a recognition device; it
records an essential part of the weighted problem.

\section{Priority layers and residual optimization}\label{nh:ur:sec:layers}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\subsection{The recurrence}
The leading term is simpler than the whole ordinal. It depends only on the
highest-priority rows. The lower terms record what can be saved after
choosing the high-priority endpoints.

Suppose $\Qlo\ne\varnothing$, let $p=\max_{\ell\in\Qlo}\rho_\ell$, and put
\[
 \Qhi=\{\ell\in\Qlo:\rho_\ell=p\},\qquad
 G_{\mathrm{hi}}=G[\Qhi,\Qup],\qquad k_{\mathrm{hi}}=\nuur(G_{\mathrm{hi}}).
\]
Let $\mathscr S_{\mathrm{hi}}$ be the family of upper endpoint sets of maximum
uniquely restricted matchings in $G_{\mathrm{hi}}$. Thus every
$X\in\mathscr S_{\mathrm{hi}}$ has cardinality $k_{\mathrm{hi}}$. For each
such $X$ define
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:residual}
 \Qlo^{(X)}=\{\ell\in\Qlo\setminus\Qhi:\Nb(\ell)\cap X=\varnothing\},
 \qquad G^{(X)}=G[\Qlo^{(X)},\Qup\setminus X].
\end{equation}
Every row in $G^{(X)}$ still has a nonempty neighbourhood. The terminal
exponent remains $\rtop$; it is not recomputed from the residual columns.

\begin{theorem}[Exact priority-layer calculus; 04]\label{nh:ur:thm:layers}
The following rules determine $\Psi$ recursively.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If $\Qlo=\varnothing$ or $p<\rtop$, then $\Psi=\omega^{\rtop}$.
\item If $p=\rtop$, then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:layer-equal}
 \Psi=\omega^{\rtop}\cdot(k_{\mathrm{hi}}+1).
\end{equation}
\item If $p>\rtop$, then
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:layer-main}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Psi(G,\boldsymbol\rho;\rtop)=
 \omega^p\cdot k_{\mathrm{hi}}+
 \max_{X\in\mathscr S_{\mathrm{hi}}}
 \Psi\bigl(G^{(X)},\boldsymbol\rho|_{\Qlo^{(X)}};\rtop\bigr).}
\end{equation}
Each residual value on the right is strictly below $\omega^p$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If there are no lower rows the definition gives the terminal term. If all row
exponents are below $\rtop$, every finite sum of their monomials is absorbed
by that term, proving (i).

For an arbitrary full schedule, the steps at which at least one row of $\Qhi$
retires are exactly the productive steps of the same upper order applied to
$G_{\mathrm{hi}}$. Their maximum number is $k_{\mathrm{hi}}$ by
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:productive}. If $p=\rtop$, all smaller intervening
terms are absorbed by subsequent $\omega^{\rtop}$ terms, including the final
one. The value is therefore $\omega^{\rtop}$ times one plus the number of high
productive steps, proving (ii).

Now suppose $p>\rtop$. Every schedule has at least one high productive step.
If it has $k$ such steps, its Cantor normal form is
\[
 \omega^p\cdot k+\eta,\qquad \eta<\omega^p;
\]
all lower terms before the last high step are absorbed. Schedules with
$k_{\mathrm{hi}}$ high steps exist, so an optimal schedule must have exactly
$k_{\mathrm{hi}}$. Choose a maximum-priority witness at each productive step
of such a schedule, as in Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:weighted}. The high
witnesses alone form a maximum uniquely restricted matching in
$G_{\mathrm{hi}}$, with some upper endpoint set
$X\in\mathscr S_{\mathrm{hi}}$.

Consider the witness edges strictly after the last high step. Their rows have
lower priority and are adjacent to no earlier chosen high column; thus they
lie in $\Qlo^{(X)}$. Their columns lie outside $X$. In their inherited order
they form a triangular matching in $G^{(X)}$. Applying
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:weighted} to the residual graph bounds their ordered
sum plus the unchanged terminal term by
$\Psi(G^{(X)},\boldsymbol\rho|_{\Qlo^{(X)}};\rtop)$. Earlier lower terms were
absorbed. This proves the upper bound in \eqref{nh:ur:eq:layer-main}. This
argument deliberately uses the matching witnesses: simply moving all low
columns to a later position could introduce unwanted retirements and is not a
valid substitute.

For the reverse bound, fix $X\in\mathscr S_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and triangularly
order a maximum high matching with upper endpoints $X$. Schedule those columns
first. Every one retires a fresh high row and contributes $\omega^p$. By
Lemma~\ref{nh:ur:lem:coverage} applied to $G_{\mathrm{hi}}$, these columns
cover all high rows. They also retire exactly the low rows adjacent to $X$.
The remaining live rows are precisely $\Qlo^{(X)}$, so following with an
optimal schedule on $\Qup\setminus X$ gives the value
\[
 \omega^p\cdot k_{\mathrm{hi}}+\Psi\bigl(G^{(X)},\boldsymbol\rho|_{\Qlo^{(X)}};\rtop\bigr).
\]
All residual exponents, including $\rtop$, are below $p$, so this tail is
strictly below $\omega^p$. Maximize over $X$ to prove the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Highest coefficient; 04]\label{nh:ur:cor:leading}
If the largest lower exponent $p$ exceeds $\rtop$, the coefficient of
$\omega^p$ in $\Psi$ is exactly $\nuur(G_{\mathrm{hi}})$. If $p=\rtop$, its
coefficient is $\nuur(G_{\mathrm{hi}})+1$. If $p<\rtop$, the only surviving
coefficient is the terminal coefficient $1$.
\end{corollary}
This gives an exact answer to the leading-coefficient part of the complexity
item of Section~\ref{nh:sec:questions} (07's question whether the leading
coefficient exceeds a threshold). The answer is graph-theoretic and
independent of the magnitudes of the ordinal exponents, but it remains
computationally hard in general (Corollary~\ref{nh:ur:cor:priorityhard}).

\subsection{Why a maximum high matching is not enough}
\begin{example}[A tie requiring lower-priority lookahead; 04]\label{nh:ur:ex:tie}
Let
\[
 \Nb(h)=\{u,v\},\qquad \Nb(\ell)=\{u\},\qquad
 \rho_h=3,\quad\rho_\ell=2,\quad\rtop=1.
\]
The high graph has maximum uniquely restricted matching size $1$ and two
possible upper endpoint sets. Choosing $X=\{u\}$ also retires the low row, so
its residual value is $\omega$ and the resulting value is $\omega^3+\omega$.
Choosing $X=\{v\}$ leaves the low row alive, so the residual is a single edge
of weight $\omega^2$ and the result is
\[
 \Psi=\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega.
\]
Both high matchings are optimal at the leading scale. Only one is globally
optimal. Thus arbitrary tie-breaking in a maximum high matching is incorrect.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.65cm,y=1.15cm,every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[circle,draw,label=left:{$\omega^3$}] (h) at (0,1) {$h$};
\node[circle,draw,label=left:{$\omega^2$}] (l) at (0,0) {$\ell$};
\node[circle,draw] (u) at (2,0) {$u$};
\node[circle,draw] (v) at (2,1) {$v$};
\draw (h)--(u) (h)--(v) (l)--(u);
\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (2.8,0.5)
{$u$ first: $\omega^3+\omega$\\[4pt]
 $v$ first: $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Example~\ref{nh:ur:ex:tie} (04). The residual graph, not just the
cardinality of the high matching, determines the lower coefficients.}
\label{nh:ur:fig:tie}
\end{figure}

\subsection{What the recurrence does and does not simplify}
The recursion decreases the number of distinct lower priorities until the
terminal scale absorbs everything left. It identifies exactly which residual
information controls the next coefficient: the low rows whose neighbourhoods
avoid the selected high columns, together with their remaining incidence
data. Different admissible orders of the \emph{same} high endpoint set need
not be retained at this stage; their lower terms occur before the final high
term and are erased. Distinct endpoint sets, however, need not be equivalent.

This is a structural reduction, not a polynomial-time algorithm. Enumerating
all maximum uniquely restricted matchings can be exponential, and even
finding their maximum cardinality is NP-hard. The recurrence is nevertheless
useful for proofs, exact branch-and-bound designs, and testing a different
implementation against the subset dynamic program. It also suggests that
equivalence classes of residual graphs, rather than arbitrary matching
witnesses, are the right objects for further compression.

\section{Exact dynamic programming and finite certificates}\label{nh:ur:sec:algorithm}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\subsection{A subset recurrence}
Let $r=|\Qlo|$ and $s=|\Qup|$. For a processed subset $X\subseteq\Qup$ and a
new column $u\notin X$ put
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:stepweight}
 b(X,u)=\max\{\omega^{\rho_\ell}:u\in\Nb(\ell),\ \Nb(\ell)\cap X=\varnothing\},
\end{equation}
with value $0$ when the set is empty. This depends on the processed
\emph{set}, not its order. Let $\mathfrak f(X)$ be the greatest ordinary sum
of retirement monomials over orderings of $X$, without a terminal term.

\begin{theorem}[Bellman recurrence; 04]\label{nh:ur:thm:dp}
The exact values satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:dp}
 \mathfrak f(\varnothing)=0,\qquad
 \mathfrak f(X)=\max_{u\in X}\Bigl(\mathfrak f(X\setminus\{u\})+b(X\setminus\{u\},u)\Bigr),
 \qquad \Psi=\mathfrak f(\Qup)+\omega^{\rtop}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Partition the orderings of $X$ according to their last column $u$. The
preceding retirement history has processed exactly $X\setminus\{u\}$, so the
last summand is \eqref{nh:ur:eq:stepweight}, regardless of its earlier order.
Ordinary addition by this fixed right summand is weakly increasing in the
prefix value. The largest preceding value is attained because the set of
orderings is finite. Thus its addition gives the maximum for this last column.
Maximize over $u$. The final terminal addition is handled by the same
monotonicity and attainment argument.
\end{proof}

Strict monotonicity in the left argument is not required and would be false
for ordinary addition. Weak monotonicity suffices. This is exactly the
distinction that permits a scalar state value $\mathfrak f(X)$ despite
ordinal absorption. On a height-two skeleton this is the specialization of
Part~III's support-ideal program \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:dp} (09) to the ideals
$\Qlo\cup\Qiso\cup X$, whose value differs only by the placement of the
terminal weight; the proof above does not use Part~III.

\subsection{Representation and complexity}
A finite result is stored as a list
\[
 [(e_1,c_1),\ldots,(e_d,c_d)],\qquad e_1>\cdots>e_d>0,
\]
representing $\omega^{e_1}c_1+\cdots+\omega^{e_d}c_d$. Appending $\omega^e$
deletes the trailing entries of exponent below $e$, then increments the last
coefficient if its exponent is $e$, or appends $(e,1)$ otherwise. The empty
list represents $0$. Lexicographic comparison of these lists gives ordinal
comparison.

Computing a step weight directly scans the $r$ lower rows. With at most $d$
distinct priorities, a straightforward implementation takes
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:time}
 O\!\left(2^s s(r+d)\right)
\end{equation}
elementary operations and $O(2^s d)$ stored ordinal entries, apart from the
incidence input. This expression treats a bit-mask intersection or exponent
comparison as an elementary operation. In a bit-cost model, multiply by the
appropriate polynomial factor in mask length, exponent bit length and
$\log(r+1)$ for coefficients. In particular the problem is fixed-parameter
tractable in $s$ with ordinary binary integer input.

The program's default guard is $s\le22$. It is a protection against
accidental state-space explosion, not a theorem restriction or a promise that
every 22-column instance is inexpensive. The implementation stores full
certificates; this can dominate memory long before arithmetic becomes
difficult.

\subsection{A dual dynamic program on covered rows}
A second state representation is useful when the lower side is smaller. For
$C\subseteq\Qlo$ retain the maximum prefix value among productive sequences
covering exactly $C$; unreachable states have no value. Start with
$\mathfrak c(\varnothing)=0$ and, for every column $u$ with
$\Nb(u)\setminus C\ne\varnothing$, relax the transition
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:covered-dp}
 C\longrightarrow C\cup\Nb(u),\qquad
 \mathfrak c(C)+\max_{\ell\in\Nb(u)\setminus C}\omega^{\rho_\ell}.
\end{equation}
The target state's value is the maximum of its incoming candidates.

\begin{proposition}[Covered-row state sufficiency; 04]\label{nh:ur:prop:covered}
The recurrence \eqref{nh:ur:eq:covered-dp} has exact final value
$\mathfrak c(\Qlo)+\omega^{\rtop}=\Psi$. It takes $O(2^r s(r+d))$ elementary
operations in a straightforward implementation.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A previously used column has its whole neighbourhood inside the current
covered set. It can never again make a productive transition. Thus every
productive transition allowed by the covered set automatically uses a
previously unused column, without needing to store the used-column set.
Conversely every productive sequence traces these transitions. Delete all
zero steps from a full schedule, or extend a productive sequence covering
$\Qlo$ by the remaining zero steps, to pass between the two representations
without changing the value. The transition strictly increases $|C|$, so
induction and weak monotonicity of ordinal addition prove the maximization
recurrence. There are $2^r$ states and at most $s$ transitions per state,
with at most $r$ rows scanned per weight.
\end{proof}
The delivered function \texttt{solve\_by\_covered\_rows} implements this
value-only variant; it does not emit the upper-subset certificate. The
verification suite compares it with the production optimizer. Choosing the
smaller side gives an exponential dependence on $\min(r,s)$, with polynomial
factors in the full input length, before any twin compression.

\subsection{A certificate of optimality, not just a good schedule}
A schedule certifies a lower bound but generally does not certify optimality.
The program therefore emits, for every $X\subseteq\Qup$, a proposed value
$\mathfrak v(X)$ and, for nonempty $X$, one selected last column
$\mathfrak p(X)\in X$.

\begin{theorem}[Bellman certificate soundness; 04]\label{nh:ur:thm:certificate}
Suppose a finite table satisfies $\mathfrak v(\varnothing)=0$, and for every
nonempty $X$ and every $u\in X$,
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak v(X\setminus\{u\})+b(X\setminus\{u\},u)&\le\mathfrak v(X),\label{nh:ur:eq:certbound}\\
 \mathfrak v(X\setminus\{\mathfrak p(X)\})+b(X\setminus\{\mathfrak p(X)\},\mathfrak p(X))&=\mathfrak v(X).
 \label{nh:ur:eq:certattain}
\end{align}
Then $\mathfrak v(X)=\mathfrak f(X)$ for all $X$, and
$\mathfrak v(\Qup)+\omega^{\rtop}=\Psi$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Induct on $|X|$. The already verified predecessor values are the true maxima.
The inequalities bound every candidate in \eqref{nh:ur:eq:dp} above, while
the selected equality exhibits one candidate equal to the proposed value.
Hence the proposed value is the maximum. The base case and final terminal
addition complete the proof.
\end{proof}

The supplied checker does not call the optimizer. It validates the instance,
the entire state table, every Bellman inequality, each selected equality, the
terminal value, and an explicit attaining schedule with its claimed
retirement groups. It does share the program's exact ordinal arithmetic and
input representation. It is an independent checking path, not an independent
trusted formal kernel. These certificates can be exponentially large; their
existence does not put the threshold problem in a stronger complexity class.
Part~III's support certificates (09) have the same shape over ideals of $Q$.

\subsection{Running the delivered implementation}\label{nh:ur:sec:running}
The package uses only the Python standard library and requires Python~3.10 or
later. Its solver is shipped as
\path{code/06-ur-matchings-ordinal_matchings.py} and needs no sibling module.
A complete input is, for example,
\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "upper_count": 2,
  "neighbours": [[0, 1], [0]],
  "priorities": [3, 2],
  "terminal": 1
}
\end{lstlisting}
Rows are indexed by their position, and upper columns by
$0,\ldots,\texttt{upper\_count}-1$. Every row must have a nonempty list of
neighbours. Duplicate indices, out-of-range indices, zero priorities, and
Boolean values used as integer priorities are rejected. The terminal priority
is explicit, even for an empty residual graph. Exponents and terminal
priorities must be positive integers; zero means an empty group internally,
not $\omega^0$. Empty lower and upper lists with a terminal represent a
nonempty terminal antichain, not the empty skeleton. From this report's
directory, with the output written outside the repository:
\begin{lstlisting}
py code/06-ur-matchings-ordinal_matchings.py solve \
  data/06-ur-matchings-tie_requires_lookahead.json --output /scratch/certificate.json
py code/06-ur-matchings-ordinal_matchings.py check \
  data/06-ur-matchings-tie_requires_lookahead.json /scratch/certificate.json
\end{lstlisting}
The first command reports \texttt{omega\^{}3 + omega\^{}2 + omega}. The
second checks the finite optimum. Neither command checks a transfinite height
theorem. The solver returns the exact Cantor normal form as decreasing
\texttt{[exponent, coefficient]} pairs (an empty list is zero), a maximizing
upper order, its retirement groups and a full Bellman table. It is a
pure-positive-priority solver, not an implementation for finite or successor
fibres, and it keeps the terminal priority fixed when residual graphs or twin
quotients are formed. The function \texttt{compress\_twins} applies
equality-only weighted compression; the command line does not silently
transform inputs. The test script \path{code/06-ur-matchings-verify.py}
imports its sibling under the delivered name \texttt{ordinal\_matchings} and
rewrites its examples and ledgers; it must be run on a copy that recreates the
delivered layout (see the README).

\section{Safe compression and structural parameters}\label{nh:ur:sec:compression}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

Problem~\ref{nh:prob:fpt} asks for algorithms avoiding the full frontier
expansion when neighbourhood data have few types. The following weighted
compression rules provide one answer for height-two skeletons.

\begin{theorem}[Weighted twin compression; 04]\label{nh:ur:thm:twins}
The value of $\Psi$ is preserved by the following operations, with the
terminal exponent kept unchanged.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Replace all lower rows with the same upper-neighbour set by one row
whose exponent is the maximum exponent of the class.
\item Replace all upper columns with the same lower-neighbour set by one
column.
\item Delete columns with no lower neighbours.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Rows with equal neighbourhoods retire at exactly the same first column in
every schedule. Their only contribution to that group's maximum is the
largest of their exponents, proving (i).

For columns with equal neighbourhoods, only the first scheduled member of the
class can retire any rows. Every later member has an empty group. Delete
those later members from a schedule; the first member represents the class
and all nonzero groups are unchanged. Conversely, a schedule on the quotient
expands by inserting all other members of each class after its
representative, where they contribute zero. This proves (ii). A column with
no neighbours contributes zero in every position, proving (iii).
\end{proof}

In the transfinite interpretation, removing an upper or isolated fibre must
not silently lower the terminal maximum. It is enough to retain that maximum
as the separate terminal parameter. Alternatively one can keep a virtual
isolated fibre of height $\omega^{\rtop}$ in the represented skeleton.

\begin{corollary}[Few distinct upper neighbourhoods; 04]\label{nh:ur:cor:types}
Let $t$ be the number of distinct nonempty neighbourhoods of upper vertices,
after safe row compression if desired. The weighted height-two objective is
computable in $f(t)N^{O(1)}$ bit operations, for example by the subset
dynamic program on the $t$ retained columns. The number and binary sizes of
the priorities are part of the input length $N$, not additional fixed
parameters.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Hashing or sorting incidence rows and columns computes the quotient in
polynomial time. Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:twins} preserves the answer and
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:dp} uses $2^t$ states; the remaining work and bit costs
are polynomial in the input length.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Few distinct upper-neighbour sets of lower rows; 04]\label{nh:ur:cor:rowtypes}
Let $a$ be the number of distinct sets $\Nb(\ell)\subseteq\Qup$ as $\ell$
ranges over the lower rows. The weighted objective is computable in
$2^aN^{O(1)}$ bit operations.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Merge equal lower neighbourhoods, retaining their largest priority. This
leaves $a$ rows without changing the objective. Apply the covered-row dynamic
program of Proposition~\ref{nh:ur:prop:covered}; all other costs are
polynomial in the encoded input.
\end{proof}
This distinguishes the two possible orientations of ``neighbourhood types''.
Both are tractable parameters: distinct neighbourhoods of upper vertices use
the upper-subset program, and distinct upper-neighbour sets of lower rows use
the covered-row program. The latter is the direct height-two answer to the
upper-neighbour-set parameter of Problem~\ref{nh:prob:fpt}.

\begin{corollary}[Vertex-cover parameter; 04]\label{nh:ur:cor:vc}
If the incidence graph has a vertex cover of size $k$, the weighted objective
is fixed-parameter tractable in $k$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let the cover be $C=C_{\mathrm{lo}}\sqcup C_{\mathrm{up}}$. Every upper vertex
outside $C_{\mathrm{up}}$ has all its neighbours in $C_{\mathrm{lo}}$, so there
are at most $2^{|C_{\mathrm{lo}}|}-1$ nonempty neighbourhood types outside the
cover and at most $|C_{\mathrm{up}}|$ additional types. Consequently
\[
 t\le |C_{\mathrm{up}}|+2^{|C_{\mathrm{lo}}|}-1\le k+2^k.
\]
Apply Corollary~\ref{nh:ur:cor:types}. If a cover is not supplied, the
standard edge-branching search chooses one endpoint of an uncovered edge at
each step and finds a cover of size at most $k$, if one exists, in
$2^kN^{O(1)}$ time. Thus no unmentioned cover oracle is needed. The resulting
double-exponential parameter dependence is a simple bound, not an optimality
claim.
\end{proof}

These positive results concern height-two skeletons. They do not answer
Problem~\ref{nh:prob:fpt} for arbitrary finite skeletons. They also do not
contradict Corollary~\ref{nh:ur:cor:priorityhard}: a bound on incidence types
controls combinatorial structure, whereas a bound on priorities alone does
not.

\begin{example}[Containment is not a safe deletion rule; 04]\label{nh:ur:ex:containment}
Return to the path-shaped graph $\Nb(a)=\{c\}$, $\Nb(b)=\{c,d\}$ of
Example~\ref{nh:ur:ex:forced} (the $N$-poset), but give every fibre weight
$\omega$. It has a two-edge uniquely restricted matching and uniform height
$\omega\cdot3$. Deleting $a$ merely because $\Nb(a)\subseteq\Nb(b)$ leaves a
one-row graph with matching number $1$ and height $\omega\cdot2$. Thus
neighbourhood \emph{equality} is safe, but replacing it with containment in
the compression proof is false, already on a four-vertex path.
\end{example}

\section{Exact classes and algebraic upper certificates}\label{nh:ur:sec:classes}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

\subsection{Components, forests and degree two}
\begin{proposition}[04]\label{nh:ur:prop:components}
For disjoint graph components $G_1,\ldots,G_m$,
\[
 \nuur(G_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_m)=\sum_{i=1}^m\nuur(G_i).
\]
If $G$ is a forest, then $\nuur(G)$ is its ordinary maximum matching number.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every alternating cycle lies inside one component. Thus a matching is
uniquely restricted exactly when its restrictions to the components are, and
component optima may be united. A forest has no cycle at all, so every
matching is uniquely restricted.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[04]\label{nh:ur:cor:degree2}
For a path $P_n$ on $n$ vertices and a bipartite cycle $C_{2m}$,
\[
 \nuur(P_n)=\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\qquad
 \nuur(C_{2m})=m-1\quad(m\ge2).
\]
Hence for a nonempty skeleton whose incidence graph has maximum degree at
most two, the uniform height is
\begin{equation}\label{nh:ur:eq:degree2height}
 \alpha\cdot\Bigl(1+
   \sum_{P_n\text{ component}}\lfloor n/2\rfloor+
   \sum_{C_{2m}\text{ component}}(m-1)\Bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A path is a forest and has a matching of size $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, which is
also the trivial vertex-count upper bound. A cycle has two perfect matchings,
so a matching of size $m$ is not uniquely restricted. Removing one edge from
one perfect matching gives a matching of size $m-1$. Its saturated induced
subgraph is a proper induced subgraph of the cycle, hence a forest, so this
matching is uniquely restricted. Apply component additivity and
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:uniform}.
\end{proof}

For a general forest, an ordinary maximum matching can also be found directly
by repeatedly matching a leaf to its neighbour and deleting their endpoints.
An exchange argument proves that some maximum matching contains that edge: if
its neighbour is matched elsewhere, exchange the incident matching edge; if
both are unmatched, add the leaf edge. This gives a simple polynomial
algorithm and makes the forest consequence independent of a
matching-algorithm citation.

Beyond these elementary classes, published algorithms transfer without a new
ordinal argument. For example, Francis, Jacob and Jana give a linear-time
algorithm for maximum uniquely restricted matching in bipartite permutation
graphs~\cite{FJJ2018}; it therefore computes the uniform height coefficient
for that incidence class. Such a transfer concerns the uniform objective only.
An algorithm maximizing matching cardinality is not automatically an
algorithm for the ordered weighted objective of
Theorem~\ref{nh:ur:thm:weighted}.

\subsection{Field-rank bounds}
Let $A_G$ be the $0$--$1$ matrix with lower rows and upper columns,
interpreted over any chosen field $F$.
\begin{proposition}[04]\label{nh:ur:prop:rank}
For every field $F$,
\[
 \nuur(G)\le\operatorname{rank}_F(A_G).
\]
More generally the same bound holds for any matrix over $F$ with exactly the
same zero--nonzero pattern as $G$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An admissibly ordered matching of size $k$ selects a $k\times k$ submatrix
whose diagonal entries are nonzero and whose entries below the diagonal
vanish, by \eqref{nh:ur:eq:triangular}. Its determinant is the product of its
nonzero diagonal entries, so it is nonsingular. Therefore the full matrix has
rank at least $k$. Maximize over the matching.
\end{proof}
This is the matrix-rank connection motivating the established matching
notion~\cite{GHL2001}, restated as an upper certificate for an ordinal
height. A triangular matching of size $k$, together with a rank-$k$
factorization of a matrix with the required zero--nonzero pattern, certifies
equality $\nuur(G)=k$ using only finite algebra and incidence checks. It need
not exist for an arbitrary graph at its optimum; no general equality with
matrix rank is claimed.

\begin{example}[A characteristic-dependent certificate; 04]\label{nh:ur:ex:cycle}
For the six-cycle incidence matrix
\[
 A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\0&1&1\\1&0&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
the first two rows are independent over $\mathbb F_2$ and the third is their
sum. Hence $\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb F_2}(A)=2$. A two-edge triangular
matching gives the opposite bound, so the corresponding uniform height is
$\alpha\cdot3$. Over $\mathbb Q$ the determinant is $2$, and the same matrix
has rank $3$, which gives a weaker upper certificate. Choice of field can
therefore matter.
\end{example}

\section{Worked weighted examples}\label{nh:ur:sec:examples}
\setcounter{equation}{0}

The example inputs and their complete Bellman tables are shipped
(Table~\ref{nh:ur:tab:examples}). The following values can also be checked by
hand.

\subsection{A weighted six-cycle}
Take lower rows $0,1,2$ and upper columns $0,1,2$ with
\[
 \Nb(0)=\{0,1\},\quad \Nb(1)=\{1,2\},\quad \Nb(2)=\{0,2\},
 \quad (\rho_0,\rho_1,\rho_2)=(3,2,1),\quad\rtop=1.
\]
The order $(0,2,1)$ retires rows $\{0,2\}$, then $\{1\}$, then nothing,
producing $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$. To see optimality from the layers, the
unique high row can be matched to column $0$ or $1$. The former leaves the
priority-$2$ row alive; the latter retires it immediately. The best residual
is therefore $\omega^2+\omega$. Unlike the uniform cycle example, the proof
must consider which high endpoint is selected, not only the high matching
size. This is the example following Theorem~\ref{nh:wpo:thm:heighttwo}
(09's \path{data/03-wpo-height_two_overlap.json}), with the upper vertices
$3,4,5$ there renamed $0,1,2$; both manuscripts obtain the same value by
different certificates.

\subsection{Independent edges and terminal absorption}
For three disjoint edges with lower priorities $3,1,2$ and terminal priority
$1$, schedule the priorities in decreasing order to obtain
\[
 \omega^3+\omega^2+\omega\cdot2.
\]
Any order with a smaller term before a larger one erases that smaller term,
so this is optimal. More generally, for disjoint edges the optimal prefix is
the natural sum of their monomials, followed by the ordinary addition of the
terminal monomial (compare \eqref{nh:wpo:eq:parallel}). This statement is
special to unconstrained independent edges; Example~\ref{nh:ur:ex:forced}
explains why it fails for general incidence.

With two disjoint edges of priorities $1,2$ but terminal priority $3$, the
answer is simply $\omega^3$. The entire lower schedule is absorbed. An
isolated maximal fibre can thus change which lower information survives, even
though it has no effect on the unweighted matching number.

\subsection{Summary of shipped examples}

\centering\small

Input (shipped as \texttt{data/06-ur-matchings-}\emph{name}\texttt{.json}) & Exact value\\
\midrule
\texttt{antichain} & $\omega^3$\\
\texttt{single\_edge} & $\omega\cdot2$\\
\texttt{six\_cycle} & $\omega\cdot3$\\
\texttt{weighted\_cycle} & $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$\\

\texttt{tie\_requires\_lookahead} & $\omega^3+\omega^2+\omega$\\
\texttt{terminal\_absorption} & $\omega^3$\\
